# Husserl, Transzendentaler Idealismus: Texte aus dem Nachlass (1908–1921), Husserliana XXXVI — Commentary

References give page and line of Hua XXXVI (e.g. 7.34–8.2); the editors' introduction, which has no line numbering, is cited by its roman page numbers. Ideen I is cited by section number, with Hua III/1 pages where useful. The second section of Ideen I, the "phenomenological fundamental consideration" (§§27–62), is the reference point throughout: its first chapter (§§27–32) describes the natural attitude and suspends its general thesis; its second (§§33–46) contrasts the being of consciousness with the being of the real; its third (§§47–55) draws from that contrast the thesis that the real world is dependent on actual consciousness while consciousness is absolute; its fourth (§§56–62) fixes the reach of the reductions. The texts of this volume are the workshop in which that argument was forged, and the commentary marks, unit by unit, which of its pieces each text supplies.

Six confusions beset these texts and are guarded against throughout. First, Husserl's thesis here is the "special" thesis that real, transcendent being is unthinkable without actual, experiencing consciousness; it is narrower than the general correlation of every kind of being with consciousness, and it holds that merely possible consciousness does not suffice. Second, the dependence asserted is a dependence of sense and legitimacy, not of causation or production: consciousness does not make things, and the real world is not thereby reduced to sensations or appearances (Husserl rejects phenomenalism as firmly as Berkeley). Third, the "proofs" work by exhibiting a countersense (Widersinn) in the opposite thesis, not by empirical premises about minds. Fourth, everything turns on the difference between merely logical possibility and real possibility, that is, possibility motivated by an actual course of experience. Fifth, the absolute consciousness on which the world depends is not the psychological consciousness of a human being, which is itself a worldly reality. Sixth, the claim that things have "only intentional existence" does not assimilate them to figments; it locates their actuality in the lawful harmony of legitimating experience.

## Introduction by the Editors (Robin D. Rollinger and Rochus Sowa)

The editors open by naming the single thesis to which every text in the volume is devoted: the existence of real objects, and so of the real world, is not thinkable without relation to an actually experiencing consciousness (ix). They stress that this is idealism in a narrower sense than the general correlation of being and consciousness, which covers ideal objects too and which Husserl later defends under the name "transcendental idealism," citing the Cartesian Meditations (Hua I, 116–121). A note gives three equivalent formulations from the texts (19, 64, 156), and another lists Husserl's variants: "phenomenological," "transcendental-phenomenological," "phenomenological-transcendental" idealism. The distinction between the "special" and the "general" thesis is the editors' most useful contribution and the one this commentary adopts from the start. It locates the volume's argument exactly: ideal objects are left out, and the claim concerns not possible but actual consciousness. Ideen I §49's formula, "not on logically conceived but on actual consciousness," is the published form of the special thesis.

Although Husserl seems to have used the term and its variants for his own position only from 1918 on, the earliest texts arguing for the special thesis date from 1908, and he tried until 1921, in research manuscripts and lectures, to elucidate it and ground it as a proof; these attempts are published in chronological order (ix–x). A note records the label on the folder B IV 6, from which many texts come: "1908–1924. On the doctrine of transcendental idealism, but also sheets on the countersense of transcendental realism"; a 1924 manuscript from it appeared as Hua XIV, Text No. 17. Another note observes that in what is probably the first use of the term (146), Husserl means the general correlation thesis. The dating matters for the question this commentary pursues. The arguments of Ideen I §§47–49 were worked out five years before the book and before Husserl called them "transcendental idealism"; the name came after the result.

The transcendental-phenomenological idealism of Ideen I met fierce rejection from Husserl's Göttingen students and the Munich phenomenologists (x–xi). As convinced realists who had read the antipsychologism of the Logical Investigations as a "turn to the object" and conceived phenomenology chiefly as ontological research into essences, they saw Ideen I as a relapse into the transcendental subjectivism that the earlier work seemed to have overcome. The notes document the controversy: Edith Stein's memoir, Ingarden's draft letter of July 1918 and his 1929 "Remarks on the problem 'Idealism-Realism,'" Conrad-Martius; Husserl's letter to Bell of 13 December 1922, that nothing in phenomenology is as certain to him as its transcendental idealism, which the Munich group rejects "as did the good Reinach in his time"; and his 1933 letter to Feuling, complaining that they imputed to him a "sudden reversal" into idealism in Ideen that they could not distinguish from traditional idealisms. A further note recounts Gerda Walther's index to the second edition of Ideen I (1923), which sorted passages under "pro" and "contra" phenomenological idealism; Husserl replaced it in 1928 with Landgrebe's index, and in 1932 told Walther he had been "completely certain" she was incapable of understanding transcendental phenomenology. The reception history explains why the volume matters. The published Ideen I gives the idealist thesis in three dense sections, and its first readers took it for a sudden metaphysical addition. These manuscripts show the thesis as the conclusion of sustained argument, which is the editors' implicit purpose in assembling them.

The Logical Investigations also defend an "idealism," but it concerns the non-empirical character of logical laws and the ideality of meanings and has nothing to do with transcendental idealism or the relativity of real being to actual consciousness (xi). Idealism there is "the form of epistemology that recognizes the ideal as condition of the possibility of objective knowledge in general and does not explain it away psychologistically" (Hua XIX/1, 112). A note adds that the first edition came close to the special thesis when it did not exclude the phenomenalist possibility that the objective grounds of all talk of physical things lie in lawful correlations among the experiences of many consciousnesses (Hua XIX/1, 371), a passage dropped from the second edition. The note is interesting in a way the editors do not pursue. The 1901 remark entertains precisely the phenomenalism that Text No. 4 (71) calls "the height of perversity," so the path from 1901 to 1908 runs not from realism to idealism but from an undecided, phenomenalism-tolerant position to an idealism that excludes phenomenalism.

Nonetheless the Logical Investigations contain a view that proved highly significant for the development of transcendental idealism: the appendix to the second chapter of the fifth Investigation, with its critique of the image and sign theories of perception (Hua XIX/1, 436ff.; compare Ideen I §43) (xi–xii). As in many texts of this volume that attack the inference theory of external perception, Husserl there criticizes a realist theory that starts from the false presupposition of a transcendent reality in itself and interprets the relation between immanent phenomena and the transcendent reality appearing in them as representation, either of image to original or of sign to signified. The notes name the targets: Eduard von Hartmann's Critical Foundation of Transcendental Realism (1875) for the image theory, a copy annotated by Husserl; Helmholtz's 1878 address "The Facts in Perception" for the sign theory, which Husserl heard discussed in Brentano's seminar and in Stumpf's psychology lectures of 1886/87. The identification of Helmholtz is useful for reading Appendix II and Text No. 10, where the inference theory appears in its probabilistic, causal form. The genealogy the editors draw, from the critique of representation in 1901 to the idealism of 1908, is the right one: the special thesis results from removing the representational intermediary and finding nothing left behind it.

The rejection of the image and sign theory leads in the fifth Investigation to the claim that it is a "grave error" to identify the distinction between "merely immanent" or "intentional" objects and "transcendent" objects with that between a sign or image present in consciousness and the thing signified or depicted, and then to the positive thesis "that the intentional object of the presentation is the same as its actual and, where applicable, external object, and that it is countersensical to distinguish between them" (Hua XIX/1, 438f.) (xiii). This general thesis of the identity of intentional and transcendent being stands behind the many texts in which Husserl tries to prove his special thesis indirectly by criticizing the inference theory. A note draws the editors' strongest conclusion: there can be no question of a "sudden reversal into idealism," and Husserl's phenomenological idealism, with its doctrine of the intentional immanence of everything transcendent, is "basically nothing other than the consistent unfolding" of the conception of intentionality already in the Logical Investigations and in the 1894 essay "Intentional Objects." Another note identifies this thesis as the basis of Ideen I §43. The continuity claim is defensible but too strong as stated. The identity of intentional and actual object is necessary for the idealism of these texts, but not sufficient. The special thesis needs, besides, the distinction between real and merely logical possibility and the requirement that real possibilities be anchored in actual experience (Texts No. 3, 5), and these have no counterpart in 1901. A realist can accept that the intentional object is the actual object, as the Munich phenomenologists did, and still deny that the actual object requires actual consciousness. The step from intentional identity to existential dependence is precisely what the volume argues for, and it is new.

In the 1908 texts, especially their appendices, the critique of the image or sign theory, and above all of the associated inference theory, is one of the central themes (references to 4f., 25f., 39–41, 45–49, 69; Text No. 10 is devoted to it entirely) (xiii–xv). Husserl shows that the traditional problem of how I can get beyond the immanent sphere of my consciousness to a reality "in itself" is falsely posed. Instead of starting, as Descartes's successors did, from the cogitationes and immanent sense data as the only immediate givens and asking how consciousness reaches transcendent reality, one must start from things showing themselves in external perception as immediate givens and ask, in Husserl's words, how the "actual world," which is no cogitatio, legitimates its being in the cogitationes, before the forum before which it must legitimate itself (7f.). The phenomenological way of answering is to investigate descriptively "the essence of the legitimate positing of things" (15). A long note sets out Husserl's relation to Brentano and Stumpf. Both rejected the inference theory, holding that external perception involves an instinctive belief, not an inference; Stumpf's lecture said the belief in the external world rests on an "original assent" to all appearing contents, which is why a child assents to fairy tales. Yet both held inner perception to be the only perception in the proper sense (Brentano 1874), the same presupposition as the inference theory, and Stumpf allowed that conscious, scientific inferences lead to knowledge of the causal relation between thing and sensation, which Husserl criticizes in Appendix II (46) and Text No. 10. The note settles a point that Appendix II leaves implicit: the "instinctive" view Husserl attacks is Stumpf's and Brentano's, and his objection to it is not that it misdescribes perception (both agree perception is not inference) but that it denies external perception the status of perception proper. The editors' formulation of the new starting point, things given in external perception, is accurate for the 1908 texts; it is worth noting that Text No. 10 (177) later says the same in sharper words, calling the positing of the ego "an artifact" and placing the perceived world at the beginning.

What proves decisive for the legitimacy of positing things and real being in general is the principle of legitimability (Ausweisbarkeit) (xv). In its general form it says that positing something as existing is legitimate or "rational" only if it is possible in principle to legitimate what is posited as existing. This principle, which underlies all Husserl's arguments for transcendental idealism in these texts (15f., 73, 117, 146), is applied to the transcendent real. In its special form, that transcendent reals posited as existing must be legitimable immediately or mediately by experience, it explicates a condition constitutive of the sense of "being real," whose neglect produces countersense. All modern epistemologies that start from a reality in itself transcending consciousness overlook it. Positing such an in-itself is countersensical because, as positing of something existing, it claims legitimability by experience, and at the same time it denies this legitimability by positing an in-itself inaccessible in principle. A note quotes Text No. 2 (32) in full. The editors' reconstruction of the countersense argument is exact and shows its logical form: a self-contradiction within the realist's positing, not a premise about minds. The principle of legitimability is the theory of reason of Ideen I §§136–142, and its status as a principle of sense rather than an epistemic norm is what allows it to yield an ontological conclusion.

Since existence assumptions have sense only as legitimable in principle, and since, as Husserl shows, the legitimability of positing a transcendent real requires starting from an actual experience and so an actually experiencing consciousness, which justifies its existence-assumptions immediately by perception or mediately by empirical inference from the experienced to the unexperienced but experienceable (12, 116), relation to an actually experiencing consciousness belongs to the sense of the existence of the real and is implied in every positing of thingly being (xvi). Denying this relation is countersensical or, in the logical sense, "unthinkable," and the state of affairs that transcendent reals exist but no actual consciousness does is impossible. Husserl expresses this, for example, at the beginning of Text No. 3: "Is the being of things, of a nature… thinkable if there is no consciousness at all? I say: No!" (53). The editors' compression shows where the weight of the argument lies. The general principle of legitimability gives only possible consciousness; the special thesis requires the further premise that legitimation must "start from an actual experience." That premise is what the distinction between real and logical possibility is meant to establish, and the editors rightly turn to it next.

For the thesis of a necessary relation between real transcendent being and actual consciousness, Husserl tries in Texts No. 2 and 3 to give a proof starting from the distinction between merely logical and real possibilities, those motivated by the actual connection of experience (xvi). Since he repeated this first proof in similar but more detailed form in 1913 and 1915 (Texts No. 5 and 6), its main features are discussed with those texts. The editorial decision is sensible: the proof is clearest in its lecture versions. The commentary on Texts No. 3 and 5 shows that its core, the selection argument, remains the same across all versions.

Besides the two proof attempts, the 1908 texts contain other considerations supporting the thesis (xvii–xviii). Husserl develops the identity of transcendent and intentional being, showing that a transcendent real, whether a sense object or a physical object, has sense only as the intentional unity of its constituting actual and potential manifolds and is nothing "beside and outside" it (Texts No. 2 and 4; notes quote 30 and 29). In Text No. 3 he shows by variation that a "functional dependence" (56) holds between the world as intentional unity and consciousness: the world appearing in consciousness cannot remain the same if consciousness is varied without limit, nor consciousness of the world if the world is varied freely. This correlation nonetheless has a centre of gravity, as Text No. 2 shows: the absolute consciousness constitutive of all transcendent realities. Real being as intentional being is "only in relation to absolute consciousness. It is only apparently absolute being" (32); a note adds that this absolute "emerges only in the phenomenological reduction and in metaphysical consideration." So, as a later text says, the relativity between world and consciousness is "existentially no correlativity," and "the being of a real world is contingent for the being of a consciousness" (79). The summary assembles the components of the thesis correctly: intentional identity, functional dependence, and asymmetric existential priority. It also shows that the 1908 texts already contain all three, while the asymmetry received its decisive formulation only in 1913 (Text No. 5), weeks after Ideen I §49 had stated it in print.

From the special thesis and these considerations follows the transcendental "immanence" thesis, expressed in Text No. 4: as the immanent object is nothing beside and outside consciousness, so the natural thing and all nature are immanent objects in a wider sense, nothing beside and outside the actual and possible experience to which they belong, "wholly sheltered in consciousness," in a sense quite similar to that in which the tone is sheltered in tone-consciousness (69) (xviii). The editors call this a "transcendental immanence thesis." The label is apt for Text No. 4 but should be handled with care in relation to Ideen I, which reserves "immanent" for the reell and calls the thing "transcendent" (§§38, 42); the book's terminology is chosen precisely to prevent the reading of the immanence thesis as phenomenalism, which the next paragraph addresses.

Though the world or nature is "only" an intentional unity in absolute consciousness and "sheltered" in it (a note quotes Text No. 1 on "only intentional existence," 13), the real world counting as existing for us in and from our experiences may not be discredited, as in phenomenalism, as unreal semblance or mere fiction, for "natural objects are of course true objects" and to say that natural science deals only with sensations, with things and atoms as thought-economical abbreviations, "is the height of perversity" (71) (xviii–xix). Husserl's transcendental idealism, which says that real transcendent being can be spoken of meaningfully only in relation to actual experience and the possible confirming experience it motivates, therefore does not annul the natural belief in the world; it is "nothing other than the position of epistemologically radically enlightened world-consciousness." Notes name Mill and Mach as the unnamed phenomenalists, whose works Husserl annotated, cite the 1930 Nachwort (Hua V, 152: phenomenological idealism does not deny the actual existence of the real world, its only task is to clarify the sense of this world), Ideen I's rejection of "subjective" or "Berkeleyan" idealism (Hua III/1, 120f.), and a 1928 letter to Maximilian Beck speaking of the "abysses" that separate his idealism from Berkeley's and all traditional idealisms. The formula "radically enlightened world-consciousness" is the editors' and it states Husserl's self-understanding well. It also identifies the tension the commentary has marked throughout: the same texts that call nature "wholly sheltered in consciousness" insist that nothing in nature's reality is diminished, and the coherence of the two depends on the distinction between the reell and the intentional.

The special thesis is also found, in various formulations, in Ideen I, in the three opening sections of the third chapter of the "phenomenological fundamental consideration" (xix–xx). In §49, "Absolute consciousness as residuum of world-annihilation," Husserl declares at a central point that "the world of transcendent 'res' is entirely referred to consciousness, and not to logically conceived but to actual consciousness" (Hua III/1, 104). In the two preceding sections he gives explanations and justifications in which his 1908 considerations are deposited in condensed form. This is the point on which the volume and this commentary converge. The editors locate the special thesis precisely in §§47–49 of the fundamental consideration and describe these sections as a condensation of 1908. The commentary has tried to show the condensation in detail: §47's motivated possibility comes from Texts No. 2 and 3; §48's "material countersense" of a world outside ours from the countersense argument of Text No. 2 and the variation of Text No. 3; §49's world-annihilation inverts the consciousness-annihilation of Text No. 1; and the restriction to "actual" consciousness is the result of the real-possibility argument.

Since the idea of real transcendence is the correlate of the idea of "legitimating experience," and everything experienceable from my Here and Now belongs to my "surrounding world" in the wider sense, it holds: "the thing is a thing of the surrounding world, even the unseen, even the really possible, not experienced but experienceable or perhaps experienceable thing. Experienceability never means an empty logical possibility, but one motivated in the connection of experience" (Hua III/1, 101) (xx). A note reports that Husserl wrote "rationally motivated" in the margin of his copy (Hua III/2, 497). Since "world" means "totality of thingly realities," and it belongs to the essential conditions of positing the existence of a thingly real "that it must necessarily be experienceable, and not just for an ego conceived through an empty logical possibility but for some actual ego, as a legitimable unity of its connections of experience" (102), it follows that among all possible worlds "the one spatiotemporal world is fixed by our actual experience" (103). So all talk of a really existing thing means talk of something existing in this space and this time and points back to actual experience of an actual ego. The quotations show how closely the published §§47–48 follow the manuscripts; the marginal correction "rationally motivated" shows Husserl tightening the link between motivation and reason, the link that Text No. 6 §10 makes explicit with its "primal right" of perception.

Husserl tried in the summer semester 1913, drawing on the 1908 distinction between real and merely ideal or logical possibilities, to work this argument of Ideen I into a formal "proof" (xxi–xxii). It is Text No. 5, a fragment of the 1913 lecture course that Husserl later labelled "on transcendental idealism." A second attempt is among the texts written in summer 1913, right after Ideen I appeared, for the revision of the sixth Logical Investigation (Hua XX/1, 266–271, marked "phenomenological idealism"). Husserl argues similarly in the central passage of the second half of the 1915 lecture course, Text No. 6. Notes add that Husserl uses "merely logical," "merely ideal," and "empty" possibility synonymously, real possibilities being ideal but not merely ideal (37, 61, 116, 118; Hua XX/1, 177–181); that Margarete Orthmann's transcript shows the fragment was delivered on 14 June 1913 and was followed by a discussion of the distinction between transcendental and psychological consciousness and then of the phenomenological reduction; and that the folder containing Texts No. 7–9 and Appendices V–VI is labelled "1914/15, 1918 and 1921. Important studies for the proof of transcendental idealism, namely proof of the general thesis of the correlation 'real being and actual subjectivity,' but also of the thesis that a world-existence demands its correlative subjectivity as animal and finally human subjectivity in the world." The note on the 1913 lecture's order, proof, then transcendental versus psychological consciousness, then reduction, confirms what the commentary found in Text No. 6 §§7–14: in the lectures as in Ideen I (§§49, 53–54, 50), the reduction is introduced after the absoluteness of consciousness has been established and is justified by it. The folder label also shows that Husserl himself distinguished the "formal" proof of correlation from the "material" thesis of embodied, human subjectivity, which Text No. 7 calls the "formally general proof."

The peculiarity of these partly complementary texts is, first, that Husserl conducts his proof independently of the method of phenomenological reduction introduced in 1906 (Hua XXIV, 211–216; Hua II, 43–50), commonly regarded as the way of access to transcendental-phenomenological idealism; and second, that he builds the proof, besides the principle of legitimability, above all on the distinction between real and ideal possibilities, using it to work out by contrast with the legitimation of ideal actualities the special conditions under which real actualities are legitimable (xxii). Already in 1908 Husserl had called possibility a "main concept" (12), but only in 1913 and 1915 does it really become one. A note explains that the first part of the 1915 lecture, on epoché and eidetic reduction at the level of Ideen I, was omitted. The independence claim is correct in a precise sense and needs that precision. The proof does not use the reduction as a premise; the reduction appears afterwards as its consequence. But the proof does use an eidetic method of variation that operates on consciousness "in suitable reduction" (Text No. 3, 61) and explicitly suspends the factual ego (Text No. 6 §§4a, 10), so it is not carried out from within the natural attitude either. Its independence is independence from the epoché as a starting decision, not from the reflective, eidetic standpoint. This matters for the user of Ideen I: the book's argument for idealism does not presuppose the reduction it later justifies, which removes the charge of circularity sometimes brought against §§47–50.

Something is ideally possible if it is thinkable without contradiction or can be presented intuitively in harmony (xxii–xxiii). Accordingly "ideal possibilities" in concreto are, on one side, ideal or real objectivities (objects or states of affairs) thinkable without contradiction or harmoniously presentable, and on the other, equally thinkable or presentable acts and connections of acts in which ideal or real objectivities come to givenness. The two types are correlated. The definition joins logical consistency and intuitive harmony. Husserl's texts lean on the second (harmonious phantasy, the centaur), and the editors' inclusion of both is accurate: the sun-sized diamond is established as possible by intuitive consistency, not only by formal non-contradiction (Text No. 3, 60, note: "not analytic possibility").

For ideal objectivities, ideal possibility coincides with ideal actuality (xxiii–xxiv). In the sphere of real, individual objectivities, actuality is a distinction among competing ideal possibilities, one excluding all others from being (if a man has fair hair, all other colours imaginable for him are excluded), whereas the ideally actual has no excluded possibilities beside it. The prime number 17 has no ideally possible competitors that could be "actual" primes in its place; all possible primes are, as ideal possibilities, ideal actualities. Equally, the actual and the ideally possible legitimation are equivalent. The sense of positing a mathematical object, "there is a prime between 13 and 19," contains the idea of a possible legitimation by some possible subject, but no relation to actual subjects of this world or possible subjects of a determinate world. Ideal actualities are indifferent to actual and merely possible, fictive consciousness, so a fictive subject in a fictive world can bring the same ideal object to legitimating givenness as an actual subject in the actual world: a centaur in phantasy that sees Pythagoras's theorem sees it as I do, though our worlds are without connection. Notes add that Husserl here considers only "free" idealities; "bound" idealities such as Beethoven's Ninth Symphony have possibilities beside them (Experience and Judgment, 321), and quote the principle of legitimability from Texts No. 5 (73) and 6 (117). The example of the centaur proving Pythagoras is the editors' own and it is excellent: it shows in one image why ideal objects need only possible consciousness. The note on bound idealities identifies a limit of the volume's dichotomy. Cultural objects are ideal yet historically individuated, and the special thesis would have to be extended to them, a step Husserl takes only in the Crisis and the "Origin of Geometry."

Similarly, for the legitimation of ideal possibilities of real transcendent objects, the factual legitimating subjectivity with its Here and Now is "inessential" (122), although the ideal possibility of a real object is not, as for ideal objects, the same as its actuality (xxiv–xxv). What matters for legitimating the ideal possibility of a fair centaur or a sun-sized diamond are the connections of appearance producible in free phantasy: harmonious quasi-experiences in which the real comes to givenness as intentional unity and so legitimates itself as something that could exist in principle (75). To an ideally possible real object correspond ideally possible systems of harmonious quasi-experience in ideally possible phantasy worlds, and all these are equally possible, all at the same distance from actuality; nothing in the actual world speaks for them; they are "groundless," empty, merely ideal possibilities. A note remarks that attempting to imagine harmoniously the "impossible objects" of M. C. Escher's prints leads to the insight that such things cannot exist. The Escher example is apt for the intuitive test of possibility: an image can be drawn but not consistently quasi-experienced from all sides, so the drawing does not establish the possibility of its object. The phrase "at the same distance from actuality" is the editors' concise statement of the premise of the selection argument.

Since the Here and Now of the imagining, actual subject stands outside all connection with the fictive Here and Now of the fictive subject in the phantasy world, and identification of actual individuals with quasi-individuals is excluded in principle, all legitimations concerning quasi-individuals have meaning only for that phantasy world and, unlike legitimations of ideal objects, which are the same "in" all possible worlds, no meaning for determining actual individuals in the actual world (xxv). "So a voyage of discovery in phantasy cannot replace an actual voyage of discovery." The aphorism states the consequence of Text No. 11's "unbridgeable abysses" (183) in a form that makes the point vivid. It also explains why the realist's world in itself, if merely possible, can never be identified with the world of experience.

By the principle of legitimability, the existence of real objects is equivalent to the possibility of legitimating their existence (xxv–xxvi). Unlike legitimation of their ideal possibility, fictive, merely ideally possible quasi-experiences no longer suffice for legitimating existence, "for with them we do not get beyond ideally possible or fictable things" (78). Nor is actual experience or perception, the primary ground of right for positing the existence of a real (109), necessary, since we rightly call things not immediately perceived existing. What entitles us to posit reals as existing beyond the immediately given is what Husserl calls "real possibilities of experience": possibilities bound and motivated by what is actually experienced and posited as existing. As the co-positing of the properties of a thing's back, co-posited in thing-perception, is not arbitrary but prescribed and motivated by the properly perceived front, and as a range of real, bound and motivated possibilities of perception corresponds to it, so every real counting or asserted as existing beyond the currently experienced field of things lies in another field reachable in principle from the actual one, and so in a horizon of possibilities bound and motivated by the actually experienced. "Not every actual thing is actually experienced by me. But if I am to assert rationally that it exists, it must be in the range of my possible experience" (114; compare Hua III/1, 96). The thing must be "necessarily in the one infinite space that is distinguished as real space by the things I actually perceive" (115). The editors' account of real possibility through the analogy of the back side is the clearest short statement of the core of the proof, and it identifies the continuity between the micro-structure of perception (horizon of a single thing) and the macro-structure of the world (horizon of all things), which Text No. 6 §9 makes explicit.

Husserl calls this infinite spatiotemporal horizon of really experienceable realities "surroundings" (Umgebung): "Every thing lies a priori in the surroundings of an actual ego… A real world is what it is only as surroundings" (114; Ideen I uses "Umwelt," Hua III/1, 101, 96) (xxvii–xxviii). The concept expresses the necessary relation of real objects and a real world to the Here and Now of an actually experiencing subject. This holds not only for things of sense perception, given always in an orientation to the perceiver, as the eidetic analysis of perception in Text No. 6 shows, but also for the physical thing. Every transcendent real, however objectively determined and however free of explicit reference to subjectivity, has spatiotemporal determinations that ultimately imply a There and Then meaningful only in relation to an actual Here and Now (a note quotes Text No. 2, 33, on the hic et nunc of empirical science and refers to Hua XX/1, 271). This Here and Now is the centre of orientation presupposed in all talk of existing reals; the real possibilities of legitimation, however mediated, are finally "anchored" in such a subjective centre. So all positing of transcendent realities presupposes an actually experiencing consciousness, and one that exists in the same spatiotemporal world as the real to be legitimated. The final clause is significant and goes beyond the formal proof. If the anchoring consciousness must exist "in the same spatiotemporal world," it must be embodied in that world, which is the thesis of Text No. 7. The editors thereby bring out that the indexical argument of Text No. 2 (33) already contains the seed of the "upper stage."

Husserl supplements his proof in Texts No. 5 and 6 by a closer determination of "real existence" and a closer characterization of the consciousness correlated with the whole world of realities counting as actual (xxviii). The two supplements are treated in the next two paragraphs.

The idea of legitimability implies the idea of rationality (xxviii). As both texts stress, positing the existence of a transcendent reality can be more or less "rational"; more or less can speak for it. "Exactly as far as the force of harmonious positing already carried out reaches in the actual consciousness, so far reaches the rationality of assuming the thing's existence" (76). But this rationality is limited in principle by the presumptive character of the experience of transcendent realities, and future legitimations can show well-confirmed positings to be false. "Real existence" is therefore an idea in the Kantian sense, the correlate of the idea of an absolutely rational positing of existence grounded in an ultimately legitimating experience harmonious to infinity. The editors' formula, real existence as idea in the Kantian sense, joins Text No. 5 (77) with Ideen I §143 and Appendix VI. Text No. 10 (181) refines it by preferring the communicative form of the idea, the harmonization of all egos' experiences, to the form of a single infinitely harmonious experience.

The actual consciousness correlated with the world of realities is, as Husserl stresses, not human but transcendental or absolute consciousness, in which the totality of realities, including the human psyche as component of the real "human being," constitutes itself (xxviii–xxix). As irrelative centre of all constitution, transcendental consciousness has a priority of being over all constituted realities. As Husserl tries to show by the thought-experiment of a dissolution of the intentional unity "actual real world" into a chaotic flow of appearances, the resulting non-existence of the real world is not the non-existence of consciousness, since a consciousness whose flow constituted no stable thing-unities, no "res," would be essentially modified in intentional content but still consciousness in concept or essence. Hence the correlation is "existentially" no correlation, and transcendental consciousness has priority of being (79). A note cites Ideen I §49's "nulla 're' indiget ad existendum" (Hua III/1, 104) and observes that at the end of Text No. 5 Husserl restricts this thinkability of consciousness without constituted world to a single "monadic" consciousness (79). The note identifies the limit that the later texts exploit. A single monad can exist without a world, but whether a plurality of monads can, given that their coexistence requires bodies in a shared nature (Text No. 9), is another question. The commentary on Appendix VI noted a second limit: whether the "chaotic flow" would demonstrate the non-existence of nature or only the madness of one ego.

The 1915 lecture had little influence because many of Husserl's German students were on military service (xxix–xxx). Yet Husserl regarded it as important: he kept the manuscript under the title "Proof for transcendental idealism" and read and revised it at least twice, in 1921 and 1926, which shows that he thought the argument worth serious consideration long afterwards, though dissatisfied with its presentation. An inserted sheet commenting critically on the lecture is published as Appendix IV, where he expresses great dissatisfaction with the absence of intersubjectivity. He nonetheless sees the possibility of giving the lecture again in its old form, but only as "a lower stage, as transcendental idealism of the primordial sphere," to be supplemented by an "upper stage" accounting for intersubjectivity and bodiliness. Notes report Husserl's description of the course in a 1915 letter to Fritz Kaufmann as "a pure ladies' course," Ingarden's later lack of recollection, and the "war psychosis" note (78). The lower/upper distinction is the key to the place of Ideen I in Husserl's later self-understanding. The fundamental consideration is not retracted; it is reclassified as the primordial stage of an idealism whose full form requires the constitution of the other and the body.

Beginnings of such an upper-stage idealism are found in Text No. 7, originally written in 1914 or 1915 (xxx). A later note at its head says that real being requires not only some factually existing knowing subjectivity (the "formally general proof") but that the being of a real world is thinkable only if the correlative subjectivity is bodily, human subjectivity in this world (132). The formally general proof of the preceding texts is thus assigned to the lower stage; the upper stage corresponds to the remarks near the end of Text No. 7, where Husserl shows that the subject has access to the real world and to other bodily present subjects only through its body, so that intersubjectivity and an objective world as intersubjective unity of validity are possible only for a bodily subject. A note refers to the intersubjective part of the manuscript in Hua XIII, 236–241. The editors' assignment is correct, with one qualification: the argument for embodiment in Text No. 7 is mostly conditional and self-critical ("not proved with a single word," 137), and its decisive formulation comes only in Text No. 9 (1921).

On the presupposition that relation to actual bodily subjects belongs to the being-sense of the real world, the fact that a world existed before the development of living beings, and especially of higher animals and humans, becomes a problem; on that presupposition it seems impossible (xxx–xxxi). Text No. 7 offers a solution: constituting the world in its infinite temporal extension does not require actual knowing subjects "for the whole infinite time of the world's existence"; it requires only a subjectivity that rationally constitutes the world as its present surroundings and can rationally reconstruct the preceding stretches, including a stretch of merely material nature (141). This solution frees the special thesis from paradoxical consequences and makes intelligible the remark that "a world without subjects who actually experience it is thinkable only as the past of a world with such subjects" (144, note; compare Text No. 1, 19). The editors present the reconstructive solution more confidently than Text No. 7 does. Husserl there doubts it at once ("what does it mean that a world... exists even when its constitution is not actual?", 141) and explores an alternative, a monadology of dormant subjects present at every time, which the editors do not mention. The solution they cite is the one Husserl kept, but it was a hypothesis under examination, not a settled doctrine.

After moving from Göttingen to Freiburg in 1916, Husserl resumed his reflections on transcendental idealism, as the correspondence between Edith Stein and Roman Ingarden documents (xxxi–xxxii). On 20 February 1917 Stein wrote that she had "solemnly submitted" her misgivings about idealism to the Master, who said he was not averse to changing his standpoint if shown it was necessary, which she had not yet managed, though he felt he must think the point through again. On 24 June 1918: "Idealism is on the agenda again… He is gathering everything he has on the question and talked with me about it in recent days." The letters show the volume's later texts in their dialogical context. Stein's objections, preserved in her later work, concerned precisely the dependence of real being on consciousness, and Husserl's willingness to revise is documented here; the 1921 texts show that he revised by deepening the conditions of the thesis, not by abandoning it.

Which manuscripts were "gathered" in 1918 can only be guessed (a note suggests Text No. 7, given marginal titles by Stein as assistant) (xxxii). Perhaps, prompted by the discussions with Stein, Husserl wrote the reflections titled "Main pieces for the proof of transcendental-phenomenological idealism," published as Text No. 8, dated "at the latest 1918," probably the first manuscript using this or a similar term. Unlike the preceding texts, Text No. 8 starts from the general thesis that the existence of an objectivity is the correlate of the possibility of its original givenness and asks what the possibility of original givenness requires for eidetic objects and whether every such object can be grasped originally by every possible subject. It introduces the distinction between formal and material eide and shows that for material eide, such as the genus "colour," there are contingent limits of knowledge, so that not every conceivable subject can bring all material eide to original givenness. The last sentence needs correction. Text No. 8 acknowledges genetic preconditions for material eide (a subject must have had sensations of the genus) but then states explicitly that "this does not cancel the eidetic insight" that every ego can be re-thought so as to have every material eidos, since its stream can be continued so that the eidos appears (148). The contingent limits concern the factual stock of a stream, not the eidetic accessibility of material essences to every possible subject. Text No. 9 (154–155) raises the limit again, for colours I cannot imagine, and leaves it open.

Texts No. 9 to 12 and Appendices V and VI all date from 1921 and are closely connected with other texts of the period already published in Hua XIV (a note lists them) (xxxii–xxxiii). Husserl then wrote many manuscripts and reread and revised older ones, including the 1915 lecture, probably as preparatory work for a planned "great systematic work" meant to replace Ideen as the "basic work of phenomenology" (letter to Ingarden, 25 November 1921; Kern, Hua XIV, xviiff.). In it, transcendental idealism would presumably have had its place at the new level of the "upper stage," since the 1921 texts make explicit, with bodiliness and intersubjectivity, the moments of experience demonstrable as necessary constituents of every experience of transcendent realities and so co-determining our concept and experiential sense of "really existing," which earlier texts had not considered. The context explains the character of the 1921 texts as drafts for a systematic replacement of Ideen I. It also supports the reading that the "upper stage" is not a refutation of the fundamental consideration but a supplementation that would have rewritten it.

In Text No. 9 Husserl examines the conditions under which ideal possibilities of the real are legitimated in harmonious quasi-experiences (xxxiii–xxxiv). He discusses the relation of the fictive quasi-experiencing subject to the actual imagining subject, distinguishes direct and indirect fictions, and shows the binding of phantasy to contingent limits and the limits of empathy. The proof of the thesis that "a nature is not thinkable without coexisting subjects of possible experience of it" (156) takes up thoughts from the 1915 lecture (122–123) but develops them with emphasis on bodiliness. Husserl shows (1) that only one nature is experienceable for an ego, (2) that two different subjects cannot constitute two different natures, (3) that several subjects are compossible, thinkable as existing together, only if they are psychophysical, bodily subjects related to the same nature to which they belong bodily. A nature thus seems thinkable only as correlate of a bodily intersubjectivity connected by empathy. Husserl nonetheless considers carefully the counter-possibility of a solipsistic, bodiless constitution of nature, with the result that "the possibility of a nature is unthinkable without possible subjectivity fulfilling the conditions of its knowledge," among which bodiliness with the kinaestheses constitutive for the appearing of nature counts (161). The three-point summary is accurate, but the second point was not shown in the text; Husserl calls it "a main desideratum of the proof" (160, note) and states it conditionally, on the assumption of Kant's thesis of one spatiotemporal form, which he says must still be proved (162). The editors' "Husserl shows" should be read as "Husserl argues for, conditionally."

Text No. 10 is an important supplement to the critique of the realist inference theory, according to which perception infers "instinctively" from sense data given immediately in consciousness to an external world as their cause (xxxiv). Husserl makes clear that the consciousness this theory speaks of, by its own intention of admitting only the immanent as immediately given, can only be the Cartesian cogito purified by phenomenological reduction. This pure immanent sphere of transcendental consciousness, in which the world of transcendent realities constitutes itself as intentional unity of sense and validity, has as all-constituting consciousness no "outside." Causal inferences from it to an outside are therefore senseless; they make sense only within the constituted world of realities, since "all inferences move necessarily on a homogeneous ground of experience" and so cannot lead from the ego cogito into the "transcendent" (178). A note adds that this argument also rejects the view of Stumpf and Brentano that such inferences, though not descriptively present in perception, are legitimate in explanatory science. The summary is precise and singles out the two decisive premises, the reduction implicit in the inference theory and the homogeneity of inference.

Text No. 11, which asks, restricted to individual temporal objects, whether every conceivable object lies within the ideal range of knowledge of every conceivable subject, offers like Text No. 9 important pieces of a theory of phantasy and the fictum, needed for a precise determination of the relation of ideal possibility and real actuality (xxxiv–xxxv). Husserl stresses the "unbridgeable abysses" between the experienceable and the fictive and states that even the assumption of experiencing a pure possibility is not only "nonsense" but a countersense "of a quite peculiar type," lying in the incompatibility of experienced A and imagined A in one consciousness of identity (184). He shows that by the "neutrality modification" every actual can be transformed into an ideal possibility by constructing a coinciding fictum, and contrasts free with experience-bound phantasy, whose existence-assumptions, unlike free phantasy's, can in principle be legitimated in future experience. The concept of a possible world is sharpened, from the viewpoint of compossibility, as joint compatibility "at one time point." For the opening question, Husserl concludes that to every compossible totality of factual objects, as individual temporal objects, belongs a knowing subject with a determinate rule of consciousness prescribed by this totality, and that all ideally possible subjects distinguished by another rule are incompossible with it and its totality. One correction: Text No. 11 defines compatibility of facts as unity "in one time," "coexistence or succession" (190), not compatibility at a single time point. The broader temporal unity is essential, since a possible world includes its past and future, and the restriction to a time point would make worlds into instantaneous states.

In Text No. 12, the last text, Husserl elucidates the concept of the in-itself of real objects and the real world, central for understanding his idealism (xxxv–xxxvi). It receives its ordinary sense, left untouched by Husserl's idealism, from the fact that real objects need not be actually experienced by me or anyone to count rightly as existing, and from the fact that relation to subjects is inessential to the natural determinations of things and only the causal connection of nature essential. This is accurate as far as Text No. 12 goes. The text also asks, without answering, whether the in-itself differs by level of constitution, phantom, causal thing, intersubjective nature, which suggests that the "ordinary sense" of the in-itself is itself stratified and that the idealist analysis would distinguish several senses of independence, not merely leave one untouched.

Looking back, one might think the phases in which Husserl tried to prove the special thesis were inconsequential episodes (xxxvi–xxxvii). But although such proofs are absent from the later work, so are revisions of them or any distancing from the thesis. It only recedes behind the general thesis and the general problem of constitution. Husserl evidently never abandoned his conviction of the special thesis, and in the 1930 Nachwort to Ideen, with regard also to the special thesis stated there (Hua III/1, 101–106), he could write that he had "nothing at all to retract regarding transcendental-phenomenological idealism" and still held every form of the usual philosophical realism to be countersensical in principle, no less than every idealism he opposes and "refutes" (Hua V, 150f.). In place of the misleading talk of a "proof," the late work speaks of a "demonstration" (Erweis), no longer the closed form of a single argument but the open form of an accumulation of phenomenological exhibitions, consisting, as the Cartesian Meditations say, in the "systematic uncovering of constituting intentionality itself. The demonstration of this idealism is therefore phenomenology itself" (Hua I, 119). The editors' conclusion places the volume correctly. The special thesis of Ideen I §§47–49 is the result of these investigations and was never retracted; what changed is its mode of justification, from proof to the whole of constitutional analysis. The commentary on the 1921 texts suggests a further point: the shift from "proof" to "demonstration" was forced partly by the discovery, in Texts No. 7 and 9, that the formal proof needed material premises about body and intersubjectivity that only a full constitutional analysis could supply.

The volume, the editors record, was begun at the Husserl-Archiv in Freiburg, largely carried out there by Robin D. Rollinger with support from the Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft, and completed at the Husserl-Archives in Leuven in collaboration with Rochus Sowa (xxxvii). The project was first directed by Bernhard Rang, director of the Freiburg Archive, who died before its completion; Hans-Helmuth Gander, Regula Giuliani, Thomas Vongehr, student assistants in Freiburg, and in Leuven Rudolf Bernet and Ullrich Melle (who read the textual apparatus and the introduction critically) are thanked. The introduction is dated Leuven, December 2002. The acknowledgment records the institutional history of the edition. It also explains a feature of the volume that matters for its use: the texts were deciphered from Gabelsberger shorthand by several hands across two archives, which is why the editors' dating of some texts (notably Appendix IV, "1921 or perhaps 1926") remains approximate, and why the commentary has treated such dates as editorial estimates rather than facts.

## No. 1. The Problem of Constitution. Perception as Immediate Givenness. The Sphere of the Cogitationes as the Fundamental Sphere. The Problem of the Legitimation of the Actual World in the Cogitationes. The Distinction between Truths of Essence and Truths of Fact

The manuscript, dated September 1908, opens with Husserl's own short synopsis keyed to his manuscript pages (3.9–4.28). It lays out a sequence that is already the skeleton of Ideen I, §§33–49. A false starting point is named first: the view that only phenomena are given and things must be reached by inference. Against it stands the true residue of that view, namely that only the cogitationes in reflection are absolutely guaranteed. That residue is then re-described as a "fundamental sphere," a unified field of consciousness, and the governing problem is formulated: how does a world that is perceived and thought in the cogitationes, and called "actual," legitimate its being within them? The method is a return to what is truly given, with judgments meant to be mere expressions of what is seen. Then come the special tasks: the basic forms of givenness, the problem of constitution, the correlation of thing and consciousness, and the "origin" of the thing-presentation taken in a transcendental rather than psychological sense. The synopsis ends with the two questions that generate the idealist thesis: can anything be without presenting itself in some consciousness, and can anything be if there is no consciousness at all? The answer is divided between truths of essence and truths of fact, and the second branch is handed on to Text No. 3. The arc from epistemological method to an existential question about the world is the same arc Ideen I travels from §33 to §49.

Husserl begins by refusing a formula: "Only phenomena are given to me; the things themselves are not; I know only that I have such and such appearances and must infer from them to the things" (4.29–34). This is the inference theory of external perception, the doctrine this volume attacks more often than any other. Husserl's marginal note calls it "the alleged inference to the transcendent" and points ahead to Text No. 3. The formula installs the epistemological problem in its classical modern shape, as a passage from an inner given to an outer ungiven, and Husserl's whole procedure in these texts is to show that the shape itself is wrong. Ideen I §43 ("Elucidation of a fundamental error") and §52 (against the physical thing as "unknown cause" of appearances) are the published descendants of this rejection.

The rejection rests first on description (5.1–8). Things stand before my eyes; I see and grasp them; my claim that they are actual is not arbitrary but has a ground, the ground of experience. And no inference has taken place: I have seen, and given expression to what I saw. The claim is phenomenological, not psychological: Husserl does not claim that inference happens unconsciously and quickly; he claims that the perceptual claim to actuality carries its own legitimating ground and that an inference would be a different kind of act with a different sense. This is the root of the principle that perception is the original source of right for claims about things, which Ideen I §§24 and 136–138 generalize.

Husserl then extracts what is true in the false starting point (5.9–27). Behind it lies the experience of dream, illusion, and hallucination: perceptions to which nothing perceived corresponds. From this one concludes, correctly, that even an unwavering perceptual certainty lacks the full and final ground of right. It is subjectively certain without the certainty that excludes all doubt. Husserl accepts this limitation of external perception but not the inference theory's diagnosis of it. The defect lies in the incompleteness of perceptual legitimation, not in a gap between a sign and its referent. This is the doctrine of Ideen I §46: the transcendent is dubitable in principle because its givenness is never complete, not because it is given only by proxy.

The contrast case is the cogitatio (5.28–6.1). When a thing appears, the appearing itself, taken in its sense, is beyond doubt. Of it I have an adequate perception, one that does not merely posit its object but holds it so fully that the question whether it exists cannot arise. The thing appears as being there itself; it presents itself. The perception of the thing does not present itself; it simply is there. A note sharpens the claim: doubt here would be countersensical, which is not to say psychologically impossible, and a general epistemological principle lies hidden in it. Husserl thus distinguishes two modes of givenness, presentation and sheer being-there, and declines to ground the difference in a feeling of certainty. Ideen I §§38, 42 and 46 rest on exactly this pair: immanent perception guarantees the existence of its object, transcendent perception does not.

From adequacy Husserl derives a field (6.2–27). Wherever a thing is perceived, remembered, or judged, the perceiving, remembering, or judging is itself absolutely given, whether or not the thing exists. Every justification of thing-judgments therefore takes place within absolute givens. Yet no perception, however well-grounded, is the thing, contains the thing, or legitimates its existence on its own. Each confers a certain weight of right; none excludes the thing's non-being; each presents it only partially and one-sidedly. Husserl compresses the point into a formula: presenting a thing is adumbrating it (Abschattung). Adequate perception gives without adumbration. Here the decisive asymmetry of Ideen I §§41–44 is in place: the experience is given absolutely, the thing only through adumbrations, and this difference in mode of givenness will become a difference in mode of being.

The sphere of cogitationes is accordingly "the fundamental sphere," since every positing of being is carried out in it (6.28–7.5). Cogitationes are not isolated; they form a unity of consciousness, and this unity is internal to them, lived through, and as absolutely given as its members. A note exposes a weakness the main text passes over. Calling the sphere fundamental already posits past cogitationes and a continuing flow, and adequate inner perception guarantees only the present cogitatio: "so there is a gap here." The note marks the problem that Husserl's time-consciousness manuscripts of these years take up; Ideen I §46 meets it by extending the indubitability of immanent perception to the flowing stream as held in retention. The absoluteness of consciousness in §49 depends on closing this gap.

Husserl next fixes the extension of the sphere (7.6–13). One consciousness includes whatever belongs in such a unity, starting from any absolutely given cogitatio. Whether there are several consciousnesses is left open. A second consciousness would by definition not belong to the first, so it can only be posited within the first, and this positing and its justification are themselves cogitationes of the first. The methodological solipsism is strict: other consciousness can enter only as something posited and legitimated within one's own. This anticipates the structure of the fifth Cartesian Meditation, and it explains why later texts in this volume (Nos. 6 §12, 7, 9) must reckon with empathy before the idealist thesis can be stated for a shared world.

Then the problem itself is set out (7.14–23). To say "I" is already to posit something that is not a cogitatio; I attribute to myself this or that cogitatio, including the one in which I posit the I. Husserl suspends the ego and keeps only the cogitationes and the unity they determine. (His marginal exclamation, "they determine!", stresses that the unity is fixed by the cogitationes themselves, not by an ego standing behind them.) In this unity things, living beings, persons, the external world, and God appear and are judged, rightly or wrongly. This is the "egoless" phase of Husserl's thought, before the pure ego of Ideen I §57; but the operation of setting aside the empirical I as a posited unity anticipates Ideen I §§53–54, where the psychological subject is itself a transcendent apperception.

The epistemological question is now reformulated (7.24–8.2). Only its cogitationes are absolutely given to this consciousness, and only in them is the world posited. If the positing is correct, the world is; but consciousness can know this only by judging legitimately and becoming aware of the legitimacy. Husserl therefore discards the question "how can consciousness get outside itself?" and puts in its place the question how consciousness can become certain of the legitimacy of its judging: "How does the 'actual world', which is not a cogitatio in consciousness, legitimate its being in the cogitationes, that is, before the forum before which it must legitimate itself?" The juridical image is exact. Consciousness is not a container from which one must escape but a court before which every claim to being must present its credentials. With this reformulation the inference theory loses its problem, and the problem of constitution takes its place. Ideen I's fourth section, the "phenomenology of reason" (§§128–153), is the full development of this forum.

Husserl guards the reformulation against skepticism (8.3–16). We do not doubt that actual things exist. We see only that their existence, like any existence, must legitimate itself in consciousness if it is to count as actual for consciousness, and we ask what that legitimation looks like. But we also do not presuppose their existence, and we cannot, so long as we do not know what legitimate grounding is, how the cogitationes that achieve it differ from those that merely claim to, and whether such differences exist at all. We put the positing of things in question without being skeptics. This is the epoché in embryo, the "parenthesizing" of Ideen I §§31–32, which suspends the general thesis without denying it and expressly distinguishes itself from Cartesian doubt.

The method is described in two steps (8.17–9.2). First I posit something as given; second, I check whether what appears as given is really given, or whether something not given has been co-posited along with it. I do not simply stay with the matter in the act of evidence; I reflect, judge about the judgment, and "compare the judgment with its object." To call a judgment evident is to say that it is a giving act or the expression of one; giving acts (giving perception, giving memory) are distinguished from non-giving, "symbolic" ones. The task is to see "how far" the giving reaches and how far the expression matches the giving act. A note concedes that only immediate perceptual evidence is in view and that mediate insight remains unexplained. Here the principle of all principles (Ideen I §24) is already formulated in its critical form: intuition is the source of right, but only within the limits in which it actually gives.

Husserl analyses seeing (9.3–24). Seeing is essentially seeing of something; it is a form of meaning (intending) that does not mean beyond what is given. One can therefore compare a seeing, moment by moment, with what it claims, and separate components that are truly seen from components that are merely meant and refer to the unseen. The expression that clings to the seeing can be analysed so as to dissolve those components that point beyond it. The distinction between the properly given and the merely co-meant, which Text No. 6 §4b will develop and Ideen I §44 compresses, here appears as an instrument of critique: the phenomenologist purifies a claim of everything not covered by intuition.

Seeing is "the last" (9.25–10.13). Full seeing is seeing in which it is itself seen that every part of the seen is seen. Husserl asks whether this is only an ideal and how one could know that the separation of seen and unseen has succeeded. The regress cannot be stopped by argument, since every argument already presupposes the validity of seeing. Nor can it be stopped by a feeling: a feeling could not make a seeing genuine, and that it marked genuineness would itself have to be seen, so that the question would recur for the new seeing. "The last measure of all warrant cannot itself be measured." The target is the feeling theory of evidence that Husserl had rejected in the Prolegomena (§51) and attacks again in Ideen I §145. A note records that this reflection prompted the treatment of evidence in the summer 1909 lectures on the phenomenology of knowledge.

What is needed instead is a distinction among the basic forms of givenness, or of evidence (10.14–28). Every evidence is a giving act, but evidence is not a tag that can be attached to arbitrary acts, nor a uniform "having" of the given. There are as many basic forms of evidence as there are basic forms of objects and of presentation; each genus of presentation has its own difference between giving and non-giving modes and its own laws governing them. Perception divides again into adequate perception and perception as a partly unfulfilled member of a nexus of fulfilment. This is the regional relativity of evidence that Ideen I §§136–138 and §142 make into a doctrine: each region of being has its own type of originary giving, and thing-perception is not deficient for lacking the adequacy proper to immanent perception.

A hurried paragraph lists open questions (10.29–35): whether evidence is a function of fulfilment, how forms of evidence relate to forms of grounding and fulfilment, evidence as "logical origin," simple evidences and their composites, and the nature of mediate evidence and grounding. Husserl notes that he must finish that day. The list names the programme of the sixth Logical Investigation carried into the new transcendental framework; the theory of mediate grounding it asks for is what Ideen I §§141–145 supply in outline.

The problem of constitution is then stated for all kinds of objectivity (10.36–11.8). Every object can become conscious in various kinds of acts, intuitively or emptily, simply or through the understanding. The question is how it belongs to the essence of an objectivity of type G to become meant and given precisely through such acts, and to legitimate itself through precisely such series of givens. Constitution here is a relation of essential fit between an object-type and the acts in which it shows itself and proves itself. It is not production. Ideen I §§150–152 restate the problem as the "clue" function of the region: the essence of the region thing prescribes the systems of consciousness in which things are given.

The converse question follows (11.9–32). It must be shown that it belongs to the essence of precisely these cogitationes to bring precisely such objects to consciousness. What essential features of acts make them consciousness of G? How does consciousness, by its own essence, determine that it means this object, and means it perceptually, mnemically, or in mere phantasy? How do connections of acts, by their own essence, become legitimate positings and knowings? Is it an accident that knowledge of nature is bound back to perception and experience? Husserl answers that an a priori governs here throughout and that the acts must be exhibited from whose essence the necessary relation to natural actuality becomes intelligible. Constitution has two sides, the object's prescription of its manifolds and the manifolds' achievement of the object, and Husserl insists on both. Ideen I's noetic-noematic parallelism (§§88–98) is the later form of this double question.

Husserl begins to make the problem precise (11.33–36) by naming its first element: the essential relation of intentional existence to the possibility of intentions and to an actual consciousness. The phrase contains the whole thesis of the volume in compressed form. Intentional existence is tied not only to possible acts but to actual consciousness; the next units explain why possibility alone is not enough.

The thing is what it is only as a unity of a manifold (11.37–12.16). Its existence does not mean that it ceases to be when the manifold is not actually conscious; it means that its existence stands in relation to the possibility of that manifold. No scientific elaboration changes this. The possibility, however, is not empty. As Kant saw, it is a possibility bound to actual perceptions: we can know of a thing only as something we see or infer from what is seen. And the relation to consciousness has a stronger sense here. Without relation to some determinate consciousness, to some actual perceptions and memories, the thing loses the possibility of being given not only for that consciousness but for every consciousness. This is the first statement of the special thesis. Kant's postulate of empirical actuality, which ties the actual to perception "according to the analogies of experience," is reread so that the real possibility of further experience is anchored in an actual experience. Ideen I §47 states the same in its doctrine of "motivated" possibility, and §48 draws the consequence for any world outside ours.

Husserl contrasts the immanent and the thingly mode of being (12.17–13.3). For the immanent, esse = percipi: to be is to be lived through. The lived experience is accessible to reflection in the present and in recollection, and to others through empathy as analogical grasping in another's body; but it is given as what it is to reflection. Thingly being is, even if not perceived, that is, even if not posited in any experience. Its essence is therefore different. Yet relation to actual consciousness belongs essentially to it. It belongs to the thing's essence to be identical in such and such perceptual manifolds, to be determinable in thought on the basis of experience, and to be grounded, as to its existence, only mediately, through connection with actual perceptions in a single experience. The thing is a possibility of perception and determination, but one that "necessarily points back to actual consciousness." Husserl thus refuses both Berkeley's equation of thing-being with being perceived and the realist's detachment of it from perception. Ideen I §45 ("Unperceived experience, unperceived reality") reproduces this contrast.

The correlation of possible knowledge and being therefore means something different for things than for non-intentional, immanent items (13.4–23). "Things have only intentional existence," but in a different sense from the loose talk of the merely intentional being of fictions. Two pictures of the thing are set against each other. On one, things simply are and are later announced by mysterious signs. On the other, things are only by virtue of their possible givenness, and this givenness prescribes their sense, the content of their being. If the second is right, the task is to investigate how the first, originary givenness, to which all legitimate thing-knowledge returns, constitutes things: in which acts and combinations of acts the unity of a thing comes to appearance and is progressively determined. The guard against assimilating "intentional" to "fictive" is placed at the very point where the thesis is first formulated; Ideen I §49 will call the world "merely intentional being" and §55 will deny, in the same breath, that this turns reality into illusion.

What cogitationes are these, whose immanent essence it is to give things (13.24–33)? Husserl stresses that they do not call out to us "by a mysterious signature" that there is a thing, but make up, by necessity, the appearance of the thing and the continuing consciousness of its sameness through changing presentations. Ultimately they are giving, perceiving experiences, and they are the last source when one seeks to determine the sense of thinghood itself. The "signature" image condenses the sign theory: on it, appearances refer to things as marks to what they mark. Husserl replaces reference by constitution. The appearances do not stand for the thing; the thing is the unity they themselves make up.

To exhibit the origin of the thing-presentation is not to show by what psychological constructions it arises, for "we are not in psychology" (13.34–14.8). It is to exhibit its "history": the series of acts built teleologically on one another, matching the possible order of grounding for thing-being, and to clarify which acts make up the simple appearing of things, the continuously unified appearing of the same thing (whereby flowing manifolds yield consciousness of one object, changed or unchanged), and valid appearing. The quotation marks around "history" mark the difference between a genetic-psychological account and a stratified analysis of founding relations. The later term for this is static constitutional analysis, and Thing and Space (1907) is its first execution; Husserl's late genetic phenomenology will reopen the temporal sense of the word.

Husserl separates ontology from phenomenology (14.9–27). We can state a priori ontological propositions about things in general: what things cannot fail to be if they are to be thinkable at all, and so what cannot hold of a given thing if it is to exist. Such propositions presuppose no existence and are norms for every positing of things. A different line of research concerns the cogitationes: what positing a thing consists in and which system of cogitationes belongs essentially to knowledge of things. The task is to analyse "experience in the Kantian sense," taken purely phenomenologically, as the experience of consciousness and not of an empirical individual in space and time. Husserl here separates the regional ontology of nature (Ideen I §§9, 16) from the phenomenology that clarifies it, and explicitly assigns the latter Kant's question about the possibility of experience, minus Kant's anthropological and faculty-psychological framing.

He wants to understand "how I come to" posit things, a world, space, time, and other persons (14.28–38). This does not mean how I can get out of myself or overcome solipsism. Having seen that in the absolute sphere all judgments must legitimate themselves, whether about itself or about other being, whether about the I, things, other I's, or relations of ideas, we study this sphere with regard to the intellective connections that make up legitimating thought. The rejection of the "getting out" question is repeated because it is the hinge of the whole argument. Once it is dropped, the problem of the external world is no longer a problem of bridging but of analysing the structure of legitimation inside the forum, and the conclusion of Ideen I §49 becomes possible.

Secondly, the aim is to investigate the essence of the legitimate positing of things (15.1–9). This investigation stays wholly within the cogitationes. Things are not cogitationes; they are what is posited in cogitationes, and if they are actual, what is posited with right. To clarify the essence of rightful positing, the existence neither of determinate things nor of things in general can be presupposed. The rule anticipates the methodological core of the reduction: what is to be clarified cannot serve as a premise of its own clarification.

A logical argument reinforces the rule (15.10–24). Within experience I can ground the existence of some things on the existence of others. But to clarify the essence of grounding a thingly existence at all, and to see what the right to posit such existence looks like and how far it reaches, I may presuppose no thingly existence. From "things are" nothing follows about the right of the judgment "things are"; to argue otherwise would be a shift into another genus (metabasis eis allo genos). The question concerns only what is in consciousness, what is absolutely given before this forum. The charge of metabasis, Aristotle's term for proving across the boundary of a genus, is aimed at naturalistic epistemology, which explains knowledge by facts of the world whose knowability is the question. "Philosophy as Rigorous Science" (1911) and Ideen I §26 renew the charge.

What is gained (15.25–30)? The study of these connections of consciousness is interesting and valuable in itself, as is its parallel in ethics and value-theory, the study of evaluations and their legitimation built on intellective acts. But it has a further significance. A marginal note: "From here the purpose of transcendental phenomenology—the metaphysical." Husserl distinguishes the descriptive interest of phenomenology from its epistemological-metaphysical yield. The parallel with axiology foreshadows the extension of the phenomenology of reason to valuing and willing reason in Ideen I §§139 and 147.

The further significance is epistemological (15.31–16.2). Epistemology is the science of the "sense" and "reach" of human knowledge. I know of things only through certain cogitationes. If I think something in such a way that it cannot legitimate itself in this sphere, I can have no ground to assume that it is. "And if it cannot in principle legitimate itself there, then it cannot be at all." The step from no ground for assuming its existence to its impossibility is the pivot of the volume. Husserl moves from an epistemic restriction to an ontological one by way of the concept of sense: to posit something as existing is to posit it as legitimable, so a being that is unlegitimable in principle is a countersense. Ideen I §48 applies this to a world outside ours; §142 states the general equivalence of true being and the possibility of rational positing.
