The proper content of the cogitationes prescribes its sense to everything that can possibly be, and this sense includes the possibility of its fulfilment, a legitimating givenness within the sphere (16.3–12). Anyone who imagines the substance that bears properties as a thing behind the thing, as something sensibly perceptible in the manner of a sense content, even if perceptible only to "God," or as an I-know-not-what, violates the sense of "substance" as it legitimates itself: as the unity seen and given in genuine identification within the manifold. The target is Locke's substratum and every doctrine of a substance behind appearances. Husserl's counter-concept, substance as the identical given in synthesis, becomes the "determinable X" of Ideen I §131, and his remark about "God" anticipates the refutation in Ideen I §§43 and 52 of the idea that a divine intuition might see the thing as it is in itself without adumbration.

A further question is then posed: can something be without either presenting itself in a consciousness or being positable in a logically groundable way from what does present itself (16.13–22)? It may be that I have no experiences now that could ground such a positing; but ideally a course of experience must be possible that would justify it. The possibility in question is ideal, a possibility of justification in consciousness. Husserl then defines it: "possible consciousness means consciousness that can be fitted into the unity of actual consciousness." The definition carries the special thesis. Possible consciousness is not any consciousness one can think up but a continuation of the actual one. Ideen I §47 restates this when it says that possible experience must be "motivated" by actual experience, and the later "proofs" in this volume (Nos. 3, 5, 6) turn entirely on it.

The question is sharpened (16.22–32): can there be no consciousness at all, and yet something be? That something would not be an item of consciousness but something that, like a thing, could legitimate itself in consciousness as a unity of a manifold while not having the mode of being of the manifold. One might say that if there is no consciousness, there is no right to assume such a thing, so long as no consciousness is; only when there is a consciousness that perceives and thinks can positing be grounded. Husserl here performs the inverse of Ideen I §49. There, the world is thought away and consciousness remains; here consciousness is thought away and the question is what remains of the world. The two experiments are halves of one argument, and the volume shows that the second, consciousness-annihilation, came first.

The realist objection follows (16.33–37). The consciousness that carries out the justification need not be actual; justification need only be possible. Things exist whether or not there is a human being or any conscious being, provided only that a consciousness is possible that could justify them. The objection states the position Husserl must refute. If it holds, idealism collapses into a harmless correlation of being with possible knowledge, compatible with realism. Husserl's entire effort in these texts is to show that "possible consciousness" in this objection is an empty logical possibility that cannot carry the sense of existence.

Husserl's first reply is to exclude humans and conscious beings from the argument (16.38–17.8). Human beings are themselves something that can legitimate its being only in a consciousness, and only as intentional unities, not as being of the kind cogitationes have. We must stay with pure consciousness and try the thought, whether it proves possible or countersensical, that no consciousness, no cogitatio, no nexus of cogitationes is actual. Then, it seems, "everything is possible." The reply blocks a confusion that will recur in the reception: the consciousness at issue is not a psychophysical being, whose non-existence is easily thinkable and changes nothing in nature. Ideen I §§53–54 secure the same distinction between psychological and transcendental consciousness before stating the thesis of dependence.

The case divides, first, into truths of essence (17.9–33). Essential connections grounded in the essences of cogitationes, the relations of ideas, presuppose no existing consciousness. They say only that if there are cogitationes, these laws hold and the possibility of seeing them exists. As to that possibility, "what is ideally possible in one consciousness is possible in every one." Every consciousness can be ideally modified so as to pass over into any other. A factual cogitatio may exclude one required for a justifying nexus, as red excludes green in the same place, but no consciousness can exclude in principle the ideal possibility of a justifying nexus. "Every cogitatio can in principle occur in every consciousness." The factual is contingent; the possibility of an essential nexus is a priori. Husserl uses Hume's term "relations of ideas" deliberately: eidetic truths are independent of fact, and any consciousness whatever is the possible site of their insight. Ideen I §§2–7 rest on the same independence of essence from fact.

The possibility of a grounding nexus can be cancelled for essential reasons (17.34–18.2). Then the nexus does not "hold"; it is a false one. The essential law that a factual nexus would realize does not hold, and the factual nexus is impossible a priori in any consciousness. Falsity in the eidetic sphere is thus defined phenomenologically: a proposition is false when the justifying connection it would require is excluded by essence.

Alternatively, the possibility may be excluded in a given factual consciousness by factual grounds (18.3–21): its factual content offers cogitationes that leave no room for those the grounding nexus would need. "But this factual incompatibility means nothing." The factual is the irrational, which can be otherwise; factual can be replaced by factual. In principle, consciousness can have no objection. If a proposition holds, the ideal possibility of its justification holds, and an objection from ideal grounds would mean that essential laws conflict, which is impossible: essential laws are bound to no consciousness and cannot contradict one another. The argument is built to fail for facts. Eidetic truth is indifferent to which consciousness exists because any could exist; the next step shows that factual truth cannot be indifferent in the same way.

Returning to the fiction that there is no consciousness at all, Husserl concludes for essential truths (18.22–25): their holding requires no factual consciousness, though saying that they hold does require one. The distinction between holding and being asserted protects the ideality of logic, defended in the Prolegomena against psychologism, from the idealist thesis. Husserl's idealism here concerns real being; it does not touch ideal objects, and the Logical Investigations' "idealism" of meanings remains intact.

Truths of fact are different (18.26–19.16). If no consciousness exists, every possible fact is equally possible, that is, every ideally possible individuation of essences. "But that means that it makes no sense to ascribe existence to a fact if there is no consciousness at all," neither to a fact of consciousness nor to a thingly, intentional fact. When an actual consciousness says that certain things are real, this means that they find justification in its determinate experiential grounds, that is, in determinate connections of factual cogitationes. Were the connections different, other things would be posited, excluding these. If factual consciousness is varied according to logical possibility, we enter a sphere of unlimited possibilities in which any world, including worlds conflicting with this one, is as possible as this world, within the limits fixed by ideal possibilities. Positing the given world depends on what is given to consciousness; without such givens no determinate world can be posited, and in a merely possible consciousness nothing is given except hypothetically. The editor notes that Husserl struck out the rest of the paragraph and wrote "reworked in Bα" (Text No. 3). A note adds a tempting but rejected argument: that non-existence of consciousness is the most absurd of absurdities, since "there is not" has sense only in conflict with being and thus presupposes consciousness; but this presupposes only possible consciousness, "so it does not work." The positive argument is the decisive one. Logical possibility cannot single out a world; only an actual stream of experience, with its determinate content, motivates the positing of this world rather than another. This is the kernel of Ideen I §§47–48 and of the variation argument in Text No. 3.

Hence, if there is no factual consciousness, there is no factual world, or, as Husserl corrects himself, "only no positing of a factual world!" (19.17–22). One may not argue that the world simply goes unposited while there is no consciousness and that, once consciousness arises, a world is prescribed retroactively for the time before. A note voices the realist's rejoinder: without actual consciousness there is no actual positing of a world, but can a world not be without being posited? The self-correction shows Husserl's caution. He is not yet sure that he has shown more than that a world cannot be posited without consciousness, and the rejoinder in the note is exactly the realist position that Text No. 3 sets out to refute.

The answer comes through the forms of reality (19.23–20.3). Is consciousness in time, and is there a time before and after consciousness? Time is a form of thinghood, of physical and egoic thinghood alike. "If there is no consciousness, the being of real facts, of things, of I's loses its sense, and with it the being of the forms of reality." Space likewise has its facticity: it is neither a cogitatio nor a form of cogitationes but something that "presents and fulfils itself" through determinate factual connections of cogitationes. Were the connections different, space could not be posited; ideal possibilities belong to space, but they alone yield no world-space. The editor's introduction cites this sentence as a canonical formulation of the special thesis. The argument blocks the retroactive-prescription objection by denying its presupposition: a "before consciousness" would be a position in objective time, and objective time is itself one of the forms whose sense depends on consciousness.

The same holds for time (20.4–16). Objective time is not the form of the flow of consciousness, with its now, before, and after; it is the form of things, and if things lose their sense without consciousness, so does thingly time. It may be necessary that every objectivation by a consciousness also objectivates the stream with its pre-empirical time; but only through the factual element that enters objectivation, determinate sensibility, does a world with space and time arise. Before all sensibility and givenness, time is nothing but an empty possibility among others. Husserl here separates the immanent, "pre-empirical" time of the stream from objective time, the distinction that Ideen I §81 states as that between phenomenological and cosmic time, and he ties objective time to the hyletic facticity of sensation, a point Ideen I §85 leaves implicit.

So "a time before consciousness, before all actual consciousness, is nonsense" (20.17–21). Empirical consciousness gains a temporal position, and a spatial one, only by the attachment of cogitationes as "subjective" phenomena to a body, that is, on the basis of an already constituted thinghood, world, and time. This anticipates Ideen I §53, where consciousness acquires a place in nature only through its apperceptive connection with a body, and explains why the evolutionary story of consciousness emerging late in cosmic time cannot touch transcendental consciousness: the story is told in a time that consciousness constitutes.

The text closes with an ontological conclusion (20.22–26). It is unthinkable that consciousness should arise and pass away. Cogitationes surface and sink in the stream, but they do not act or suffer action, and they do not arise or pass away in the thingly sense. "Only something real can arise. But consciousness is not something real; it is the condition of all reality." With this sentence the special thesis passes into the thesis of the absolute being of consciousness, Ideen I §49's "nulla 're' indiget ad existendum." The sentence also sets a problem for the rest of the volume. Text No. 7 will argue for the birth and death of every subject as an a priori necessity, and that thesis can only be reconciled with this one by distinguishing the worldly arising of a psychophysical subject from the non-arising of transcendental consciousness.

## No. 2. The Epistemologist and the Metaphysician. The Problem of Epistemology. The "Dissolution" of Empirical "Being" into Connections of Absolute Consciousness

The manuscript, probably also from September 1908, opens again with Husserl's own table of contents and short summaries (21.7–22.27). The metaphysician asks what the absolute is and what reality is in the ultimate sense, and Husserl asks what motivates the question. Natural knowledge meets no difficulty until reflection turns to the relation between knowing and being and asks what "wonderful affinity" binds objects in themselves to thinking. If knowledge of objects in themselves becomes problematic, so does the "sense" of known objectivity: the question becomes whether it can count as absolute being or only as intentional being of a determinate lawfulness, a unity of manifolds of consciousness. The summary lists the other stations: consciousness as being in the primal sense; an attempt to show that an in-itself unexperienceable in principle is a perversity (with a reference to the "better and clearer" version in Text No. 3); the anchoring of every empirical determination, physics included, in subjectivity here and now, and the illusion of absolute being that arises from it; the "very important" proof of an unbreakable correlation by which being without actual consciousness is unthinkable. It ends by describing the companion sheets (Beilage I and a lost "third sheet") as an introductory catalogue of perplexities about knowing the transcendent, among them the inference theory, which is shown to be wholly false, and Lotze's pre-established harmony. The title's "dissolution" in quotation marks announces the text's thesis and its guard at once: objective being dissolves into consciousness only in a qualified sense still to be fixed. The synopsis pairs an epistemological question with a metaphysical one in the order Ideen I will keep: first the sense of transcendence, then the absolute being of consciousness.

The metaphysician asks about the absolute and seeks "interpretations of empirical reality" (22.28–23.18). His motive is the following. The natural attitude takes the reality of experience as ultimate. Then one notices the difficulties in the relation between that reality and knowledge. To empirical reality belong I among other persons, the environment, physical nature; but things and persons outside me are given to me only through perceptions, empathies, judgments. How do I get beyond my own experiences, and how can those connections of experience called "knowledge" grasp an objectivity that is in itself whether known or not? Living naturally, I take myself to have things directly in perception, to read off their properties, to infer from perceived to unperceived, to form reasonable expectations. Husserl does not caricature the natural attitude; he grants it its full epistemic competence. The problem arises not from natural experience but from a reflection that sets knowing over against being as two things. Ideen I §§27–30 describe the same natural attitude with the same sympathy before suspending its thesis.

Thinking about things also seems unproblematic (23.19–33). A thing cannot have contradictory predicates; things are necessarily in space, endure, change or persist in one all-embracing time; they can be counted and measured, and laws of number hold independently of what is counted; laws can be stated about objects, determinations, and truth in general. One judges about "ideal unities" such as the order of the number series, and judges a priori about manifolds. So long as one does not reflect, there is no difficulty. Husserl includes formal ontology and mathematics in the naive stance. The epistemological problem therefore concerns ideal objectivity as much as real, which is why the next units extend the puzzle to ideal objects before Husserl narrows his thesis to the real.

Then the misgivings arise (23.34–24.17). What my thinking, intuiting, and determining grasp is nothing subjective but an in-itself, which is what it is whether or not anyone thinks it, and which is discovered rather than made. Real and ideal objects stand before me; I judge and infer about them; always they are what they are whether I have them in view or not. Being perceived or thought is accidental to them; they enter into relation with me by chance. The marginal note refers the reader from here on to Beilage I. Husserl states the realist self-understanding of knowledge in its own words, including the claim that the relation to the knower is accidental. The idealist thesis will deny exactly this accidentality while keeping the independence of the object from any particular act.

The puzzle follows immediately (24.18–25). If things are in themselves, how can I ever grasp them? If they are separate from thinking, how do they "come into" thinking? What wonderful affinity do all other things, all objects in themselves, have with thinking? The question of affinity recalls Kant's transcendental affinity of the manifold and, more directly, the "wonder" of knowledge in Husserl's 1907 lectures on the idea of phenomenology. Posed in this form, the question already contains the error: it assumes two self-standing terms and asks for their bond.

Husserl presses it (24.26–38). Can I see things that are in themselves as they are in themselves? Can I perceive anything but the immanent, can knowledge go beyond itself and hold something as it is that is not immanent to it? But if it is immanent, it is no longer in itself. We are all certain that we perceive and determine physical things that are not psychic by their own sense and that are, whether I am or not, yet knowable as they are. The known is physical, the knowing psychic. The dilemma is now explicit: either the object is immanent and loses its in-itself, or it is in itself and becomes unknowable. Its hidden premise is the identification of the epistemological contrast immanent/transcendent with the ontological contrast psychic/physical, an identification Ideen I §§38 and 41–42 dismantle by showing that the transcendent is given in the immanent without being a part of it.

Deceptive perception sharpens the puzzle (25.1–16). Illusions and hallucinations present their objects exactly as normal perceptions do, yet the objects do not exist. One answers that the hallucination conflicts with my other experiences. But conflict is a feature of my thinking; how can accidental features of my thinking decide anything about what is not thinking? In thought I find only the subjective: connections marked "true," others marked "false," and these marks too are subjective. Consciousness, the sphere of immanence, is the only sphere of immediately intelligible and direct givenness, and I do not understand how I am ever to get beyond it. The argument from illusion here works against itself. It uses the coherence of experience to unmask the illusion and then declares coherence merely subjective. Husserl will resolve the tension by making harmony of experience the very sense of real existence (Ideen I §§46, 138, 151), so that conflict among experiences is not a subjective quirk but the phenomenological meaning of non-being.

Whatever the status of the supposed reality in itself, one thing is absolutely beyond doubt always and everywhere: that thinking is, that the cogitationes are (25.17–23). The Cartesian residue is now stated in the voice of the puzzled epistemologist. It is the same residue Text No. 1 called the fundamental sphere, but here it appears as a prison rather than a forum.

Taking physical nature, any single finding may in fact be false, as everyone concedes; every given thing may be imaginary, so no case yields absolute certainty, and only the cogitatio is absolutely given (25.24–29). A note objects that this presupposes that conflict among experiences means something objectively. The note detects a circularity in the skeptic's own premise. To say that any thing may be imaginary is to rely on the corrective power of further experience, so the skeptic already credits experience with the objective significance he then denies it.

The inference theory is the natural way out (25.30–26.10). Can anything have happened other than an inference from the certain to the uncertain, so that at least the scientific assumption of a world in itself is justified only by inference from the absolutely given? But how is such an inference possible? And what of ideal objects: do I infer them too from the cogitationes? Here also thinking must be distinguished from what is thought, without the usual confusions. How can thinking posit out of itself something that is not thinking and is not even individual? Are such objects not fictions, so that all one has is a thinking with a certain character, a psychic peculiarity? The extension to ideal objects is decisive for Husserl's case against the inference theory. No one thinks that numbers are inferred as causes of our number-thoughts; yet the epistemological structure is the same. If inference cannot explain knowledge of the ideal, the inference theory does not reach the real root of the problem either.

The problem is now general (26.11–26). How can objects be perceived, given, determined, inferred in consciousness without being anything in it; how can knowledge be intelligible if objects do not reduce to features of consciousness; and how can it fail to be illusion if they do? Since the possibility of knowing an objectivity in itself has become questionable, so has the "sense" of the in-itself of known objectivity of every kind. Do not such difficulties press us again and again to reduce the in-itself to a feature of consciousness and so to change the sense of transcendence presupposed in the dispute? Husserl here converts an epistemological puzzle into a question of sense. The dilemma between unknowable in-itself and illusory immanence cannot be decided as stated; it dissolves only if the sense of "transcendence" itself is clarified. That is the move from epistemology in the traditional sense to transcendental phenomenology, and it is the move Ideen I makes when §§42–44 replace the question "how does consciousness reach the thing?" with the question "what does it mean for a thing to be given?"

What objectivity is, in all its kinds, natural thought tells us, at its highest as natural science, with logic and mathematics as formal ontology (26.27–27.3). The "sense" of objectivity as a unity of knowledge lies on another line. In the one case we are directed at objects and judge about them; in the other we judge about objectivity in relation to knowledge and ask how knowledge gains relation to objects and how the validity of its positing of being is intelligible from the essence of certain connections of consciousness. Husserl draws the line that will separate the natural from the phenomenological attitude (Ideen I §§1, 27, 50). It is not a line between two kinds of object but between two directions of judgment on the same objects.

When one disputes whether objectivity is what it is only in relation to knowing consciousness, or whether a certain relativity to actual and possible consciousness is essential to it, the dispute concerns the "sense" of the being-in-itself of knowable objectivity, namely whether it can count as absolutely existing (27.4–9). "Absolute" is the term in dispute. Husserl does not ask whether objects are independent of my acts, which no one doubts, but whether their being is self-standing in the sense in which the being of consciousness is.

The question is not about content, which objective science determines, but about the absolute being, and its essence, that corresponds to objectivity in correlation with knowledge and underlies all relativities (27.10–23). Natural being is not absolute; it is being as correlate of consciousness. This means that it is intentional being, which points back necessarily to connections of intention, to a thinking consciousness which is itself absolute, since it does not point back in this way in turn. And since it belongs to the immanent essence of such connections that the object is thought, posited, and finally validly determined in them, objective being "dissolves" into connections of consciousness under essential laws. The definition of the absolute is functional: absolute is what does not refer back to something else for its being. This is the sense of Ideen I §49, where consciousness "needs no thing in order to exist" while the world is "relative" to consciousness. The quotation marks around "dissolves" are a warning, made explicit in the next units, that the dissolution concerns sense and legitimacy, not content.

The thing in scientific consideration is what it is, and epistemology and metaphysics change nothing in this (27.24–31). They carry out their own reflections and bring out the absolute significance of science. They show that all objectivities are "appearances" in a peculiar sense: unities of thought, unities of manifolds, which make up, as consciousness, the absolute in which all objectivities constitute themselves. Husserl protects the idealist thesis from phenomenalism by a division of labour. Science's results stand; philosophy adds their absolute interpretation. "Appearance" here does not mean semblance or sense-datum but unity constituted in manifolds. Ideen I §55 ends its statement of idealism with the same assurance that nothing in the world is "reinterpreted," and the 1930 Nachwort repeats it.

So the issue is not "sense" in the sense of the essence of things or of ideal objects like numbers (27.32–28.12). That sense is given, and so is what belongs to it necessarily; objective science, general ontology and mathematics unfold it. The issue is not ultimate sense but ultimate being, and this means that being in the sense of the objective sciences is "no ultimate being" but "dissolves" into consciousness. "The thing itself does not dissolve into consciousness. It dissolves into atoms and molecules." But "a thing is actually" and "there is a reality" point back to connections of knowledge, to formations of consciousness, in which the being of the thing gains its sense. A note names the ambiguity at stake: sense as an object's essence or content given in intuition, and sense of objective being, its ultimate "absolute sense," which allows every objective being a reduction to the absolute being that constitutes it. Another note reads: "Just look it through. Certainly interesting as a first proof of transcendental idealism." The atoms-and-molecules sentence is the sharpest guard in the volume against a reductive reading. What dissolves is not the thing's content but its existence-claim, which is shown to be a claim about connections of consciousness. The note's vocabulary postdates the text: on the editor's evidence Husserl adopts "transcendental idealism" as a name for his position only from about 1918, so the note claims this argument retrospectively as its first proof.

An immanent content, a moment of consciousness, simply is (28.13–24). A thing, by contrast, gains its sense. It is a sense, and every sense presupposes a bestowal of sense and belongs in connections of bestowing and legitimating sense, of meaning and grounding meaning, in which the object "stands there," is determined, and legitimates itself as truly being. If these connections are cancelled, talk of the thing's being loses all possibility. Not so for immanent contents: they are lived through and, if seen, absolutely there; they are not ideal unities of manifolds that would be nothing without the manifolds in which they present themselves. A note adds that one must go back further, from immanent contents that are themselves constituted to the ultimate flow of consciousness. The paragraph is the direct ancestor of Ideen I §55: "All real unities are 'unities of sense.' Unities of sense presuppose a sense-bestowing consciousness." The note on the flow marks the level of Hua X, where even immanent contents are constituted in absolute time-consciousness, a level Ideen I §81 acknowledges and brackets.

If manifolds of consciousness and their motivational connections are actual, then everything that exists in the objective sense is "co-included" in them, yet not really contained in them and not existing somehow outside them as well (28.25–32). It is included as a unity of validity that belongs to them essentially, and a unity of validity is what it is only in relation to the connection in which the validity "makes itself." Husserl introduces the decisive distinction between real inclusion, as a part in a whole, and the inclusion of a validity-unity in the process of validating. Ideen I §§41 and 97 call this the difference between the reell content of an experience and its intentional object, and §49's formulation that the world is "merely intentional" depends on it.

"The world constitutes itself in consciousness. It is what it is only in relation to consciousness" (29.1–30). This does not mean that the real contains consciousness in its content, as if objective statements about it had to mention consciousness; to say that the house is here and what it is, I need not speak of consciousness. But if I consider how a house can be perceived and determined, I see two things. If the house is, as it is, possibilities of perceiving and determining it are necessary. And if such possibilities hold, and as valid possibilities they must be tied to actualities of consciousness, then the house is, and the sense "it is" is exhausted in them. So the house is nothing beside actual and possible consciousness; "it is" means that consciousness is and that lawful possibilities of consciousness hold in which it constitutes itself. "The being of the house is, so to speak, nothing but another 'expression' for consciousness and for such and such actual and possible courses of consciousness." Where thought posits a real, reflection finds the corresponding connections; where it finds them, the real is, and science can find it; and the connections include scientific thought itself. Two features of this formulation matter for Ideen I. The equivalence runs in both directions: being implies the possibilities, and possibilities tied to actuality imply being. And the parenthesis requiring valid possibilities to be tied to actual consciousness is the special thesis in compressed form. The formula "another expression for consciousness" is the riskiest in the volume and the one most open to a phenomenalist misreading; Husserl hedges it with "so to speak," and Ideen I does not reproduce it.

Science itself means possibilities and actualities of perception, presentation, memory, expectation, inference (29.31–30.10). Their totality consists of possibilities of consciousness, possibilities of thinking, finding, determining, and validly knowing the thing. The thing is the unifying element in these possible acts, a unity of thought. "The thing is not a second thing beside them," as if the possibilities could hold and the thing not be, or the thing be without them. Their "agreement" is not accidental, as though two series of absolute being happened to fit. "Thing" is nothing but an index of these actualities and possibilities; it is given as a unity is given, and unity is unthinkable without the consciousness to which it is or can be given as unity. The argument against two series is the core of the critique of realism. If thing and consciousness were two absolute series, their correspondence would be a miracle, the "pre-established harmony" the synopsis attributed to Lotze. Husserl removes the miracle by removing the second series. The term "index" (Anzeige) will return in Ideen I §§149–150 as the "clue" function of the object for the study of its manifolds.

So may I not after all say that "the sense of transcendent objectivity is determined from possible knowledge, as what we call a unity of a manifold, an intentional unity, a unity of meaning and of validity legitimating itself in consciousness, and not as something that would exist beside and outside this unity" (30.11–17)? The question is rhetorical and refers back to the note on the ambiguity of "sense." It restates the result in the language of sense so as to block the reading that sense here means content. The phrase "beside and outside" will recur in Text No. 4 (69) and in Ideen I §49.

"The flow of consciousness is!" (30.18–32). This being is itself consciousness, not only intentional being. It can of course be the object of acts of knowledge, but it is not only a unity constituted in the acts that think it. It is what it is "outside" all actual and possible acts directed at it, except for immediate perception; it is content of consciousness, lived experience. It stands to knowledge quite differently from the transcendent object, which cannot be lived through or given adequately in a simple seeing with all that it is. The transcendent is given as the unity of all perceptions of it and of its relations to other things, and of the harmonious, logically valid judgments founded on them. Here the asymmetry of Ideen I §§42–44 is turned into a contrast of modes of being. The experience is independent of reflection, the thing is not independent of its manifold; the experience is "perceived" because it is, the thing is because it is perceivable within a nexus. This is the exact sense of the claim that consciousness is absolute and the thing relative.

Is the transcendent a "focus imaginarius" within immanence (30.33–31.16)? Husserl rejects the image. Nothing here is imaginary; the thing is no figment. Transcendent being means objective validity, and validity presupposes consciousness, a meaning in which judgments of right can be passed. "Transcendent-real being" means objective validity in relation to determinate consciousness, for which correct judgment and knowledge are real possibilities. It expresses: consciousness filled with such and such content, plus motivational connections that rationally motivate this or that; rules for immanence and a determinate existing immanence. The scientist who lives in knowledge posits the transcendent absolutely. The motivational process runs "in" him, and living in it he sees and determines what is, attending to the identical and maintaining a continuous identity-consciousness that unifies his acts. Kant's "focus imaginarius" names the point toward which the ideas of reason direct the understanding without that point being an object. Husserl's refusal is significant: the thing is not a regulative idea projected beyond experience but an actual unity of validity, and its transcendence is real, not imaginary. Ideen I §143 does use the Kantian idea, but for the infinite completion of thing-givenness, not for the thing itself. The phrase "rules for immanence" and the stress on the scientist's absolute positing anticipate the double description in Ideen I §§47 and 55: the natural attitude rightly posits the world absolutely, and reflection shows this positing to be the achievement of motivated consciousness.

The perceiver knows nothing of his perceiving (31.17–23). He perceives things and the one same thing in many perceptions; he determines the thing without knowing of his determining; the thing stands before his "mental gaze" as this or that. Straightforward consciousness is anonymous to itself, and this explains why the natural attitude's absolutizing of the world is not a mistake within experience but a feature of how experience functions; Ideen I §§28 and 38 describe the same "living in" the act and the same need for a turn of reflection.

Only reflection exhibits the acts, and only in reflective thought can one ask how what is perceived in them relates to the perceiving (31.24–33). The acts may then be objectivated empirically as states and activities of a person, or taken as pure acts. Only then does it turn out that transcendent, intentional being necessarily presupposes an immanent being in the sense of absolutely pure acts and a certain connection among them, and that "intentional being exists" can in the last analysis mean nothing other than such connections. The fork between empirical and pure reflection is the place where the phenomenological reduction enters. Taken as states of a person, acts are themselves worldly and the question of the world's being would be begged; only as pure acts can they bear the thesis. Ideen I §§33 and 50 make this fork the first step of the fundamental consideration.

Only in this reflection does it appear what the being-in-itself of the transcendent amounts to, and its independence of individual consciousness and of particular acts (31.34–32.16). If we posit absolute consciousness, as we must, as being in the first and primal sense, then under "intentional being" we posit nothing beyond certain actual and possible connections of consciousness, coordinated and unified by essential laws according to which determinate consciousness has its rules and can run so that perception, judgment, and finally justified knowledge of things of a given kind take place. Given this, nothing new remains to be assumed, as though a "being in itself" were still missing. There is only one being in itself besides the absolute: that which comes to givenness through actual and possible consciousness of being in itself, in the manner of valid and rightfully grounded consciousness. To the essence of being belongs being able to be given, and being able to be rightfully, perhaps perfectly, given. Husserl keeps the phrase "being in itself" and redefines it. The in-itself of the thing is its independence from particular acts and persons, and this independence is itself a feature of what is given in valid consciousness. Text No. 12 will devote itself entirely to this redefinition.

Empirical being is rightfully given in the infinite process of experience, and it belongs to its essence that it makes no sense to expect or demand another givenness of it (32.17–33). To substruct beneath the being in itself of the thing a being unexperienceable in principle is nonsense. To ascribe unexperienceable determinations to the thing is to assert two incompatible things at once. On the one hand: these determinations belong to the thing, so they must be able to come to givenness and be grounded in what of the thing falls within experience, so they must emerge in the progress of possible experience and of ideal science. On the other hand: they cannot, since there is something behind the experienceable, an unexperienceable in-itself. A note says that the good sense of this argument is better presented in Text No. 3. The editor's introduction rightly treats this as the logical core of the whole volume. The argument does not deny that things have properties no one has experienced; it denies that they could have properties no possible experience could reveal. To be a determination of a thing is to be in principle determinable from what is experienced of it; an unexperienceable determination is a contradiction in terms. Ideen I §§48 and 52 apply this to the Kantian thing in itself and to the hypothesis of a divine intuition that would see things otherwise than through appearances.

"Intentional being is being only in relation to absolute consciousness. It is only apparently absolute being" (32.34–33.15). In empirical science the tie to subjectivity shows itself in the here and now, which cannot be kept out of the determination of facts. Every spatial and temporal determination presupposes a reference place and motion, the earth, the sun, and ultimately a this-here that can gain sense only through the subject. Insofar as we who judge reach understanding, we have these reference points before us and say "this thing," "this here," "the earth"; that is how the illusion of absolute things and absolute determinations arises. If we imagine, were it not absurd, the annulment of all determinate consciousness, the world constituted in determinate consciousness would also fall away; within determinate consciousness, however, the choice of reference point is indifferent, and determination can proceed equally well from different places in the absolute. A note: the world as intentional unity would fall away, and with it every world, since what is not consciousness is thinkable only as intentional unity. The argument from the indexical anchoring of physics is distinctive of this period and is not repeated in Ideen I, though §52 argues on the same basis that the physical thing is determined only through relations to the thing of perception. The illusion of absoluteness has an exact source: the indexical reference points are shared and taken for granted, so the subjective anchor drops out of view. The note anticipates the annihilation thesis of Ideen I §49 in its strongest form: not this world only, but any world.

Empirically, all physical being shows itself in relation to and "dependence" on me, you, perceiving persons; the thing belongs to the "surroundings" of humans and animals (33.16–23). Behind this, closer analysis finds a necessary relation of all empirical being, the being of humans and egos included, to absolute ego-being, absolute consciousness, which emerges only in the phenomenological reduction and in metaphysical consideration. Husserl separates two relativities. The first is empirical and concerns persons in the world; the second is transcendental and includes persons among the relata. The term "phenomenological reduction" is already in use (it appears in Husserl's lectures from 1907), and here it is paired with "metaphysics," a pairing Ideen I suppresses by postponing metaphysics (§58) while keeping its result.

"All empirical being is relative" (33.24–33). It appears to an ego, appears differently, appears to different egos, and even if it appears to no ego in the strict sense, it can be determined only through relation to ego-appearances. But this factual relativity has an essential one behind it. It belongs to the essence of the transcendent to appear, to present itself, and to be given and givable only through appearances. A priori, the relation to the absolute ego, to absolute consciousness "in its various ego-concretions," belongs to the essence of transcendent-real being. The phrase "ego-concretions" shows that the absolute is from the start thought as plural. One absolute consciousness individuates itself in many concrete egos, and the world is relative to this plurality. Ideen I mostly argues solipsistically, as Beilage IV will say of the 1915 lecture, but §§29 and 151 presuppose this plurality, and Text No. 7 makes it a condition of the thesis.

How then does the difficulty stand, that nothing of the subject belongs to the essence of the empirically real object, and yet its relation to subjectivity belongs to its essence (34.1–11)? First, we can determine what appears "purely objectively," physically, abstracting from subjectivity. But perception gives the object, and through perception it is a this-here, something to be identified in further perceptions. Thereby it has a relation to some ego, the ego of the knower, who belongs to the world, communicates with others, can exchange places with them. The solution begins by locating the relation to subjectivity precisely. It lies not in the object's properties but in the act of identification that singles it out.

The relation to subjectivity thus remains, but is eliminated down to a small residue (34.12–38). It does not enter the content of the identical but only its "this," its individuation. This marks only one direction of judgment, which cannot cut the relation entirely: if nothing can be posited as a this, nothing can be determined in the sense of physics or psychology. Psychology is no different: it determines the person by determining the body as a this-here that "has" experiences and reacts to stimuli and motives. Here one must distinguish the determined subjectivity from the determining one. The relation to the determining subjectivity is excluded as far as possible but remains, in the perception, in the intuitive positing of the this, and in the relation to reference places and times. The same holds when I determine myself as a person: my experiences then belong to the content of my person as determined, but the determining acts remain outside that content. The distinction between determined and determining subjectivity is the germ of the distinction between empirical and transcendental subject and of the "paradox of subjectivity" Husserl will confront in the Crisis (§53). It also explains why the transcendental relation is invisible to science: it attaches to the "this," not to the "what."

Every empirical object therefore has a relation to the determining thinker; this relation extends exactly as far as real transcendence does (35.1–13). Yet what is posited and determined can be determined at first only as it is "in itself," with its properties and states; the relation to subjectivity is contained in it but hidden. One can also consider how the object presents itself differently in different appearances, with judgments directed at the same object, first in relation to empirical observers; and the same holds for the observers themselves, insofar as they are transcendent. The final clause carries the regress that forces the step to the absolute. If observers are themselves transcendent, their relation to objects is another relation among realities, and the relativity of the real cannot be grounded on it.

Hence the return to absolute consciousness (35.14–31). There we find the manifold of actual and possible intuitive presentations, judgments, conjectures, and we recognize that if they are, and if their motivations hold rightfully in evidence, the being of the object is already fully included in them, and conversely that this manifold belongs to the essence of the existing object. The object may be separable from the empirical ego, or empirically inseparable from some empirical ego; but inseparability from the absolute ego, absolute consciousness, belongs to its essence. Everything that in objective consideration really constitutes the object is a unity of consciousness and as such unthinkable without consciousness, actual and possible. When objective consideration abstracts from the ego, this means that it lives in determining and simply states the determination; reflection shows that the objective is only in and with consciousness. The double inclusion, being in the manifold and the manifold in being, is Husserl's equivalence thesis, and the distinction between empirical separability and essential inseparability fixes the scope of the idealism precisely: it concerns the absolute, not any human mind.

We can judge about an object simply, about earth, sun, and stars, or about its knowledge, the a priori conditions of its knowability and the possible connections of perception and judgment that make up its givenness (35.32–36.12). If acts of the right group exist, perceptions, memories, judgments, they are the consciousness "there is a house, so constituted"; if the right evidences hold in grounding, they are the consciousness "this is truly and actually so" or "with reason it is, and must confirm itself further." Conceiving the sequence of ever new confirmations, we have "it is ever newly grounded." If we conceive this group of possible acts ideally, then "the object is actual" holds ideally with absolute certainty; conversely, if it is actual, this group belongs to it. Husserl's own note: "But that is not proved!" The note is an honest verdict on the equivalence just asserted. The direction from ideal completeness of confirmation to actuality is plausible; the converse, that actuality requires the group, is what the realist denies, and Husserl sees that he has stated rather than demonstrated it. Text No. 3 is his attempt to supply the proof.

What then can it mean that "the object is in itself, independent of all such consciousness, has nothing to do with consciousness" (36.13–26)? As transcendent it is contained in none of these acts as an immanent content; there is no adequate simple perception of it as there is, or seems to be, of inner states. So it is reell in no consciousness, for if it were, reflection on it and adequate perception of it would be possible a priori. But if it is not immanent, it still is something with such and such properties, and that must be capable of being given. So perception of it is possible, and determination and grounding of it, hence in consciousness. It is not itself consciousness but other; yet always something perceivable, determinable, groundable. The realist's "independence" is thus split into two claims. One is true: the thing is not a part of consciousness. The other is false: that it could be something not givable to consciousness. Ideen I §§41–42 and 44 keep the true claim, as the transcendence of the thing to the stream, and refute the false one.

The first of two theses follows (36.27–31). Suppose absolute consciousness regulated so that it contains the sources of all possible grounding. Is anything then missing from any being? No being could be struck out; the relation to consciousness belongs to the essence of all being. A note refers again to Text No. 3. The thesis is sufficiency: a consciousness whose regulation includes all grounds leaves no room for an additional being in itself. It is the positive counterpart to the countersense argument.

The second thesis concerns necessity (36.32–37.11). Can there be being in itself that is groundable in possible consciousness but such that no absolutely existing consciousness contains motives for assuming it? Suppose consciousness so explored that every motive is followed in ideal perfection and nothing points to the being of this "thing." Could it then be? Are the possibilities of knowledge empty possibilities? What does it mean that possibilities of perceiving and knowing "hold"? In an "empty" sense, possibilities of knowledge hold for everything merely possible but not actual, for example a diamond the size of the sun. If a thing actually exists, there are not merely such logical possibilities but real possibilities, "and that has no other sense than that they are motivated possibilities, which have their motivation in an actual consciousness of knowledge." This is the decisive step toward Ideen I §47. The realist appeal to "possible experience" fails because possibility comes in two kinds. Logical possibility holds for every consistent fiction, so it cannot distinguish the existing from the non-existing. Real possibility distinguishes them, but only because it is motivated by actual experience. The sun-sized diamond becomes Husserl's standard example of a consistent but merely logical possibility; it reappears in Text No. 5 and Text No. 9.

If actual consciousness and actual possibilities of consciousness belong to every real being, what can "being" still mean beside the totality of absolute consciousness, the flow of the absolute egos, apart from the regulation of consciousness by a priori and empirical "laws" (37.12–30)? A note qualifies: "but not only that." It belongs to the essence of consciousness to have its "intention" and in it to be consciousness "of" something; that this something is, is a thing or property of a thing, means that within absolute consciousness motivated possibilities exist for further acts of evident grounding "for this same something." The ego that lives through these groundings knows of this object's being and properties, and knows that it is even when not perceived or thought. Thingly being is not an act and not a piece of the flow, and yet is something only to be given through perception and experience and grounded logically. Husserl's note blocks an overreading of his own sentence. Being does not reduce to the regulation of consciousness alone; intentionality, the "of," belongs to it as well, and it is through the intentional reference that the thing is something other than the flow while being nothing outside it. The closing contrast, not an act yet only givable through acts, is the formula Ideen I §§41–42 work out.

"So with all this we are doing metaphysics," general metaphysics, as against "pure natural science" as the a priori ontology of nature: "the reduction of being (ὄν) to the absolute, consciousness" (37.31–38.14). The editor notes a zero in the margin here. The task can then be set of an eidetics of knowledge, running through all kinds of knowledge and the correlative kinds of objects whose eidetics lies in a priori ontology, pure logic in the widest sense. Posed purely a priori, this yields no doctrine of the absolute as it is but of the essence of the absolute and its "regulations" corresponding to logical laws and categories. It enables us to cut off every skewed and countersensical interpretation of the sciences of reality and of mathematics. That the constitution of sciences, value-formations, the ideal state, belongs to the essence of consciousness is clear. If we want to speak of reality in the absolute sense, we must speak of what is actual and valuable: first objective science, then the reduction to consciousness. A final note: "Best to go straight to Bα 1–6 (Text No. 3)." The text distinguishes three disciplines: objective science and ontology, which determine what things are; an eidetic phenomenology of knowledge, which determines the essence of absolute consciousness and its regulations; and a metaphysics of the factual absolute, which would determine the absolute as it is. Ideen I presents itself as the second, an eidetic science of transcendental consciousness, and defers the third. The ordering "first objective science, then the reduction" also explains the structure of the fundamental consideration, which begins from the world of the natural attitude (§§27–30) before reducing it.

## Appendix I. On Establishing Epistemology. The Immediate Givenness of Real Transcendent Objects in External Perception. Critique of the Inference Theory of External Perception. The Legitimation of All Being in Thought

These are the "two sheets" that Text No. 2 cites, dated by the editor to 1908 or 1909. They open with the epistemologist's question in quotation marks: "How can an experience 'know' something that is not an experience?" (38.21–29). Knowledge is supposed to "hit" an object that is what it is whether known or not; it is supposed to be a belief that something is and is so, and a belief that is valid. The riddle is first that knowledge is to "agree" with the object, and then how I could know of this agreement and what agreement could even mean here. The appendix is introductory by design. It collects the perplexities that motivate epistemology before any of them is solved, and its first move is to fix the realist correspondence concept of truth as the source of the puzzle.

The puzzle is spelled out (38.30–39.5). The object is taken in advance to exist, and knowledge is a second object, a state in my consciousness. How does this state come to be correct, to say something that holds of the object? What does the object care about knowledge, and how can knowledge care about the object? The object is as it is whether knowledge is or not, and knowledge is not correct because the object conforms to it. How then is knowledge to conform to the object, to something outside consciousness? Husserl is playing Kant's "Copernican" alternatives against each other: neither object conforming to knowledge nor knowledge conforming to object makes sense if both are self-standing. The phenomenological solution will be neither alternative but the denial of the self-standing second term.

Three presuppositions are now identified (39.6–16): the object's being in itself, decided in advance; knowledge as my contingent state, the state of a contingent I; and the idea of "agreement," of correctness. Knowledge is taken to express the known, either as an image of the object or as an analogue of an image. Linguistic expression is an example: I say "S is P"; these are my words and thoughts, and in the thing S the being-P really holds. Even if I had a fully adequate image of the object, how could I know that I had it? The image theory fails at its own test, since an image can be compared with its original only by someone who has access to the original independently of the image. The same argument governs the critique of the image and sign theories in the appendix to the fifth Logical Investigation and in Ideen I §43.

All knowing is either immediate or mediate, and the mediate presupposes the immediate (39.17–21). So the problem is aimed first at immediate knowledge; if that is not clarified, neither is the mediate. The step narrows the inquiry to perception. It also anticipates the order of Ideen I's phenomenology of reason, which treats immediate rational positing (§§136–140) before mediate positing (§141).

Take immediate perception (39.22–26). Can we know a priori that an object ever corresponds to it? That would be a miracle. A posteriori? Every a posteriori knowledge presupposes perception, and if it is to be mediate it presupposes immediate knowledge. Can this be immanent perception? The dilemma closes every route but one: if perception does not carry its own right, nothing can supply it.

The usual way out is inference (39.27–32). Starting from immediate immanent perception, we infer to external things. I am at first sure of my phenomena and know nothing of anything outside me. The phenomena show a mixture of regularity and irregularity, and the hypothesis of something standing in a causal relation to them verifies itself within the appearances. A marginal note refers to the alleged "inference" to the transcendent in Text No. 1. The version described is the hypothetico-causal realism of Helmholtz and of much nineteenth-century philosophy of science, with the external world as the best explanation of the regularities in sensation.

But who has ever made this mode of inference clear (39.33–40.6)? If appearances, psychic states, immanent contents are the only things truly given, "existing" can mean only "existing as immanent content," and at most an inference from contents to contents is intelligible, for instance the expectation that d will follow a b c because the series has recurred. How could we infer to something that is not a content? We see things that no one takes for contents of consciousness; we do not infer from non-things to things. We do infer from the seen to the unseen, and here the right of seeing is presupposed, even though we speak of illusions and hallucinations. "Only perception unseats perception." The objection is a dilemma. Within immanentist premises an inference can only reach further contents, never a non-content, so the inference theory either fails to reach the world or tacitly presupposes the perceptual givenness of things that it denies. The aphorism about perception unseating perception states the principle that Ideen I §§138 and 151 use for the cancellation of experience: an illusion is unmasked by further perception, not by inference from sensations.

It is the miracle of seeing that is in question, and seeing is supposed to have a right (40.7–20). Apart from exceptions, the perceived objects, which are not immanent contents, are supposed to exist, though not exactly as they present themselves, and to do so by virtue of the right of perception itself and of the thought built on it. "So it is fundamentally false that we infer the external world." We see it and determine it on the basis of perceptions and experiences. Only when we have it rightfully can we carry out psychophysical considerations and ask whether causal relations hold between my body, my soul, and the perceived things. But I cannot use those causal relations to infer an external world that I see directly and whose seeing is the foundation of all science of the external world, even though that science ends by saying the external world is not in truth as it appears. The order of founding is fixed: perception first, then the causal account of perception within the world it presents. The inference theory reverses this order and uses a result of science to ground the experience on which science rests. Ideen I §52 repeats the argument against the physical thing as the cause of appearances, and grants that physics rightly says the world is not as it appears while denying that this makes the appearing world a sign of a hidden one.

"So perception is a riddle" (40.21–41.7). How can connections of perceptions and thought, immanent processes, have significance for the outside of consciousness, such that, if they run in a certain way, things that are not consciousness are rightly posited? Or does it make no proper sense, after all, to speak of an "outside" of consciousness? Perhaps one may speak only of things and egos, of ego-experiences such as perceptions, and of experiencing egos and perceived things, which are not experiences; and then of consciousness, which is neither a thing nor an ego (a note: the ego as person), but a title for the absolute being of "contents of consciousness" in the flow in which egos and things first constitute themselves. Perhaps constituting means a regulation of the flow by virtue of which apperception and judgment are possible in accordance with norms, laws of motivation, grounded in the essence of consciousness, and thereby carry a validity according to which what appears "is what it is" and keeps its identity. Is objectivity in the end something belonging to the essence of consciousness, so that consciousness is not itself an object but the necessary correlate of possible objectivity in general, objectivity in the sense of the self-identical real? The questions sketch the solution in advance. "Outside consciousness" loses its sense, consciousness is distinguished from both ego and thing, constitution is defined as lawful regulation by laws of motivation, and objectivity is redefined as validity-unity. Each element reappears in Ideen I: consciousness as the absolute "in which" all transcendence constitutes itself (§49), the empirical ego as constituted (§§53–54), motivation as the basic law of the spiritual world (§47 and, more fully, Ideen II §56), and the correlation of being and rational consciousness (§§135, 142).

Husserl checks himself (41.8–22). This already leads deep into the matters. At the beginning, what matters is to clarify the kind of unclarity that motivates an immanent phenomenology and a phenomenological critique of "reason." The beginning is naivety: obviously things are and I am, with my changing experiences, some of which give phenomena of things. How can I know of things in themselves? I think I perceive them, but perception is my state; how can it call out to me with any right that a thing of such a kind exists, something that does not fall into my state or into my consciousness at all? How can any inference ground the being and nature of things, and what does the course of reality care about my "logical" sequence of experiences? The phrase "phenomenological critique of reason" gives the programme its Kantian title. The appendix is the "installation" of that critique, not its execution, and its method is to make the perplexity precise before resolving it.

The real and formal significance of the logical is discussed, as in Lotze and Kant (41.23–30). Is there a pre-established harmony between thought and being? Would that be an explanation, and how could I ground it? Do I not see that I can speak of being with right only if my speaking and thinking have right, so that the right to speak of being and to posit it must legitimate itself to me, and that, if it does so within thought, I can have no use for a hypothesis of harmony? Lotze's Logik (1874) had asked how the forms of thought could have a "real" significance for things and answered with a harmony between the two. Husserl objects that the harmony would itself need grounding, and the only grounding available would be within thought, which would make the harmony superfluous. The argument is a transcendental short-circuit: any bridge between thought and being would have to be known, and known by thought.

"So everything must legitimate itself in thought itself," as Lotze too had noted without making the right use of it (41.31–35). Do I not then see that I may not set a being over against thought in advance, but can ground being only in thought and on the basis of its motives? This is the principle of legitimation from Text No. 1 stated against its historical background. Being is not presupposed as a partner of thought but grounded through thought's motives, which is Text No. 2's thesis that being is a unity of validity.

One might say: I see that logic, reason, rules (41.36–42.13). I have perceptions, memories, abstractions, counts, measurements, judgments; I posit things and events in them, and I see that if I proceed according to logical laws, I proceed correctly, that is, the things then exist, and I see the truth of logic generally. That would be a step forward; before logic and its lawful procedures I would have no things, persons, or egos. But are these procedures not mine, human procedures? Are the ego and the human being not presupposed after all? And logic itself: what of its "evidence"? I follow it in science, but I also see things that are not; evidence deceives, can it not always deceive? And granting logic, how is it intelligible that thought in accordance with it should be correct and hit something that does not fall within consciousness? One will readily concede that logic holds for the sphere of consciousness, but for the transcendent? The appendix ends on its hardest perplexity. A rationalist solution, logic as the ruler of being, relapses into psychologism if logic is taken as human procedure, and into skepticism if evidence can deceive. Both relapses are answered later by the distinction between transcendental and psychological consciousness (Ideen I §§53–54) and by the theory of evidence by regions (§§136–145). The question whether logic holds of the transcendent returns in Appendix III as the problem of the a priori.

## Appendix II. Problems of Transcendence. The Problem of External Perception. The Analogous Difficulties of Self-Knowledge. The False Ideal of Immanent Perception of the Transcendent and the Inference Theory of Perception. The Riddle of Knowledge of an In-Itself

The note on the manuscript reads: "Summer semester 1909. Notabene. Good. Read 1912, 1918." The first question is how something can be posited as existing, with supposed right, and determined in knowledge, when it is not "truly and actually self-given," that is, immanent, in it (42.22–33). We have a nature we believe we know. Is nature, or any thing, ever immanently given in knowledge? And even if sense-perception were immanent perception, how could we posit nature beyond the immediately given? By inference? But how is inferential, indirect knowledge to be understood, and how can it know something not immanently given in the knowing? The repeated readings in 1912 and 1918 show that Husserl treated these sheets as a working inventory; the 1912 reading falls in the period of the drafting of Ideen I.

At first all this is self-evident, immediate perception and inference from the perceived to the unperceived (42.34–43.6). In reflection the possibility of immediate perception of the transcendent becomes puzzling: it seems not to be an actual grasping of the object, since the object is not immanent to it. One may still think inference intelligible, but on reflection it too becomes a riddle. Husserl records how the problem of knowledge arises. It is not found in experience but produced by reflection, which brings with it an unexamined ideal of givenness as immanence.

The second problem is self-knowledge (43.7–27). Does immanent perception not have analogous difficulties? Reflection is directed at a psychic experience that was not previously perceived but was supposedly immanent to consciousness. We think we know there is unperceived consciousness; when we reflect on a judging we are sure we "look back" at a judging that was already an experience a moment before. We posit as just past something no longer really immanent to the positing. Recollection likewise posits something as past, and does so immediately; how can consciousness reach beyond itself to the past? One attitude finds this unproblematic: that I have manifold experiences, can survey them and single some out for perception is obvious; immanent perception as a direct singling out of the lived is intelligible; the actual but unperceived experience is an in-itself over against knowledge, and this in-itself is no riddle. A note corrects the formulation: knowledge of the immanent, first in experiencing and also in recollection, is no riddle. The parallel is important and double-edged. It shows that the "in-itself" of an unperceived experience, which no one finds mysterious, has the same formal structure as the in-itself of the thing, namely independence of the act that grasps it; so the formal independence cannot be what makes the thing puzzling. It also shows that retention and recollection break the identification of givenness with real immanence, the gap Text No. 1 noted. Ideen I §45 ("unperceived experience, unperceived reality") draws the first lesson; Husserl's time-consciousness analyses draw the second.

But the possibility of transcendent knowledge, as reaching beyond the sphere of consciousness, is a riddle, knowledge directed at no present or just-past experience of the knowing consciousness (43.28–44.4). It is a riddle how something in itself like a natural object can be knowable. In particular, external perception is not "perception in the strictest sense": it says "the object is itself there," yet it is not in the true sense there for knowledge. That may be due to a limitation of human knowledge, so often lamented. "But is it conceivable that this defect is remedied?" And does it not, in its "defectiveness," already pose a deep problem? The question whether the defect is remediable is the turning point. If inadequacy is a contingent human limitation, a better intellect could overcome it; if it belongs to the essence of thing-perception, it is no defect at all but the way things are given. Ideen I §§43–44 decide for the second.

The first way of remedying the defect is science (44.5–17). External perception is not supposed to be worthless: it is imperfect consciousness of objects, not fully justifying, but still justifying. How can a subjective experience have even imperfect justification for positing the self-presence of an object? And how do the "experience-logical" processes that build on this imperfect ground produce an improvement and a scientific knowledge of a nature in itself? These processes are throughout immanent connections of consciousness, yet they are supposed to be knowledge of something that is whether consciousness is or not. The notion of "imperfect justification" is the one Ideen I §138 develops into the doctrine of the weight and degrees of rational positing. Here it is still a puzzle, since partial right presupposes a partial reach of the immanent to the transcendent, which is the very thing in question.

The second way is God (44.18–29). Humans cannot improve perception in this way, but we are inclined to attribute it to a divine mind. God naturally has no imperfect acts of knowledge, so of things he has perfect perception, a direct and perfect self-grasping, and what is that but adequate perception? The problem then is how, given such perfect perceptual knowledge, the being in itself of nature over against knowledge would still be thinkable. The thought experiment turns the realist's resource against him. If God perceives things adequately, things become, for God, immanent contents, and their in-itself disappears. Ideen I §§43 and 52 draw the opposite conclusion from the same premise: since a thing is essentially given by adumbration, even God would perceive it through adumbrations, and the idea of an adequate perception of a thing is a countersense rather than a divine privilege.

The dilemma is now formulated (44.30–45.10). Setting indirect inferences aside on the assumption that they will become intelligible once their immediate starting point is clarified, we look at perception. That perception grasps the immanent is self-evident and fully intelligible; the riddle is how it is to grasp the non-immanent. Perception of the non-immanent seems an imperfection whose removal requires a return to a perfection of the corresponding kind. Hence some say that external perception is a merely instinctive grasping of what exists; to justify it fully we would have to start from what is immanently given in it and infer to the non-immanent. And yet we are compelled to regard "proper," "immanent," "adequate" perception of the non-immanent as possible. A note warns that the privileging of perception has its traps: since all experience leads back to perception, one overlooks that its derived forms, such as recollection, also pose difficulties. The "instinctive" view is Brentano's and Stumpf's: external perception is blind belief, not knowledge. Husserl locates the hidden ideal behind it, an adequate perception of the transcendent, and shows that the ideal is the source of the inference theory.

When we look more closely at this ideal of immanent perception of the transcendent, it turns out that such perception can only be perception of the subjective, and the possibility of something perceived but not conscious seems not to exist (45.11–14). The ideal is incoherent. To perceive something immanently is to perceive it as a content of consciousness, so "immanent perception of the transcendent" cancels itself. With the ideal falls the standard by which external perception was judged imperfect.

Those who, like Brentano, hold the ideal of immanent perception and regard it as "the only perception in the proper sense" ought consistently to teach that perception of the transcendent is not possible as proper perception at all, and that only inferences from the immanent justify the positing of the transcendent and so the so-called external perception (45.15–27). But then comes the difficulty of how inferences from the immanent lead beyond it. And does God too make inferences in his perfect knowledge of things? If he creates things according to "ideas," can he have absolute knowledge of them except by perceiving them fully, in the proper sense? But is the world then "immanent" to God, is space God's sensorium? Brentano's Psychology from an Empirical Standpoint (1874) had called inner perception the only perception in the proper sense. Husserl draws the consequence that Brentano did not: on that view external perception is not perception at all and the inference theory is unavoidable, with all its difficulties. The question about God and Newton's image of space as God's sensorium press the same dilemma at the level of theology. A creator's perfect knowledge either makes the world immanent to God, which abolishes its transcendence, or it is perception of the transcendent, which concedes that perception of the transcendent need not be inference.

A further difficulty: all natural science, in its experience-logical procedure, ascribes to immediate perception and memory an immediate right that helps carry the right of all mediate grounding (45.28–46.4). How is this to be understood? Does immediate experience gain its right only in the further process, by "confirmation" through new immediate experiences that again have no right of their own? Clearly not: what kind of grounding would justify an arbitrary assertion by its agreement with other arbitrary assertions? And "perceptions agree" means "perceptual statements agree." But does science ground the right of its perceptions, or does it confine itself to devaluing certain perceptions as illusions that conflict with undoubted ones? Husserl adds: "I have the impression, and it is certainly so, that Brentano and Stumpf confuse the scientific process that advances from perception to perception with a grounding of transcendent being by inferences from connections of immanent being." The argument against a pure coherence theory is decisive for Husserl's later position. Harmony among experiences confirms only if each experience already carries some right of its own; coherence amplifies an original right, it cannot create one. Ideen I §§138–141 rest on this: the perception's own "motivated" right is a weight that confirmation increases. The diagnosis of Brentano and Stumpf names the confusion of the empirical, scientific advance from perception to perception with an epistemological derivation of transcendence from immanence. The editor's introduction documents Stumpf's lectures as the target.

The in-itself is a riddle (46.5–11). How is knowledge to grasp what is in itself over against it? And for knowledge of nature: what if the supposedly immediate perception can always deceive? What use is any further course of knowledge if it never leads to the in-itself, so that it seems possible that knowledge runs as it does while the in-itself does not exist at all? The final possibility, a course of experience complete in itself while its supposed object does not exist, is the skeptical hypothesis that Text No. 3 and Ideen I §49 turn around: if experience ran harmoniously with no world, the "world" posited by it would exist in the only sense existence of a world can have.

The riddle has a double face (46.12–33). First: if mediate knowledge points back to immediate knowledge that must finally confirm it, immediate knowledge must be valid beyond doubt if knowledge is to have validity at all. If immediate experience were so clear a grasp of an in-itself that any doubt whether it was, while its object was not, were senseless, all would be in order; so far as knowledge leads back to absolute self-givenness excluding doubt as senseless, it is no riddle. Knowledge of nature does not meet this demand. A note marks an ambiguity: while I live in experience, doubt is "senseless" and impossible so long as no counter-experience checks me, though I can always think that I might later come to doubt or find non-being. Second: immediate knowledge must be absolute self-givenness excluding every possible doubt, that is, it must enclose the known; the known must be "something psychic" like the knowing; so what is in itself over against knowledge cannot be perceived if the in-itself is to be foreign and transcendent to consciousness. The two faces are the epistemological and the ontological versions of one demand, indubitability and real inclusion.
