The note already holds the solution of Ideen I §46: transcendent perception is not doubtful in fact but dubitable in principle, and this possibility of doubt is the mark of its kind of givenness, not a ground for distrusting it.

A third problem may be added (47.1–8). If the known is in itself over against knowing, being known is inessential to it. It seems obvious that different objects in themselves have different experiences belonging to them; but how can the intrinsic nature of knowing stand in essential connection with the nature of the known in itself? How can this relation itself be known, if one term is "in itself"? The problem shows that the realist must assume an essential correlation between kinds of objects and kinds of experience, and cannot, on his premises, know it. Husserl's alternative makes this correlation the theme of the phenomenology of constitution, with the region as the clue to its manifolds (Ideen I §§149–152).

The fourth problem is the possibility of a science of being in itself, or of science at all (47.9–23). Knowledge wants to know what is, and science wants to establish it in a way that rises above the single act and the single subject. How is truth in itself possible for a subject, and science for a knowing subject? And how is intersubjective science possible? Once the possibility of knowing the in-itself and the necessity of an inner relation between act and object are clear, then, if an intersubjective nature is added, with its justification presupposed, it can become intelligible how intersubjective knowledge is possible. In communication one knower can ascribe acts of knowledge to another, arouse them in him, prompt him to perform the same acts, and the same acts ground and know the same. The intersubjective question is placed last and treated as derivative. The sketch, communication as the arousal of the same acts in another, is still close to the Logical Investigations' theory of intimation; the later texts of this volume will reverse the order and make intersubjectivity a condition of the idealist thesis itself.

## Appendix III. The Problems of Epistemology. Perception as Givenness of the Transcendent, and Natural Science. Hume's Problem of the Thing. Problems of A Priori Knowledge. Questions

The note reads: "End of May 1910. Read 1918. Good. Valuable problematic." The first question is this (47.30–34). I have experience of things, all empirical knowledge rests on immediate perception, and every immediate perception can deceive. How can the existence of things be adequately grounded, when every grounding of existence presupposes the existence of things, of a nature? The circle is stated in its sharpest form. Every empirical correction of a perception is made by other perceptions of the same nature, so the existence of nature is presupposed by every attempt to establish it. The phenomenological reduction will answer this circle by refusing to presuppose existence at all and asking only for the structure of legitimation (Ideen I §§32, 55).

The second question concerns science (48.1–13). Suppose perception guarantees the existence of the perceived and memory reports the past, and both have their right. How do I get beyond the immediately given and "infer" from a given existent to an ungiven one? This concerned single objects; how further can general knowledge of things and laws of nature be gained? Husserl notes that the questions, posed for natural objects, hold for individual objects generally. The problem of transcendence is separated from the problem of induction: even granting the right of perception, the step from the perceived to the unperceived and to the universal remains to be justified.

The questions can be put from a skeptical standpoint (48.14–28). Assume there is no psychophysical nature at all; still, the cogitatio I now grasp in immanent perception is, and the certainty of recollection that a certain cogitatio was is granted. How can one go beyond these givens, infer to something ungiven, a nature, and gain general knowledge, if one holds that external objects are not given in perception? One may recall the familiar theories of the inferential proof of an external world and ask whether, from the data of immanent perception and recollection, a conceivable path can be found to "infer" an external existence, a nature of physics, and build a physics. The skeptical starting point is the Cartesian one: certain cogitationes, nothing else. Husserl now poses the inference theory's task in its full form, as the construction of physics from sense data, the form Russell and Carnap would attempt in the following two decades.

These questions recall Hume's problem, and Hume's problem of existence as well (48.29–31). Husserl distinguishes the two Humean problems: induction and causal inference on one side (the Treatise's Book I, Part iii), the belief in continued and distinct existence on the other (Part iv, section 2). The next question takes up the second.

Hume's empirical problem of the thing (48.32–49.4): in thing-perception a thing is perceived, an enduring, identical objectivity, now changing, now unchanged, supposedly given immediately. But it is said that what is "immediately given" is what really makes up the perception, what I find reell in it: a complex of sense contents. Even when the object stands there unchanged, the perception usually changes, as when I turn my head, qualitatively as well. And in repeated, separate perceptions the same object is supposed to be perceived. How can groups of temporally separate and variously changing contents, "which alone are what is immediately given," count for consciousness as one identical unchanged thing? The problem is exactly the one Husserl solves with the distinction between reell content and intentional object: sensations change while the thing appears unchanged because the thing is not composed of sensations but constituted through their apprehension. The solution is the theory of apprehension of Thing and Space (1907) and Ideen I §§41 and 85.

Hume thinks we are subject to a play of the imagination, a fiction to be explained psychologically (49.5–14). But apart from psychological explanation, there is a confusing situation here that needs to be sorted out. Is not consciousness of something "not given," of something transcendent, that passes itself off as perception, necessarily a deception, a fiction? Is not proper perception necessarily immanent perception, and must not all non-immanent perception be branded as something that oddly passes for perception and is not (Brentano)? Husserl separates Hume's psychological explanation from the descriptive problem it answers. The fiction theory and Brentano's "improper perception" share a premise: that givenness means immanence. Both therefore must declare thing-perception a counterfeit.

We become more confused still when we add the question whether knowledge of the individual is thinkable without immediate experience, and whether immediate experience is not the same as perception (49.15–27). Is immediate perception of a thing thinkable other than as "supposed," "improper" perception? Is it thinkable as proper? This already leads deep. To see that the talk of things as not properly perceived but only "instinctively" assumed, and of natural science as leading from the properly perceived, the immanent, to an imperceptible and wholly unknown nature in itself, is wrong-headed philosophy, and that even a God cannot, in principle, see nature otherwise than we do, already belongs to sorting out the difficulties. This is the first explicit statement in the volume of the thesis that a divine perception of things would also be perspectival. It answers the question Appendix II left open and states in one sentence the result of Ideen I §§43–44 and 52: the inadequacy of thing-perception is essential, so it is no defect, and so it cannot be remedied by inference or by a higher intellect.

The fourth problem is a priori knowledge, purely a priori and in its application to the a posteriori, which here covers singular knowledge both of nature and of immanent individuals (49.28–41). First, the problem of "general objects." Are objects not eo ipso individual objects given in perception? Yet we often treat numbers as objects, as when we speak of the numbers of the series, and propositional forms likewise. How do such objects come to givenness? General objects too have a transcendence: they are not the experiences in which they are given or seen. Are they too "inferred"? This is bound up with universal, unconditionally general, a priori predicative thought. The extension of transcendence to the ideal is a key result of these appendices. If general objects are transcendent to the acts that grasp them and are nonetheless given, transcendence and givenness are not opposed; the inference theory is refuted from the side of ideal objects. Ideen I §§3 and 22–23 rest the possibility of eidetic intuition on the same point.

The knowledge of relations of ideas is next (50.1–27); a note says this is closely connected with the preceding point, since these generalities too are, in the widest sense, objects in themselves. Hume the sensualist takes as immediate his impressions and ideas, sense contents, phantasms, and sensory feelings, and relations among them that depend on their content alone. But how do I know of such a law, of a relation between ideas that gives an a priori law? I can only note in these two impressions or ideas, now lived through, that they found a similarity; with what right do I say that ideas of this kind always and necessarily found a relation of similarity of this degree? One answer is that the law "depends" a priori on the ideas as general objects and must therefore hold in every case, which would be a step forward. But then it is a wonderful thing that consciousness has general knowledge and is certain that what it grasps "with insight" in pure thought must hold in every case of experience. Does the course of singular being conform to insight, to the contingent consciousness in which the a priori stands? If this difficulty arises already for immanent contents, it arises the more for the application of the a priori to things of nature. What have "immanent" data to do with nature? Do colours and sounds in the sense of nature resemble the immanent data, are they the same in essence, and how could we know it? Husserl shows that Hume's relations of ideas already require general objects that Hume's sensualism cannot admit. He then raises a problem his own theory must answer: how eidetic laws grasped in insight bind factual being. The answer of Ideen I §§6–8 is that facts are individuations of essences, so that essential laws hold of them necessarily; but the question whether the colour of the thing and the colour-sensation are "the same in essence" remains, and Ideen I §§41 and 85 answer it by distinguishing the sensed colour (hyle) from the perceived colour of the thing (an adumbrated property).

The fifth problem follows (50.28–51.6). Some may find it intelligible that the idea of a kind of immanent particulars is grasped by "abstraction" and that general laws grounded in such ideas are seen generally. But how can such abstraction grasp generalities on the basis of the quasi-perception of transcendent, natural objects? If it cannot, is there no ontology as eidetics of nature? Yet we have such ontological propositions: that a thing having a property cannot have a conflicting one, which seems a tautology and in a way is, though for every property of a thing there are conflicting ones a priori; that having one property excludes having another at the same time and place, different shades of colour, different degrees of warmth. The question concerns the possibility of a material a priori of nature. If ideation could work only on immanent data, a regional ontology of nature would be impossible. Husserl's answer, implicit here and explicit in Ideen I §§3–4 and 9, is that ideation can start from any intuition, including the inadequate intuition of things and even phantasy.

Then the whole of geometry (51.7–11), and, a note adds, the pure theory of time no less. Geometrical objects are "pure" objects, yet they do not come to givenness as immanent contents or as generalizations of them. A figure is a unity of a manifold but never adequately given; how then can I abstract its idea? Geometry is the crucial case because its objects are both ideal and spatial, and space is given only through adumbrations. The remark that a figure is never adequately given will turn, in Ideen I §§74 and 143, into the distinction between exact ideal concepts and morphological descriptive concepts and into the idea of thing-givenness as an idea in the Kantian sense.

How do ideal concepts, formed (unintelligibly how) on the basis of empirical intuition, come to be unconditional norms for real science (51.12–23)? How do I come to apply pure geometry constantly as an instrument in natural science? Why must nature conform to the "imaginings" of a priori geometry, or need it not? Does everything hold only "on the basis of experience"? Experience confirms that we do well with the idealizations of geometry; but what can experience confirm, and how does confirmation come to support judgments about the future? This leads to the problem of empirical knowledge as induction, knowledge directed to the future and the non-present generally. The application of geometry to nature is here a problem, and it will become the theme of the Crisis (§9), where Galileo's mathematization is traced to an idealization of the lifeworld. The sheet of 1910 already sees that the application cannot be explained by experience without circularity, since confirmation by experience presupposes the inductive inference it was meant to justify.

This contains a further point (51.24–33). On one side, the uniformity of nature is grounded in the possibility of empirical knowledge: if empirical knowledge of this kind is possible, one can say that the course of nature is uniform and that nature is a lawfully built unity. On the other side, a "lawfulness" of consciousness belongs to the possible givenness of nature: the lawfulness of nature prescribes a certain rule to the possible consciousness of it. "How are these wonderful relations to be understood? This must be formulated specially." The two sides form the axis of the idealist thesis. Nature's lawfulness and consciousness's lawfulness are correlative: if nature is to be given, consciousness must run according to rules, and if consciousness runs according to rules, nature is given. Ideen I §47 formulates the dependence in the direction from consciousness to world ("the world is a correlate of experiences bound by certain essential configurations"), and §§150–152 in the direction from the region thing to the rule for consciousness. Text No. 3 will try to turn the "wonderful relation" into a proof by varying consciousness and world against each other.

The sixth problem is the possibility of logical and purely arithmetical knowledge (51.34–52.3). All knowing is supposed to be bound to logical laws. Logic claims to state, in pure thought, conditions of the possibility of all objectivity; pure grammar, conditions of the possibility of meaningful, if false, thought. How can pure thought do this; how can it prescribe rules to all being, even being that is only thought, unrealizable for us, lying beyond all human experience? A note: many worlds are thinkable, and for each one could say, what does it care about the course of thought of a thinker within it? The note states the realist intuition that the volume sets out to refute. Logic claims universal validity over possible worlds, yet each world seems indifferent to thought. The idealist thesis will answer that a world indifferent to every possible consciousness is no world at all, which is the special thesis extended into a theory of possible worlds in Text No. 11.

The seventh problem is psychological subjectivity and transcendent objectivity (52.4–8). Does not all science dissolve into psychology? One must take account of axiology and practice, of the teleology of consciousness as adaptation to nature, and further of how reason can become practical and how practice in nature, objective practice, is possible. The appendix ends with the danger of psychologism, which threatens as soon as objectivity is made relative to consciousness. The whole volume is in a sense the answer: the consciousness to which the world is relative is not the psychological consciousness that science studies, and so the idealist thesis does not dissolve science into psychology. The list's reference to a "teleology of consciousness" anticipates Ideen I §58, where the factual rationality of the course of consciousness raises the question of a theological ground and is suspended.

## No. 3. Considerations of Variation: Exhibition of the Functional Dependence of the Correlates "Consciousness" and "World". Proof of Phenomenological Idealism on the Basis of the Distinction between Real and Merely Logical Possibilities

This is the "Bα" to which Texts No. 1 and No. 2 defer, a reworking of the closing pages of Text No. 2 from 1908 or shortly after. It opens with the question in its starkest form: can the being of things, of a nature that is what it is whether anyone perceives or thinks it, be thought if there is no consciousness at all? "I say: No! and give the proof" (53.8–11). A later note qualifies the claim: "A complete proof is not given, but the difficulties of the relation between being and consciousness rise up from the deepest depth." The question concedes the realist's description of nature, its independence from any perceiver, and denies only the realist's inference from that description, that nature could exist without consciousness of any kind. The distinction between independence from every particular consciousness and independence from consciousness as such governs the whole text. Ideen I §49 states the same distinction when it denies that the world is relative to any individual mind while affirming that it is relative to consciousness.

The argument starts from the fact of experience (53.12–24). Suppose things and natural processes are there for an actual consciousness: it has experiences "of them" and carries out, or can carry out, the experiential groundings that lead from what is directly experienced to the positing of such a nature. For that consciousness this means possessing a certain stock of determinate factual cogitationes. Were the stock different, with other perceptions and memories, other things would be posited, and the positing of these things, which was not only possible but demanded and rightfully groundable, would now be rightfully excluded. Husserl begins from the side of consciousness, not of the world. The rightness of a world-positing is a function of the factual content of the experiencing consciousness: what is legitimately posited depends on what is actually given. Ideen I §47 begins in the same way, from "the actual world" as correlate of "our factual experience."

If a factual consciousness is varied at will within the bounds of merely logical possibility, we move in a sphere of unlimited possibilities (53.25–54.7). Objectively speaking, infinitely many other worlds are as possible as this one, each incompatible with the others. Correlatively, infinitely many other consciousnesses are logically possible, different not as my neighbour's consciousness differs from mine, apart from its empirical anchoring, but in kind: one rightly posits a world on the basis of its perceptions, another posits a wholly different world. The variation is eidetic in method though Husserl does not yet call it so. It treats both consciousness and world as variables, and it distinguishes intra-world difference among subjects (mine and my neighbour's, which share a world) from inter-world difference (subjects whose experience motivates incompatible worlds). Ideen I §47 compresses this into the phrase that the actual world is "a special case of manifold possible worlds and non-worlds," correlates of possible variants of experiencing consciousness.

"What matters to me here is that the variation of consciousness, logically possible variation, means a variation of the world that is the rightful, true, actual world for this consciousness" (54.8–11). This is the first half of the functional dependence named in the title. To vary consciousness is to vary its legitimately posited world; the world is a dependent variable of the experiential stock.

Now consider a reality absolute in the sense that it is what it is whether consciousness exists or not: either related to no consciousness, because there is none, or related to one only accidentally (54.12–23). Should one also consider a reality such that no consciousness could in principle be related to it? This case must be excluded as nonsense. If something is, it must in principle be possible to state truly that it is and to ground the truth of the statement; that it is not must be refutable for reasons. The realist's thesis is divided into a weak form (reality whose relation to consciousness is accidental) and a strong form (reality in principle unrelatable to consciousness). The strong form falls at once to the principle of legitimation from Text No. 1: being implies the possibility of grounded truth. The weak form, which the realist actually holds, is the target of the rest of the text.

Grounding of truth takes place in cogitationes, in consciousness (54.24–33). So a consciousness must be possible that "stands in relation" to any being whatever, has "experience" of it or infers it from other experience. For every kind of objectivity there must be possible acts adapted to it, which present it, intuit it, and bring it to ultimately legitimating givenness. "So being and possible consciousness that has grounds of right, as knowing consciousness, for positing such being are certainly correlatives." The conclusion is deliberately modest. The correlation of being with possible consciousness is the general thesis, which the realist can accept. The special thesis, that actual consciousness is required, must be won by a further argument.

The realist hypothesis is now set up in full (54.34–55.5). Take any nature, a world of facticity, however constituted, and add to it an actual consciousness that rightly knows it, whose necessary possibility has been shown. Assume that this nature is in itself wholly unrelated to consciousness, so that it could exist without any consciousness, and that besides the knowing consciousness now added it is the only reality. (Any number of knowing consciousnesses could be assumed instead.) The hypothesis is constructed fairly: an independent nature and an actual consciousness that correctly knows it, with no further assumption. If a contradiction can be drawn from it, the realist's position fails on its own terms.

Now try to vary the consciousness (55.6–24). We notice that "wonderfully, we are restricted in the variation." Logically, countless variations are possible, including ones in which consciousness has judgments and experiences of a kind expressed by saying "consciousness posits a world, perceives it, and judges about it in evidently grounded fashion," while that world does not exist, because the world known is essentially different from the supposed total world. But if the grounding is rightful, the world posited and grounded as existing is. So the fact of a world W would prescribe a priori to the consciousness B connected with it a rule, according to which only such connections of consciousness are possible in B as allow the legitimate grounding of W alone. How is this a priori regulation to be understood if W, by hypothesis, has nothing to do with consciousness and could exist without any? The argument turns on a conflict between two theses the realist holds together: that W is independent of consciousness, and that B knows W rightly. Rightful knowledge excludes those variations of B in which B would legitimately posit another world. So W constrains B a priori. But an entity wholly independent of consciousness cannot prescribe a priori rules to consciousness; only an essential relation could do that. The "wonder" is the symptom of a hidden essential relation.

"We have here a wonderful situation" (55.25–31). The content of a factual consciousness prescribes: precisely this world is, and whatever else may be, this world certainly belongs to the totality of being. The content of a factual world prescribes: if a factual consciousness exists, it cannot be any logically possible one; its factual content must be such that it does not negate the being of this factual world. The two prescriptions are the two directions of the functional dependence. Ideen I §47 and §§150–152 keep both: consciousness prescribes the world it can legitimately posit, and the world, as region, prescribes the rule of the consciousness that can give it.

More precisely, the content of every conceivable total reality prescribes that if there is a knowing consciousness related to it, its content must be such that it can rightly know only this world (55.32–56.6). "So in one respect there is certainly no being independent of consciousness." Every being depends on consciousness in that only such consciousness is compatible with it as, if genuinely grounding at all, grounds only this being. Were consciousness something wholly separate or separable from being, this relation would be impossible. "What is completely separate, merely contingently together, is independently variable." So consciousness and being must somehow be connected. The principle that the contingently conjoined is independently variable is the logical core of the variation argument. It is borrowed from the theory of wholes and parts in the third Logical Investigation, where independent contents are defined by their free variability relative to one another. Applied here, it shows that world and knowing consciousness are not independent contents: they are founded on each other.

How is the connection between them to be understood, of such a kind that this functional dependence becomes intelligible (56.7–20)? The consciousness-independent world is supposed to be in itself. How does a consciousness reach this world, or a determinate thing in it? Consciousness perceives, has consciousness of givenness; but what is really contained in consciousness of givenness belongs to consciousness itself, not a transcendent thing. Perception and memory arise and pass in the flow, while the given thing is said to be in itself and to endure before and after. Is perception something that occasionally settles on things and draws them into itself, then leaves them again? Husserl now shows that the realist cannot explain the dependence he has just been forced to admit. The image of perception settling on things and drawing them in is a parody of naive realism, but it isolates the realist's real commitment: that perception reaches the thing by including it.

That will not do (56.21–33). Then perception would be immanent perception, and the immanent is itself something of consciousness, as sensed colour is. A parenthesis adds that the division between "sensory," "physical" contents and consciousness in the specific sense falls within the unity of consciousness: can a colour content exist as a thing in itself? One need only think of time-constitution, primary memory and the adumbration of memory, to see the perversity. And even if perception grasped the foreign thing itself, how could we know it? A perception can be a hallucination. The parenthesis blocks a refuge for realism: one might say that sensations at least are physical contents independent of consciousness. Husserl answers that sensation contents are constituted in time-consciousness through retentional modification ("adumbration of memory"), so they are not candidates for an in-itself either. Ideen I §§41 and 85 separate the hyletic contents from the perceived thing; this note shows why the hyle cannot be made into the thing.

If perception of the transcendent is not a grasping of it in the proper sense, how can something immanent perceive or present something non-immanent and bring it to consciousness of self-presence (56.34–57.5)? How does consciousness reach precisely this thing and no other, and how is the thing's presenting itself in consciousness, as to its being and change, thinkable? The positing of the given world depends on what is given to consciousness, and without such givens no positing of a determinate world can be thought. The question "why this thing and no other" is the specificity problem that any causal or representational theory must answer, and Husserl's reply, the last sentence, reverses the direction of explanation: the determinacy of the world is drawn from the determinacy of what is given.

To think a thing is to think something enduring, changing or unchanged, with properties that persist or alter (57.6–24); what holds for a thing holds for a world of things. To such thinking corresponds a priori and evidently a possible intuitive presentation. And is that thinkable without the a priori possibility of reflection, and do not certain complex forms of connection of sensations, adumbrations, demonstrable in reflection, belong to it essentially? How do these, which belong to consciousness, come to refer to something in itself? If they exist and the further connections hold, the thing is beyond doubt. But how does consciousness, with just these connections, reach the thing itself, which is supposed to be transcendent and to remain so? How can it conform to something that has nothing to do with consciousness? The chain of a priori implications is the backbone of the argument: thinkable thing implies possible intuition, possible intuition implies possible reflection, reflection exhibits adumbrational connections. The existence of a thing thus implies a determinate structure in possible consciousness. The question is then why, if these connections suffice for the indubitability of the thing, anything else should be needed.

"For the thing to be, a certain lawfulness of consciousness in its factual stock is required" (57.25–38). Suppose consciousness given with this lawfulness and this stock. If something more should exist, the thing itself to which consciousness relates and which is not exhausted by the actual and logically prescribed connections, it would have to be thinkable that this in-itself were struck out while all those connections remained. "And that is absolutely excluded," because the only and evident right for the statement "a thing is" lies in these connections, and for the statement "a thing is really possible" in the possibility of such connections, insofar as it relates to actual connections that themselves posit actual things. This is the thought experiment that Ideen I §49 runs in reverse. The realist must allow the in-itself to be struck out with consciousness unchanged; Husserl answers that striking it out changes nothing, since every right to assert it lies in the connections that remain. A difference that makes no difference to any possible legitimation is no difference in being. Ideen I §49's world-annihilation draws the complementary lesson: strike out the world by altering the connections, and consciousness remains.

One will say: I must acknowledge other consciousnesses besides mine, and they relate to one and the same thing (58.1–13). Certainly. But they can do so only because they are related to one another through possible and actual empathy and so make up a manifold consciousness bound together by the possibility of empathy. Taking this together in its own unity of consciousness, I can again strike out all thinghood supposed to be in itself, if it made sense to posit thinghood "in itself" rather than as an intentional unity of consciousness whose being is exhausted in this intentional being, constituted in a determinate regulation of consciousness. The intersubjective objection is absorbed rather than refuted: the many consciousnesses form a higher unity through empathy, and the argument is repeated at that level. Ideen I §48 makes exactly this move when it argues that a thing given to other egos but not to us must still lie within the empathic connection that unites us with them, since the world known by others is, through empathy, a possible world for us.

One might also think that things in themselves stand in relation to a consciousness by acting on it, producing perceptions (58.14–59.3). This does not solve the riddle. Even admitting talk of things in themselves acting on consciousness, as if consciousness were a thing that could depend causally on a second thing, something produced is not yet a perception. The riddle is how something in the connection of consciousness, with its regulations, can have the sense of what we call perception, experience, empirical knowledge of things, and refer to something that has nothing to do with consciousness. For the factual world and our factual consciousness one might think of a miracle or a pre-established harmony. But one would overlook that we speak of the things we experience and see, that every rational and scientific answer to the question how these things really are has no special riddles, and that we have no reason to operate "on the credit of an empty hypothesis," since it is evident that things are rightly posited as actual and rightly determined thus and not otherwise. Two objections are combined. First, causality is a relation between realities, and treating consciousness as an effect makes it a thing, the category error Ideen I §§53–54 diagnose. Second, causation cannot yield intentionality: an effect is not by being an effect an experience of its cause. The appeal to "the things we see" closes the argument with the principle that the evidence of experience needs no metaphysical underwriting. Ideen I §52 rejects the hypothesis of a hidden physical cause of appearances in the same terms.

What deserves particular attention is that, independently of the facticity of our world and our knowledge, essential connections hold according to which determinate being of any kind is unthinkable without the possibility of a quite determinate constituted consciousness, and this consciousness must contain in its own acts the determinacy of its direction to being (59.4–19). If so, no other relation of being to consciousness is thinkable than that nothing further is to be assumed beyond actual consciousness and its modifications and actual sequences of states. The being of the non-conscious is intelligible only as being meant and confirmed, grounded in connections of consciousness. "Transcendence in general is not denied," but every transcendent is either a unity of consciousness or posited on the basis of consciousness, and posited with ground. The passage lifts the argument from the factual to the eidetic level: the correlation holds for every possible world and every possible consciousness. The explicit clause that transcendence is not denied is the guard against the solipsistic or Berkeleyan reading, and it states the position of Ideen I §§42–49: the world transcends consciousness as its intentional correlate, not as a reality beyond it.

Husserl notes a need for a more precise concept of "consciousness" for such discussions, and refers to parallel treatments in his ethics lectures of 1908/09 (59.20–60.4; the editor identifies them as Hua XXVIII, 237–345). He then restates the countersense argument of Text No. 2: it belongs to the sense of the valid thing-positing "D is" that what is given in perception and experience, including scientifically determining thought, exists, that it truly has the determinations rightly ascribed to it in experiential determination, "and that nothing else is." The thing has no determination that is not or cannot be ascribed to it in experiential grounding, and the determinations of a thing of experience can be legitimated only from motives of experience, that is, by way of natural-scientific determination. The admission that "consciousness" needs a sharper concept is exact: the argument requires the distinction between psychological and transcendental consciousness that Husserl has not yet fixed, and that Ideen I §§33 and 53–54 will provide. The restated countersense argument now ties the thing's determinations to natural science as the only legitimate continuation of experience, so that idealism and the authority of physics are bound together, not opposed.

### Proof of Phenomenological Idealism, Carried Out More Closely and Correctly

The proof proper begins with the sufficiency thesis from Text No. 2 (60.7–10). Suppose absolute consciousness so regulated that it contains the sources of all possible grounding; would anything be missing from any being? Evidently no being could be struck out. The heading's claim to greater correctness lies in the separation of two steps. The sufficiency step shows that absolute consciousness, so regulated, leaves nothing out; the necessity step, which follows, shows that the regulation must be anchored in actual consciousness.

Second (60.11–15): for anyone who draws knowledge from the "origin of the thing," it is understandable that a thing is nothing but a "unity of consciousness." But the thing is independent of any determinate consciousness, someone will say; it has a relation only to possible consciousness. This is the realist objection of Text No. 1 (16.33–37) in its strongest form. It grants constitution and denies the special thesis by shifting the correlate from actual to possible consciousness.

So Husserl asks: can there be being in itself in the sense that it is intuitable or groundable in possible consciousness, but such that no actual consciousness contains rational motives for assuming it (60.16–23)? Imagine the whole of absolute consciousness explored, every motive followed in ideal perfection, and nothing pointing to the being of that thing. Could the thing then actually be? The thought experiment isolates the case on which everything depends: a being that is possible to experience but motivated by nothing in any actual experience.

The answer (60.24–61.15): it is conceded that if a thing is, the corresponding possibilities of experience and grounding must hold. But what does "possibilities must hold" mean? In a certain sense possibilities of experience and even far-reaching possibilities of grounding hold for the existence of a diamond as large as the sun. Such existence is possible in the logical sense (a note: not analytic possibility). It does not exist; "all actual experience speaks against it." But only facticity conflicts with its existence; logically it is possible. If mere logical possibility sufficed, everything possible, because imaginable and thinkable, would also exist. So if a totality of possibilities of experience and grounding is correlated with the actuality of a thing, these cannot be merely logical but must be real possibilities. That can only mean that they are motivated possibilities, which have their motivation somewhere in the whole of absolute consciousness, the actual one, that is, in some empirical consciousness among the many in communicative association or in some single "real" consciousness. This means again that there must be some place in the experience of consciousness from which it can be compelled logically, by paths of justified grounding that it can traverse, to assume the thing; the thing belongs to what can be posited and grounded from possible grounds of experience of actual consciousness. The argument is a reductio of the realist's appeal to possible consciousness. Possibility in the realist's sense is logical possibility, and logical possibility cannot distinguish the actual thing from the sun-sized diamond, so it cannot be what the thing's actuality consists in. Only real possibility can, and real possibility is defined by motivation from actual experience. The note excluding analytic possibility fixes the level: the sun-sized diamond is materially consistent, compatible with the essence of nature, and excluded only by fact. This is the proof that Ideen I §47 states in one sentence: what is not "motivated" by an actual experience in a nexus of possible experience is not a real possibility. The text also widens the anchor from "my" consciousness to any member of a communicative plurality, so the special thesis requires that some consciousness be actual, not that I be.

Here, Husserl observes, he started from the fact of consciousness, mine or anyone's, in suitable reduction (61.16–27). If one starts instead from the fact, or rather the hypothesis, of a world of things in itself without any consciousness, the question what the possibilities of perception and grounding belonging to this world are supposed to mean leads again to the distinction between empty and real possibilities, and to the insight that real possibility could then have no sense at all, unless the sense of a mysterious incompatibility of a newly created consciousness, if it were to posit another world in itself, with the given world in itself. The two starting points converge. From consciousness, the argument shows that real possibility needs actual experience; from the hypothesis of a world without consciousness, it shows that real possibility would have no ground at all, and the world's possibilities of experience would reduce to logical possibilities, which are equally available to every rival world. The final parenthesis names the only alternative: that a world in itself would, by some unexplained necessity, forbid any newly created consciousness from positing another world. That is the "wonderful" a priori constraint of the variation argument again, and the realist has no account of it. The phrase "in suitable reduction" is one of the earliest uses of the reductive vocabulary for the starting point of the argument; the fact of consciousness is taken not as a psychological fact in the world but as the reduced field in which the world's legitimacy is examined.

## No. 4. Esse and Percipi. Unity and Manifold. Immanent Being and Transcendent Being. Immanent Being and the "Flow of Absolute Consciousness". The Natural Object and the Manifolds. Immanence in the Narrower and the Wider Sense

The text, probably from 1908, begins with the immanent object as the model of constituted unity (62.9–15). "The" tone is not the flow, and the flow is not the tone; but the tone is the unity seen out of, or taken from, the flow and given in it (the flow itself not being objectivated), and it is what it is only as this unity. If the flow is not, neither is the tone. If it is, the tone is not something existing and evident alongside it but something taken or to be taken from it, constituted in it. The analysis comes from the time-consciousness researches of 1905–1909 (Hua X): the enduring tone is an immanent temporal object constituted in the non-objectivated flow. Husserl uses it as the template for the relation between the natural thing and experience, and the text's whole argument is that the thing stands to experience as the tone stands to the flow, with one decisive difference.

One may also say that the flow precedes the tone in itself; it is the earlier in itself (62.16–29). It can run off in its own way without being noticed or seen in reflection. The tone still is then, as a unity that can be seen out of the flow and posited in the thought "this here" as a being of this immanent category. What "being" means here, and what "it is a unity" means, can only be seen, and seen to be nothing except as such a unity rooted in and springing from consciousness. As for the natural object, it is a unity of a manifold; "unity of a manifold" is a vague but unavoidable expression, applicable to everything transcendently seen or posited. The priority of the flow is an order of founding, not of time: the flow is "earlier in itself," as Aristotle's prior by nature, because the tone is what it is only in it. The phrase "unity of a manifold," flagged as vague, is the formula Husserl will refine through the noetic-noematic analyses of Ideen I §§97–98 and the concept of synthesis in §118.

There may be various levels and kinds of transcendent things, above all levels relative to the reality of "nature" in the sense of exact science, each with its relative truth bounded by its point of view (62.30–63.6): the pure sense-thing, related to the single subject before any contrast with a "you"; the sense-thing of the normally sensing human being in general, which plays its part in descriptive natural science; the intersubjective sense-thing in a wider sense; and the object of exact natural science. The stratification is the first appearance in this volume of the levels of thing-constitution that Ideen I §151 sketches (solipsistic thing, intersubjective thing, physical thing) and Ideen II §18 works out. Each level has "relative truth": the sense-thing is not refuted by physics but superseded within a definite point of view.

These are ever higher formations of unity, always unities of manifolds, but such that what stands as manifold at a higher level may itself be a unity relative to lower manifolds (63.7–18). The physical thing relates to the intersubjective sense-thing, and the unity of the physical thing runs through manifold changes of the sense-thing. Better: the intersubjective sense-thing is a unity over against the sense-thing changes and manifolds of different humans and animals, normally and abnormally sensing, and those corresponding to "accidental" physiological changes. The iteration of the unity-manifold relation is what makes physics compatible with constitution. The physical thing is not a hidden cause behind sense-things but a higher unity whose manifold consists of sense-things across subjects and conditions. Ideen I §52 relies on precisely this: physical determinations are determinations of the thing that appears, not of a second thing.

Following the constitution of the natural object or an intermediate transcendent object, we see that it belongs to the essence of these objects to be unities (63.19–30). Analysis leads back, here as throughout phenomenology, to immanent unities: sensation contents, phansiological appearances, higher-level apprehensions based on them, perhaps conceptual grasps built on these. And "unity" leads back to synthetic connections, at bottom to continuous connections of perceptions, presentative appearances in the phansiological sense. "Phansiological," a term of Husserl's 1906–1909 lectures, designates the side of the act's appearing, what Ideen I will call the noetic. The analysis descends from object to apprehension to sensation and ends in continuous perceptual synthesis, the order of Thing and Space.

So it seems analogous to the immanent unities (63.31–38). But these are unities in manifolds related to them so that the being of the unity is "simultaneous" with the being of the manifold, really one with it and in it in a certain sense. The manifold has being; one can reflect on it and look at it; and it is one with the immanent object even without being looked at. The analogy breaks at the status of the manifold. In the immanent case the manifold actually exists, together with the unity, whether or not it is attended to.

By contrast, to posit the transcendent unity is to posit the possibility of the manifold, and no more (64.1–18). The manifold whose unity the object is is not one with the object, and "the object is actual" does not mean "the manifold is actual." To posit a thing as actual, in a spatiotemporal nexus of things, means: courses of manifolds are possible, possible actualities within the nexus of being such objects allow and require; it is possible that syntheses can be produced or manifolds run off in which the unity that confirms the positing can be given. But "it is possible" does not mean empty logical possibility; it means motivated possibility. The difficulties here are soluble difficulties of the phenomenology of things and the theory of experience. The difference between the two cases is now exact. For the immanent object, existence is the actual existence of its manifold; for the thing, existence is the motivated possibility of its manifold. This is the precise sense of Ideen I §§42–45: the thing is not perceived in order to be, yet its being is its perceivability within a motivated nexus.

Granting this, one will say: things certainly are, and are not the manifolds, nor ultimately the actual or possible connections of consciousness; but the statement "the thing is" has no sense if one denies actual consciousness and the possible consciousness attached to it, the motivations (64.19–28). One might then think: very well, there is a law that the being of things is unthinkable without the being of consciousness and, conversely, that if consciousness of such and such a form is actual, "not in nature!", the thing is actual too. The parenthetical "not in nature!" marks the consciousness at issue as non-natural, the transcendental consciousness that Ideen I §§33 and 49 separate from the psychological. Husserl has now stated the equivalence in law-like form, and he will immediately test it against cases where it fails.

Consider another case (64.29–65.3). I expect to hear a tone, and the hearing occurs; I hear the tone, it is. Here there is first consciousness, the intention of expectation, and to it corresponds motivated possible future consciousness, perceptual consciousness. But the tone itself enters as something new beyond actual and possible consciousness. Its actual being is not exhausted in the consciousness that merely aims at it; it is given as what it is in immanent perception, which exhausts its being, so that it is not something added to the perception but something existing in it. Immanent perception alone is the giving act. The case is a test of the law just formulated. For immanent objects, being is not a unity of motivated possibilities; it is given in the act and exhausted there. So the law that equates being with motivated possibility holds for transcendent objects only, and the difference between the two kinds of being is confirmed.

Or take another's experiences (65.4–14). I think them, or posit them intuitively in empathy; but all thinking and empathy merely aim at the other's experience and do not give it. Only the experiencer's own self-grasping gives it. Here it is impossible to bring the meaning to fulfilment in actual givenness, though empathy and determination of the other's experiences can be quite justified; but the grounding does not require, and leaves no possibility of, a return to the self-giving acts. The other's experiences form a third case: immanent in themselves, but for me accessible only through an intending that can never be fulfilled by original givenness. The case shows that justification is not always fulfilment and anticipates the later concept of "appresentation" (Cartesian Meditations §50). For Ideen I the point matters because the empathic relation between consciousnesses, which the special thesis needs (Text No. 3, 58), rests on a justification that is never intuitive fulfilment.

What of the thing of external nature (65.15–38)? Here too there are meaning acts that do not give, and their meanings can be justified without direct reference to giving acts that bring the object itself, as meant, to self-givenness. Yet it belongs to the essence of every thing-positing that a direct grounding leading back to the givenness of the thing itself is possible in principle, even if impossible in fact. Indeed, every real grounding of being is a piece of givenness. If I posit something because someone I trust claims to have seen it, I posit it on grounds, but that is no real grounding. When, starting from what is given or already grounded and following the nexus of being theoretically, I posit with logical correctness a being not itself given immediately, I again have givenness. It belongs to the thing's essence both to be perceivable and to stand in connections with other things such that it can be determined logically by means of other perceptions; and this logical determination is itself a piece of the extended consciousness of givenness. The distinction between the other's experience and the thing is that the thing is in principle accessible to originary givenness, which is the difference between the impossible and the merely factually impossible fulfilment. The extension of "givenness" to logically grounded determination is a significant widening: scientific inference, unlike testimony, is part of the thing's self-givenness. Ideen I §§136–141 retain the distinction between originary and mediate rational positing but keep the link: every mediate positing must be traceable to originary givenness.

This needs a better and deeper treatment (66.1–17). One must not overlook the deceptive circumstance that simple perceptual givenness is not yet thing-givenness in the full sense, if by givenness we mean the consciousness in which the thing is given as itself there in such a way that it can be determined as what it is (compare the case of immanence). It belongs, for example, to the essence of the unchanged thing that its interior cannot be perceived, and positing its inner filling of space points to possible changes of the thing and so to possible perceptual connections beyond those that give its exterior. And these connections of change are, according to the sense of thinghood, causal connections, leading back to laws that must finally be grasped as exact laws of nature. Thing-givenness is not exhausted by the visual aspect, and the perception of a single side already implies the causal nexus. A solid's interior is accessible only by breaking or cutting it, that is, by changing it, so the full sense of a thing includes its causal behaviour. This is the reason Ideen II §§15–18 define the material thing by its causal properties and the reason Ideen I §52 can claim that physical determination is a further explication of the perceived thing's own sense.

It is a great insight, appearing in Kant under all sorts of disguises or struggling to appear, that not everything we call perception, experience, and empirical knowledge of nature is a merely indirect consciousness of things (66.18–32). The naive view takes perception as immediate grasping of the thing. Reflection leads to saying that in perception, a subjective experience, I have not the thing but only a consciousness of it, and that different experiences look different, as different pictures differ from the thing itself. So we must separate perception from the thing and acknowledge that perception is no actual consciousness of givenness but only an indirect assuming or presenting, perhaps thinking, of the thing. Husserl credits Kant with the insight that empirical knowledge is not knowledge of representations standing for things, the point of the Refutation of Idealism and of the empirical realism that accompanies transcendental idealism. He reports the reflective view that he is about to reject: from the difference between perception and thing it infers that perception is indirect.

"That is fundamentally false" (66.33–67.2). It leads at once to the thought that it is a contingent fact, a limit of our contingent knowledge, that we humans cannot see the thing as it is in itself, and that every intelligence like ours is in the same position, with only the divine intelligence having a real intuition of the thing as it is in itself; God, as intellectus archetypus, sees things as they are because in intuiting them he creates them. The phrase intellectus archetypus comes from Kant's Critique of Judgment §77, where the archetypal intellect intuits what it produces. The reflective view, Husserl argues, makes the inadequacy of perception a contingent human limitation and thereby invites a divine intuition that would see the thing without appearances.

Against this the only true doctrine is maintained here: there can be no other kind of givenness of the thing than "ours" (67.3–25). Our givenness is incomplete, and other givennesses, below and above us humans, are conceivable; but they are all givennesses in the same way, they do not differ in principle and rather join together. It belongs to the essence of the thing to have such and such givennesses, and all that are actual and possible are needed for its full givenness. All perceptions and experiences of this table, mine or any other being's, belong to the table and must be capable of being brought into a unity in which the thing presents itself continuously and from every side. The idea of complete givenness can lack none of these presentations; it is a mere idea, since the manifold is infinite, but not a lawless infinity: it is wholly definite what belongs to it. Everything that belongs to it gives the thing "in this or that respect." It is the essence of the thing to be givable only so, relatively, through appearance and presentation. Two claims are combined: givenness by adumbration is the only possible givenness of a thing, for any intellect; and the infinite totality of adumbrations is a determinate idea. Ideen I §§43–44 make the first claim, explicitly including God; §143 makes the second, calling the complete givenness of a thing an "idea in the Kantian sense," a rule for infinite progress with a prescribed type.

So no absolute consciousness of the thing, in which the thing and finally nature would come to "actual intuition" and be immanent as the tone is in immanent perception, can be set against human experience and science (67.26–36). That is the fundamental prejudice whose refutation removes all mysticism and radically cancels the "thing in itself" in the sense of a false metaphysics. There is only one thing in itself: over against the thing taken according to mere sense-appearance, the thing as it is in itself, that is, in truth, in the truth that science establishes in logical grounding. The "fundamental prejudice" is the model of an intuition that would make the thing immanent. Removing it removes the Kantian thing in itself as an unknowable correlate of a divine intellect and leaves a legitimate in-itself, the thing as science determines it. This is the formula of Ideen I §52, where the physical thing is "the thing itself" as determined by physics, not a second thing behind the appearance.

So there is being that can be given absolutely, so that in its entire selfhood it becomes immanent, or so that from the giving consciousness, grasped simply and closed in itself, the object can be determined absolutely perfectly and completely as an individual reality: immanent perception (67.37–68.14). One may perhaps count among immanent givens in a wider sense intuitions of unity such as that of the oculomotor thing, which contain no infinities and can come to adequate givenness in finite continua; a note says these are transcendent objects that can be adequately given in a closed act. The natural object does not belong here. It is its essence to have infinitely many sides, determinations, and modes, and its givenness is itself infinite, coming about in infinitely many acts, each giving only one side, unfolding and determining the infinity step by step. The note on the oculomotor thing is a precise qualification taken from Thing and Space: a visual object constituted only in the oculomotor field, without bodily movement, can be given in a closed finite series, and is thus a transcendent object with adequate givenness. So adequacy is not strictly coextensive with immanence. What defines the natural object is the infinity of its manifold.

It remains true that the thing is not the connection of actual and possible consciousness that functions as giving with regard to it, but only given in it (68.15–18). This one-sentence paragraph guards the previous results against identification. The thing is not its manifold; it is given in the manifold. The distinction between being given in and being is the distinction Ideen I §§41 and 97 draw between the reell content of experience and its intentional object.

Once the fiction of an adequately givable in-itself, of a "truly giving" act in contrast to the "seemingly" giving ones, has been removed, and it has been shown that to demand a priori another mode of givenness for a natural thing than the one unfolding in the infinity of experience is a countersense, this infinity of experience and empirical determination stands to the thing exactly as immanent perception stands to its immanent object (68.19–69.36). The thing is what it is only as experienced or experienceable unity, a unity for partly actual, partly possible experience, synthetically connected or connectable. It is not reduced to an empty manifold of empty possibilities: the statement "the thing is" presupposes actual experience and grounded possibilities of experience that aim at it, mediately or immediately, and does so in principle. It does not matter whether I or anyone else makes the statement; if it holds, "someone," an experiencing consciousness, must actually exist, from which experience can actually reach the thing. But the thing is nothing outside these experiences and motivated, valid possibilities, as if a further givenness were thinkable outside the manifold that would contain it. Rather "experience itself contains the thing and nothing more," and contains it only in the way here described: the thing is the unity to be taken from experience and its motivated progress, to be seen out of it and determined on its basis; it is "immanent" to experience in an extended sense. Immanence in the narrower sense is unity taken from a closed manifold of experiences or a self-constituting experience, fully and adequately. As the immanent object is nothing beside and outside consciousness, though not a piece of it, so the natural thing and all nature are immanent objects in the wider sense: nothing beside and outside the actual and possible experience through which they come to givenness in infinite unfolding, not inferred from it but taken from it, not depicted in it with the image outside, not merely thought in it with the thought outside, but seen in it, itself and actually, always seen and always pointing beyond the seeing, yet not beyond experience but into its progress, which brings ever new sides of the thing's infinity to givenness. "Natural being is thus wholly sheltered in consciousness, in a sense quite similar to that in which the being of the tone is sheltered in the consciousness of the tone." This is the most complete statement of the transcendental-idealist doctrine in the 1908 texts, and the editor's introduction quotes it as such. Its structure is a proportion: as tone to flow, so thing to infinite experience. The proportion is exact once adequacy is no longer the measure of givenness. Transcendence survives as the thing's always pointing beyond what is seen, but this pointing is internal to experience, "into" its progress, not "beyond" it. The text also contains the special thesis: someone must actually exist. Ideen I §49's formulation of the world as an intentional unity "that consciousness posits in its experiences" and that is "nothing beyond this" is the published version, minus the provocative word "immanent," which Ideen I reserves for the reell, so that it can say of the same thing that it is "transcendent" (§42).

This is the truth we owe to the progressive research of philosophers of very different schools, above all Locke, Berkeley, Kant, and the positivists from Mill to Mach, and which none of them reached (69.37–70.21). The view presented here must not be identified with the positivist one, which does not do justice to the essence of intentional being. Our view is that consciousness, being in the radical sense, is in the radical, genuine sense; it is the root and, in another image, the source of everything else that is or can be called being. It bears every other individual being, immanent or transcendent. If being is individual, enduring, temporal being, consciousness is not a being; it is the bearer of time but not itself temporal, though by "subjectivation," a certain kind of objectivation, it is placed in time and "formed" into something enduring, a temporal object. In itself it is non-temporal, no unity of a manifold, and points back to nothing from which it could be taken as unity; all other being is unitary and points back, mediately or immediately, to the absolute flow. The lineage names the tradition of "ideas" and "experience" that Husserl takes himself to complete. The exclusion of positivism is precise: Mill's permanent possibilities of sensation and Mach's complexes of elements reduce the thing to sensations and so miss intentionality, the unity that is not a sum of its manifold. The claim that absolute consciousness is not temporal, but the bearer of time, is the thesis of Hua X §36, that the time-constituting flow is not itself in time; "subjectivation" is the apperception by which a stream becomes the psychic life of a person, the operation Ideen I §§53–54 call psychological apperception.

If the flow of consciousness is, in its way, everything else that is and can be is (70.22–28). It needs nothing further. And if anything is that we call "existing" but not "consciousness," then consciousness is, and such consciousness as needs nothing further in order to say "it is what exists"; and this "it is" means: it is in the flow, to be taken out of it as a unity sheltered and rooted in it. The phrase "needs nothing further" anticipates Ideen I §49's adaptation of Descartes's definition of substance: consciousness "nulla re indiget ad existendum." The rest of the sentence, the converse, states that every positing of a non-conscious being already implies the existence of consciousness.

The situation justifies calling consciousness, which by its nature "precedes" and gives roots, absolute being, in contrast to relative being, which is being only in relation to consciousness and its essential lawfulness (70.29–32). The terminology of absolute and relative being is introduced here as a consequence, not a premise. Ideen I §§49–50 and 76 use it in exactly this sense.

On the other hand it would be inappropriate to say that there is only absolute consciousness, as if all other being were mere appearance, unreal semblance, a figment (70.33–71.10). That would be fundamentally false. Natural objects are of course true objects, their being true being, nature reality in the genuine and full sense. It is fundamentally false to apply to this being any other standard than the one its category requires, or to discredit it because it is constituted in consciousness, rooted in it. Consciousness is such that nature can be drawn from it, and rightly drawn in scientific truth. And to say that natural science does not deal with nature, that its true objects are sensations and that things and atoms are mere symbols, thought-economical abbreviations for sensations, "is the height of perversity." The guard against phenomenalism is placed directly after the strongest statement of the absoluteness of consciousness. The phrase "thought-economical abbreviations" names Mach and Avenarius. The principle that each category of being must be measured by its own standard is the regional principle of evidence again (Ideen I §§138, 142), and it is the reason Ideen I §55 can call its position idealism while denying that it diminishes the reality of the world.

The last clarification that the doctrine of reason gives us is to be taught: that everything that is and can be called existing, the object of true existential and categorical statements, is consciousness or has its root in consciousness, to be taken from it, seen and determined out of its connections, and that this relation is not factual and accidental but a priori in the strictest sense, grounded in the essence of being and consciousness (71.11–26). The last thing we are led to is this seeing out, this taking out as being sheltered as unity in consciousness, expressions behind which lies a state of affairs to be grasped and made evident. Consciousness proves to be the last principle, the ἀρχή that harbours and bears all other being, yet not as a piece or part or moment, nor as something fictioned into it. The term ἀρχή gives the thesis its metaphysical register: consciousness is principle in the Aristotelian sense of that from which other being is. The double negation, neither part nor fiction, repeats the two guards: against real inclusion, which would make the world a content of consciousness, and against fictionalism, which would make it a projection. Ideen I §76 calls transcendental consciousness the "Urregion," the region of origin, in the same sense.

This insight is foreign to natural consciousness, to which nothing is more remote than the essential relation of natural being and consciousness (71.27–72.3). Positing nature seems to require no positing of consciousness. In truth, positing consciousness with its whole content implicitly includes the positing of all nature and all other being. And positing nature is in a certain way positing consciousness too, but not so that consciousness could be drawn from the content of nature; rather we are led back from knowledge of nature to consciousness, and from there to the insight that, if consciousness were not, not only would knowledge be impossible but nature itself would lose all hold, its root, its ἀρχή, and would be nothing. The asymmetry is decisive. From consciousness one can read off nature; from nature one cannot read off consciousness. The route runs from the naive positing of nature through reflection to consciousness, which is the route of Ideen I's fundamental consideration from §27 to §49. And the counterfactual "if consciousness were not, nature would be nothing" is the special thesis in its most absolute form.

By contrast, positing consciousness is such that being can be drawn from its content, its essence (72.4–13). By its essence consciousness is directed at "objects" in the manifold formations that make up its stock. Looking at its essence leads necessarily to the insight that its life and being consist in meaning objects and, according to actual motivations, determining them as true natural objects, objects that are what they are only in this "embedding," "rooting," or shelter in absolute consciousness. Intentionality is the reason the relation is asymmetrical. Consciousness is essentially "of" objects, so its analysis yields its objects; objects are not essentially "of" consciousness, so their analysis yields only further objects.

It is also clear that there can be no new being behind absolute consciousness, and that everything of which we can say with sense and right "it is" must be something constituted in consciousness, or obtained by abstraction in relation to what is constituted (72.14–32). General objects and ideas, though they do not point to actual consciousness, point evidently to possible consciousness, and possibility is itself thinkable only in relation to actual consciousness. This does not mean that there can be no being besides empirical nature and the world of spirit that deserves the name of reality, or that no objectivations relating to the teleological unity of the world can be grounded in consciousness. Only this remains absolutely certain: if there is a God or any other being, it must be groundable from absolute consciousness, rooted in it, and the path to it must lead, for our knowledge, through the natural objectivations of nature, spirit, social world. Three extensions close the text. Ideal objects fall under the general thesis, related to possible consciousness, while possibility itself is anchored in actuality, a remark that would extend the special thesis to the ideal and which the volume does not pursue. The world of spirit, persons and society, appears beside nature as a second constituted domain, the plan of Ideen II. And God is admitted only as a being whose sense must be grounded in absolute consciousness and reached through the teleology of the world, the position Ideen I §§51 (note) and 58 adopt when they bracket a transcendent God while leaving open a divine ground inferred from the rationality of the factual course of consciousness.

## No. 5. On Transcendental Idealism. From the Lecture Course "Nature and Spirit", Summer Semester 1913

The text, from June 1913, comes from a lecture course given a few weeks after Ideen I appeared, and it restates the argument of Ideen I §§47–49 in a form that the book leaves compressed. It opens with Husserl's heading "the thought of the last lecture, and then critique," and a first thesis (73.5–10): "A exists" is equivalent to "a path of possible legitimation of the existence of A can be constructed" and to "the ideal and insightful possibility of such legitimation holds"; in general, the ideas "truth" and "ideal possibility of insightful legitimation" are equivalent. The equivalence is the general correlation thesis, now stated as a principle of the theory of reason. Ideen I §§142 and 144 state it in the same terms: to every truly existing object corresponds in principle the idea of a possible consciousness in which it is given originarily.

What is must be capable of being brought to givenness (73.11–17). Every possible object, in the widest sense of the word, has as its correlate a possible intuition in which it would become intuitive as it is, and in principle a possible originary giving intuition in which it would be graspable in person. Whether that intuition is a simple bounded act or a continuing process of manifold intuiting is left open. The open clause anticipates the difference between immanent and transcendent objects without yet drawing it: for the one, a closed act; for the other, an infinite process.

So object, objective being, and consciousness belong together a priori, inseparably; and if every consciousness may be assigned a conscious ego, an ego too belongs in this essential connection: a possible ego and a determinately constituted possible consciousness are correlated with every possible objective being (73.18–23). The ego, suspended in the 1908 texts, now enters the correlation. This matches Ideen I, which in §57 admits the pure ego as a "transcendence in immanence" that cannot be bracketed. The correlation is still with possible consciousness; the special thesis comes next.

A radical difference appears when objects are divided into eidetic and individual (73.24–74.5). Husserl generalizes the consideration, speaking not first of transcendent individual objects, physical things or the world, but of individual objects in general. An individual object cannot exist unless an ego, an actual consciousness, exists that is "related" to it. An eidetic object requires only the possible existence of a consciousness to be related to it: the mathematical existence of numbers and manifolds, with the ideal possibility of insightful legitimation, does not require the actual existence of a consciousness related to mathematics. By contrast, every individual object posited as existing requires at once the actual existence of a consciousness. The special thesis is now stated as the principle that divides the two great classes of objects. Its generality is new: it holds for all individuals, not only for transcendent ones, and it marks the exact point at which Husserl's idealism leaves Platonism about ideal objects untouched. Text No. 1 drew the same line between truths of essence and truths of fact.

The immanent cases are quickly settled (74.5–13). If the individual object is itself an experience, the thesis is a tautology. One could go further and say that a single experience requires a whole stream, being unthinkable as isolated, so that what is required here and everywhere is the whole stream and its continuously belonging ego. The case of an immanent sense datum is settled likewise. The remark that a single experience requires a whole stream is the holism of Ideen I §§82–83: every experience belongs to an infinite stream with its horizons and its ego.

Now take a transcendent individual being, a physical thing with its properties and states (74.14–32). We conceive intuitively, from an example, the idea of something meant as a thing, and hold it in the greatest generality: we may vary it freely in intuition, let the table serving as example change in any way, provided only that the region "thing" remains identical. What belongs to the possibility in principle of such a thing's existing, or, equivalently, of its being legitimable? It belongs to thingly being in principle that it is externally experienceable; it can be seen a priori that such a being must appear and be given in perceptual appearances, and that endless further perceptual connections belong to such appearances, in which the transcendent shows itself in ever new determinations. It is in principle not adequately perceivable. A note: what is meant is evidently the intuitive thing. Eidetic variation is now explicitly the method, and the invariant is the region "thing," the concept that Ideen I §§9–10 and 149–150 develop. The note restricting the claim to the intuitive thing reserves the physical thing for a derived treatment, which comes at 78.

The next question is what this "can" means (74.33–75.14). Does an ideally possible consciousness suffice; are ideally possible transcendent perceptions the correlate of a thing's actual existence? We now assume that a certain thing is actual, and distinguish its actuality from its merely ideal possibility. In free fiction I imagine a centaur and recognize its possibility by carrying out harmonious fictive intuitions: I approach it in phantasy, view it from all sides, touch it, test its existence in every way. I carry out mere fictions, but harmonious ones, in which the unity of the object holds. My own existence is irrelevant; the centaur's possibility does not require it. I see that its possibility depends on the possibility of such series of intuitions and so on the possibility of a stream of consciousness and ego belonging to them. The centaur replaces the sun-sized diamond of the 1908 texts. Its possibility is established by harmonious phantasy, which is the eidetic method of Ideen I §§4 and 70 applied to individual objects, and its possibility is correlated with possible, not actual, consciousness, exactly as eidetic objects are.

Now assume the thing actually exists (75.15–31). The merely ideal possibility of a thing, a centaur for instance, does not exclude infinitely many other ideal possibilities incompatible with it in existence. I imagined the centaur fair and two-eyed; I can as well imagine it black-haired and one-eyed, though the two possibilities are incompatible: if one is posited as existing, the other is excluded in being, while they are equally possible as ideal possibilities. Every ideally possible being has its own system of harmonious but merely possible perceptions. As soon as we posit that a determinate centaur exists, one of the ideal possibilities is singled out over the others; it receives its thesis of existence, and the attempted theses of the others are cancelled against it. A note adds that the phantasy-givenness of an individual is incompletely determined, but if it is an individual and thought as posited, one of the possible presenting manifolds is distinguished. The argument now has its sharpest form. Existence is the selection of one possibility from a field of equally consistent alternatives.
