Ideal possibility, being indifferent among incompatibles, cannot effect the selection. Something else must, and the next units show that it can only be an actual positing in actual experience.

The argument continues (75.32–76.13). If I imagine a thing, a perceiving ego is essentially imagined along with it, which has the thing given through adumbrating data, apprehensions, and appearances; the two are inseparable. So long as the thing is merely imagined, pure ideal possibility, the consciousness and ego are correspondingly merely imagined. Conversely, so long as we present only ideally possible perceiving egos with their ideally possible perceptions, even harmoniously directed at the same object, we never get beyond the ideal possibility of things and never reach the positing of a real existence; the other possibilities incompatible with the imagined one remain equally valid. Fictive object and fictive ego are correlates. This blocks the realist's last resort: possible consciousness is fictive consciousness, and fictive consciousness can confer only fictive existence. The point condenses the whole debate of Texts No. 1–3 into a symmetry.

But as soon as we substitute an actually existing perceiving ego for a merely possible one, an ego that has actual experience with an actual thesis of the previously merely possible thing, "the possibilities are no longer free"; countless parallel possibilities can no longer be posited, and positing them must be cancelled by conflict with the actual thesis (76.13–28). If the actual thesis, a perceptual thesis that posits the thing appearing so and so as actually experienced, maintains itself harmoniously in the actual ego's further experience, this is not merely one process among others in that consciousness: "the thing then actually exists." And exactly as far as the force of harmonious positing already carried out reaches in the actual consciousness, so far reaches the rationality of assuming the thing's existence. Actuality of consciousness is what breaks the symmetry of possibilities. The proportion between the rationality of the existence-assumption and the reach of harmonious positing is the doctrine of degrees of rational positing in Ideen I §§138–141, now used as the measure of existence itself.

It is clear that if the assumption that the thing exists, that one of the ideal possibilities actually holds, is to be rational, the ideal possibility of a consciousness experiencing the thing does not suffice; an actually experiencing consciousness is required, an actually existing ego in experiential relation to the thing (76.29–77.3). If the thing is to exist actually, so that the assumption is justified not in a limited way but fully, an actual ego must exist in whose experiences the thing's being not only announces itself, but in such a way that nothing appears in its consciousness that would cancel this being, and the course of its consciousness does not leave this being open. The text distinguishes two grades: limited rationality, where experience supports the thing but further experience could cancel it, and full justification, where nothing cancels and nothing remains open. The second is an ideal limit that the next unit interprets.

It is not necessary that the infinity of experiences belonging to the full legitimation of a thing be actually realized in an actual consciousness (77.4–23). That would be countersensical: the infinity, in its multidimensionality, cannot be realized even in an infinite stream with its one-dimensional temporality. On the other hand, the idea of the existence of a thing prescribes that an actually experiencing consciousness exists which can have immediate or at least mediate experience of it (a note explains: other things are experienced from which the ego could find the thing in question), and that real possibilities of motivation for further legitimations are prescribed in this actual consciousness. The possibilities of experience arising from these motivations, anchored in actual givens, are no longer purely ideal and wholly free but bound by theses of being. The existence of the thing is always an idea for actual consciousness, not in the sense of a purely ideal being like a number or species, but a multidimensional idea in the Kantian sense; one could even use the term regulative idea, though with modified sense. The argument about dimensions is new and exact: the totality of a thing's possible appearances is a multidimensional manifold (positions, orientations, lighting, changes), while any stream of experience is linear in time, so no stream can traverse the whole. The full givenness of the thing is therefore an idea, and Ideen I §143 says the same with the same Kantian vocabulary. The "modified sense" of the regulative idea is that for Kant the idea regulates the use of the understanding without determining an object, while for Husserl it prescribes the type of a determinate object's infinite givenness.

What has been experienced prescribes regulatively a certain style to further experiences: they must run so that the experienced agrees with what is further experienced, and in this agreement the one spatiotemporal reality constitutes itself in freely continuing confirmation and closer determination (77.24–78.4). It belongs to the essence of thingly being to be placed in a spatiotemporal infinity, the openness of a world-horizon, and hence an experiencing subject for whom a thing is actual need not experience that thing itself and directly. Other things must then be experienced that hang together with it in the unity of a world of experience according to laws of experience, so that real motivations run on from an actually experienced connection whose pursuit would lead to the thing. Working this out would be a separate topic. A later note: "But here I forgot the whole doctrine of intersubjectivity! As in the whole lecture of 1915. Was that war psychosis?" The "style" of experience and the "world-horizon" are the concepts through which the special thesis becomes plausible for the countless things no one has perceived: they are actual because they are motivated from an actual experience through the lawful nexus of the world. Ideen I §§27 and 47 use the horizon in the same way. Husserl's note is a severe self-criticism. The argument has been carried out for a single ego, so that the actual consciousness that anchors the world is one consciousness, and the intersubjective constitution of the one objective world is missing. Beilage IV and Texts No. 7 and 9 repair the omission. The question about "war psychosis" dates the note after 1915 and expresses Husserl's later astonishment at the solipsistic form of an argument that the published Ideen I shares.

The actual existence of a thing, and so of a real world, requires more than an ideally possible ego and ego-consciousness, more than ideally possible experiences and connections of experience, since with these we never get beyond ideally possible, fictable things (78.5–20). It requires an actual ego distinguished by its content, an actually existing consciousness with actual experiences and experiential theses. This holds for every transcendent being that comes to givenness, and in principle can only come to givenness, through appearances and manifestations as physical things do. It holds also for all transcendences that have their footing mediately in intuitive things, so for those we call "physical transcendences," "ions," "energies," and so forth, substructed by theoretical thought on the basis of intuitively given things. The phrase "distinguished by its content" names the decisive point: the special thesis requires not just some actual consciousness but one whose actual content motivates this world. The extension to theoretical entities answers the note at 74: physical objects inherit their dependence from the intuitive things on which theory builds, the relation Ideen I §52 describes.

The in-itself of the world may have a good sense, but one thing is absolutely certain: it does not have the sense of the world's independence of actually existing consciousness (78.21–30). In principle the world is what it is only as the correlate of experiencing consciousness related to it, and not merely possible but actual consciousness. To strike every stream of consciousness from actual being is to strike the actual world, all actually existing reality. The world, every conceivable world, is thinkable only as relative to the actuality of consciousness. But this relativity is not to be reversed. Two claims are joined: the special thesis in its strongest form, "every conceivable world," and its asymmetry. The first is the formula Ideen I §49 quotes almost word for word: the world of transcendent res is entirely dependent on consciousness, "and not on logically conceived but on actual consciousness." The editor's introduction identifies this passage as one of the central formulations behind it.

Now follow "the important contrasting propositions" (78.30–79.16). Consciousness can very well be actual without there being a world to which it relates as experiencing, as the earlier considerations show. If there is a world, it means for actual consciousness rules of really possible experience; its existence has its correlates in connections of experience ordered and prescribed in content in a quite determinate way. But these connections are not necessary in themselves. A unified stream of consciousness is very well thinkable in which perceptions and other experiences are not ordered as the actual being of a world prescribes. We can imagine that things in the world suddenly begin to go as in free fiction, where in the play of phantasy everything runs irrationally into everything else and no thing-unities hold together. "So the existence of consciousness is not relative to the existence of an actual reality." Intentionality belongs to the essence of consciousness, but it does not belong to the essence of intentionality that anything transcendently intended "actually exist," or, equivalently, that consciousness's intentions of reality be harmoniously fulfilled. This is the argument of Ideen I §49 in its original lecture form, with the same image of experience dissolving into a play of phantasy without thing-unities. The final clause supplies the principle §49 leaves implicit. Intentionality is essential to consciousness, but fulfilment is not; consciousness of a world can therefore exist while the world fails to exist, so long as the stream remains a stream. The guard against a common misreading follows from this: the annihilation thought-experiment does not posit a consciousness without intentionality, only one whose intentions to reality are not harmoniously fulfilled.

The relativity between real world and consciousness is therefore, strictly speaking, not existentially a correlativity: "the being of a real world is contingent for the being of a consciousness" (79.17–23). Consciousness, the sphere of possible immanent being, can exist without any transcendent reality, while the being of the transcendent depends entirely on the being of a consciousness. The terminology fixes the result: correlation of sense holds both ways, but existential dependence holds only one way. This is the precise content of Ideen I §49's contrast between the absolute being of consciousness and the merely intentional being of the world, and the editor's introduction cites the formula as the core of the thesis of the being-priority of transcendental consciousness.

"But this says only: a single monadic being is thinkable without constituted nature" (79.24–30). "Thinkable" means that we can fill immanent time at will so that no nature is constituted. But it is already a separate and new problem when the stream of consciousness can be uniquely predetermined in advance for its ego; and the situation is new again if we assume several coexisting monads. Husserl immediately restricts the result. The independence of consciousness from the world has been shown only for a single monad, by free variation of its immanent content. Whether the factual course of a monad's stream is determined in advance, and what happens to the thesis when several monads coexist and must share a world, are open. The word "monad" appears here, in 1913, for the concrete transcendental ego with its stream; the questions it raises are those of Text No. 7, of Hua XIII–XV, and of the fifth Cartesian Meditation, where the existential independence of the single monad is qualified by its essential relation to others.

## No. 6. From the Lecture Course "Selected Problems of Phenomenology", Summer Semester 1915

### § 1. The Opposition of Immanence and Transcendence. The Relevance of the Eidetic Discussions Concerning It for Fixing the Field of Phenomenology

The lecture starts from a given fact, the natural world (80.9–20). I am, and this world is constantly there for me, a world in which I place myself. That this spatiotemporal world is present for me means that through the continuous stream of my consciousness there constantly runs a layer of intentional experiences that constitute for me, in the manner of appearance, an ordered connection of intentional objects as "realities." The reality of all these things is reality for me; my intentional experiences, as consciousness of things, are thetic experiences, acts of belief that posit being. The opening reproduces Ideen I §27 ("the world of the natural attitude: I and my surrounding world") almost sentence for sentence, but with one difference: the natural world is described from the start in the vocabulary of constitution and thesis, as Ideen I §30 describes the "general thesis." The 1915 lecture presents the fundamental consideration as a course, and its beginning shows how Husserl taught it two years after the book.

But what is believed is not straightforwardly what objectively exists (80.21–28). A thing can stand before me as reality in full bodily presence, perceived, and I may later be led to give up my belief: "it was only a hallucination." What was just characterized as actual is, so to speak, crossed out in consciousness and receives the character "null." The crossing out (Durchstreichung) is the phenomenological description of non-being: not the absence of a thing behind the appearance, but a modification of the thesis within consciousness. Ideen I §§106 and 138 treat negation and cancellation in this way.

The evidence of reflective positing of every cogito offers a sphere of being in which I see immediately that I have an absolute right to posit (80.29–81.10). Here belief is not mere opinion; its certainty is rational and given in its rationality, simply indubitable. And this can be extended. I have an indubitable right not only to posit the grasped cogito as the momentary now but also to grasp a horizon of its past; in the moment of reflection it not only is but comes into view, and it already was beyond doubt, though some of it may have grown unclear. Proceeding this way, it may show an absolute right for the whole flow of consciousness as this streaming with its more or less dark horizons. So the evidence Descartes asserted I may not only accept with clearest insight but extend with insight into its right. The extension answers the "gap" noted in Text No. 1 (6, note 2): the absolute givenness of consciousness is not confined to the punctual present but covers the retentionally held past and, in principle, the whole stream. Ideen I §46 makes the same extension when it insists that the indubitability of immanent perception covers the stream "as given in reflection."

But what of the world that is constantly there for me (81.10–29)? In the natural attitude I count the flowing consciousness with its visible formations as part of it, but also the material spatiotemporal world, in which some things stand out as bodies of animals, my own body among them; with others' bodies I connect subjects and streams of consciousness, analogues of my own. I think I have all this given in experience; I think about it and do science of it, and as rigorous natural science I take myself to perform rational activities that entitle me to posit this world with this or that content. So far as these activities are formations of my consciousness, they belong to what can be grasped indubitably. But what of the being of what is supposedly known rationally in them? With regard to reason in its relation to its correlative being, there is an essential difference from the being of the cogitationes and their rational positing. The world of the natural attitude includes consciousness itself, as the psychic life of bodily beings; this is the point Ideen I §§33 and 53 must address before the reduction can separate pure consciousness from the psychological. The question is not whether rational acts exist, which is indubitable, but whether their correlates exist, which is the problem of reason Ideen I §§135–136 open.

The difference is named by the pair immanent and transcendent (81.30–82.18). A note adds that the opposition also concerns the pure ego (person) and pure monad in general and what is constituted in it as outside, outside the concrete ego. Immanent being, the being of the concrete ego and of consciousness in its thousandfold formations, is immanently positable; reflective experience is an absolute self-grasping and thus a real having in itself. The rationality of positing something that the positing consciousness has in itself raises no questions. In reflective experience the desire on which I direct my gaze is not merely meant as something transcendent; it is not only the intentional object but at the same time really incorporated in the reflecting act. But where I posit a thing or anything transcendent, I posit something that with its own essence does not enter into the consciousness of it, which is monadic being, a possible knowing subject. In external perception we have the consciousness of "bodily there," yet we must always say that the thing only "appears," that is, appears itself only under anticipation. And wherever the intentional object is an individual that presents itself only through appearance under anticipation, it holds, rightly, that the perceived need not be, however clear and perfect the perception. The definition of immanence is that of Ideen I §38: an act is immanently directed when its object belongs to the same stream as the act, so that the object is both its intentional object and part of its reell content. "Under anticipation" is the 1915 phrase for what Ideen I §44 describes as the horizon of co-meant but not given sides, and the conclusion, that the perceived need not be, is §46's thesis of the contingency of the thing. The marginal note extends immanence to the monad as a whole, marking that what is "outside" is defined relative to the concrete ego, a later idiom.

This seems grist to the mill of "backwoods realism" (82.19–29), which says: quite right, only my flow of consciousness is immediately given; but from it, by virtue of the motives it offers in its mixture of order and disorder, I "infer" an explaining transcendent cause. The inference is never absolutely certain, but probable, and the probability is not blind but secured by rational motives. That is all that can be attained and all that can be demanded. Husserl's term (Wald- und Wiesenrealismus) dismisses as unreflective the realism that the 1908 texts attacked under the name of the inference theory. The probabilistic version is the most defensible form, Helmholtz's or Külpe's, and the lecture will refute it in §6.

Should we be satisfied with that (82.30–83.7)? The skeptics, perhaps Gorgias already, would reply: in the course of consciousness conjectures appear, and conjectures with a rational character. However much they refer to a transcendent world, one can always find it questionable whether such a world, never absolutely self-given, exists at all. In consciousness I weigh and ground probabilities and more probabilities; however much they gain in force, it remains possible that this world does not exist, and perhaps a world exists quite different from the one posited as probable. If it is in itself, external to consciousness, why should it care about our ways of consciousness, our immanent rational characters? The skeptic exploits the realist's own premise: if the world is external to consciousness and only probable from within, nothing guarantees any correspondence between rational probability and being. Gorgias's second thesis, that if anything existed it could not be known, is the classical form of the argument.

Should we appeal to divine veracity (83.8–31)? Should we assume a harmony established by God between nature in itself and our consciousness, such that the nature meant in consciousness must, necessarily or probably, mirror actual nature, and that theories built in natural-scientific method, according to logical norms, must agree with the world God has made? Such thoughts may please us theologically, but philosophically they have a flaw: the existence of God and of divine creation is again a transcendence. If it is in principle unintelligible how consciousness can grasp and determine something in principle transcendent to it, so that the transcendent actually is as determined, every attempt at solution that resorts to a special kind of transcendent existence and its doings is a circle. Descartes's divine guarantee and Lotze's harmony fall to the objection already made in Appendix I: the guarantor is itself transcendent and so falls under the problem. The charge of circularity states formally what the 1908 texts called metabasis.

Every solution to a problem must, on pain of countersense, not use anything that the problem calls into question in its generality, either as unproblematic and valid or, like the skeptics, as possible (83.32–36). The skeptical objection presupposes the possibility. The second clause turns the methodological rule against the skeptic. He too uses something the problem questions, namely the possibility of a world in itself that would be external to consciousness and so might fail to correspond. That this possibility makes sense is exactly what is in question; the idealist argument will show that it does not.

All this is taken as a mere prelude (83.37–84.9). It reminds us that the relation between consciousness and transcendent reality has great difficulties, with which "epistemology" notoriously struggles in the most varied theories. The task here is not to build an epistemology. The aim was fixed earlier as fixing the field of phenomenology. But this aim requires that we deal to some extent with this relation and carry out some eidetic discussions about it, which may contribute something relevant to epistemological questions too. The statement of purpose is the key to the place of idealism in Ideen I. In the book, as in this lecture, the discussion of consciousness and reality serves to delimit the field of pure consciousness (§33: "a new region of being"), not to answer the epistemological question for its own sake; §§51 and 55 say explicitly that the transcendental-idealist consequences are drawn only so far as the delimitation requires. The 1908 texts pursued the problem as epistemology and metaphysics; by 1913–1915 their results have been subordinated to the task of securing phenomenology's field, which is exactly the form they take in the "fundamental consideration."

### § 2. Rational Consciousness and Its Relation to Transcendent Being as a Phenomenological Problem. External Perception as the Ultimate Source of Right for All Rational Assertions about Transcendent Things

A new consideration begins, concerning the relation between transcendent being, nature, and consciousness (84.15–85.20). We stand fully consciously on the ground of reason, but without prejudicial assumptions: we will speak only of what we can bring to perfect clarity and insight. It is no dubious prejudice but can be made absolutely certain that every assertion means something, a proposition, a "so it is," and every proposition is true or false. The ideas "truth" and "falsity" point back to an essential connection between asserting consciousness in general and rational consciousness. Not every assertion is rational; an assertion can be spoken into the air without any ground, and then nothing in the opinion as living consciousness confers a ground of right on it or makes it possible, by mere analysis of its content, to state one. Yet it is no accident, nor a merely occasional ideal possibility, that such a blind assertion can be converted into one more or less illuminated by reason. Every blind assertion can be transformed into a rational one: every one prescribes by its sense paths of reason on which either it, with the sense it has, passes into a rationally characterized assertion, or its opposite does, so that it is crossed out in grounding, and as crossed out it is rational. In all scientific discussion we strive for truth, which means striving for paths of reason on which we gain rationally grounded propositions. The skeptic too, in putting forward his theses, claims to convince us; he argues and expects his claims to become insights through his groundings. The starting point is the phenomenology of reason that Ideen I places at the end, in §§136–145, now placed first. It contains the principle on which the idealist argument depends: every meaning prescribes, by its sense, a path of confirmation or refutation. If this holds, the realist's being "in itself," whose existence no path could confirm or refute, is a meaning without rational sense. The reference to the skeptic's own claim to convince is the classical retortion argument, used to show that the ground of reason is not an assumption.

This reflection says nothing but what we see (85.21–33). We make no mere assertions; we have carried them out in rational consciousness and convinced ourselves by suitable examples. We speak of reason, and reason is the greatest of all problems, especially as to the relation between rational consciousness and transcendent being. But what we have said of reason so far were only general essential features of asserting acts and the self-understanding based on them. We hold fast to what we have, and it is absolutely certain that if we wish to explore fields of reason, insightful reason must govern our statements about them; to depart from this would be to talk at random. The methodological self-reference is careful. The theory of reason is itself carried out rationally, but what has been claimed is only eidetic structure, not the solution to the problem of transcendence, so there is no circle.

Every grounding, immediate or mediate, leads back in the end to intuition (85.34–86.7). In the sphere of existence, all groundings lead back to immediate statements about individual being, those that as immediate statements of experience merely explicate what is given in simple experience. "Perception is the ultimate source of right for all rational assertion about existence," since perception is the most originary experience; memory gives itself in itself as reproduction. This is the principle of all principles (Ideen I §24) restricted to individual existence. The remark on memory places it as a derived mode, which is the doctrine of the modifications of originary givenness in Ideen I §§99–101 and 140.

Taking all simple intuitive graspings of existence, present, past, or future, under the title "experience," all further thought and assertion about existence build on genuine statements of experience, so all scientific knowledge of individual existence is called "empirical knowledge" (86.8–26). This holds for knowledge of nature, the sphere of individual realities called nature in the widest sense, "world." It is, as said, a sphere of transcendent knowledge. The transcendent, a single thing, a connection of things, a law of nature, can be rationally posited and known only on the basis of experience and finally perception, and in the ordinary sense this means "external" perception, perception of the thingly, worldly transcendent. The chain is now closed: knowledge of nature rests on external perception. Whatever external perception is, it must be the ultimate source of right for every claim about nature; so the problem of transcendence becomes the problem of what external perception gives.

How is the transcendent given in simple perception and experience, and is it actually given (86.27–87.6)? The naive person says yes. But even before science he leaves open the possibility that this givenness is a mere claim to validity, to be rejected under the titles "illusion" and "hallucination." Perceptions are characterized in themselves, evidently by their pure essence, as experiences in which the intentional object is conscious in the character of bodily presence (Leibhaftigkeit). Performing such an experience, we have consciousness of an object in the manner in which, as it were, the object calls out to us: "I am here myself, in my very own person, now, in the present." Even this is not adequate: the object does not only call so, "it stands there bodily," a general and familiar mode of givenness. This does not prevent the object, though seen, heard, grasped immediately, from not existing. The term Leibhaftigkeit, bodily or "in-person" presence, is the descriptive mark of perception that Ideen I §§1, 39 and 136 use. The correction of the image of a call is significant: the object does not announce itself through the perception, which would make perception a sign; it stands there itself. The compatibility of in-person presence with non-existence is what makes thing-perception contingent without making it indirect.

The object can confirm itself harmoniously in the course of perception, or turn out to be null (87.7–20). Despite the "bodily there" it is not; its characterization as existing must be crossed out, as when we see a ghostly apparition and, approaching, grasp at air. If not, if perceptions run into one another normally and join without discord, the naive person simply speaks about the thing; every normal statement beginning "the thing there," "this table," "my house" implies the opinion that the thing is a rightful reality, and it counts for him as real just as it stands in perception, with the properties perception shows as bodily present. The naive view is stated precisely so that its two components can be separated. Its claim that the thing is actual, within the limits of harmonious experience, is correct; its claim that the thing is just as it appears, with its sensory qualities, is what science contests. §3 turns to the second.

### § 3. Primary and Secondary Qualities. Levels of Transcendence: The Transcendence of the Perceptual Thing and That of the Physical Thing

One will object that the naive person's opinions are not worth discussing, since natural science says what things are in objective truth (87.25–88.8). The naive worldview, which takes the world as it presents itself to sense intuition, is full of deception, as the distinction between primary and secondary qualities shows. The senses show things as coloured, sounding, tasting, rough or smooth, warm or cold; these qualities, which belong to the thing's bodily presence, are mere semblance. Science distinguishes "appearance" from reality: what appears is not actually as it appears in its "secondary" qualities, which have only the value of signs for certain primary qualities, mathematical-physical ones, with extension and its determinations as their basis. §3 corresponds exactly to Ideen I §40, which bears nearly the same title. The scientific realist enters as the more sophisticated heir of the naive one, and his doctrine is the sign theory in its classical, Lockean form.

A common presentation pretends that the qualities given in perception group themselves into secondary, specific sense qualities and the rest, which physics prefers as alone usable, as Locke did (88.9–38). Berkeley raised the decisive objection: in perception I find the thing coloured and extended, rough and extended; the qualities do not lie separately side by side, but the colour spreads out, the roughness spreads out, the secondary qualities cover the extension. More precisely, we can recolour the thing or its extension at will, but if we try to strike out colour, or every visual quality, not a bare extension remains but nothing. There is an a priori here: extension without colour or other sense quality is unthinkable, nonsense. So if physics really demanded that we deny objective validity to sense qualities and compose true being from primary qualities alone, with a qualityless extension as foundation, it would be not truth but countersense. Its meaning must be another: that the whole thing standing there bodily, with all its perceptually presented qualities, primary and secondary, is mere appearance. Husserl accepts Berkeley's argument but turns it against Berkeley's conclusion. The inseparability of extension and sense quality is an eidetic law of founding, in the sense of the third Logical Investigation; so the Lockean split of the perceived thing into objective and subjective qualities is impossible, and physics, if it is to make sense, must treat the whole intuitive thing as appearance of something else. Ideen I §40 draws the same consequence and states it as the scientific realist's own view, which §52 then corrects.

What the naive person takes as reality on the basis of harmonious perceptions is not partly but wholly mere appearance, appearance of a true reality not experienced or experienceable in any single determination (89.1–13). If the physical concepts substructed beneath everything that appears, ether, ions, energies, refer back to spatial predicates and so presuppose a space in which ether vibrations, ion migrations, and propagations of energy occur, physical space must be a mere symbol for something announced somehow in perceptual space but in principle not identical with it. The realist's position is now carried to its consequence. If physics' objects are wholly unexperienceable, its space too must be a symbol, since perceptual space is part of the appearance. The realist has arrived at an in-itself without even spatial determinations, the Kantian thing in itself, which the countersense argument of the 1908 texts excludes.

The whole of experienced reality would then rank as mere appearance in its entire intuitive content (89.14–34). It would not be mere semblance, since semblance points somehow to being. Harmonious perception indicates actual being; the appearing thing gives us an X, the real, still to be determined, and the phenomenal qualities themselves give instructions for determining it. In physical method we learn to use these instructions and to interpret these "signs" for true determinations, determining the transcendent step by step by complexes of atoms and ions. But these are substructions that indicate what is in principle unimaginable and inexpressible in the language of experience. In the reverse direction, physical knowledge serves as an index for the course of possible perceptions and the connections of sense-things, which makes it practically useful for life, interested as it is in the sense-things. The realist's position contains, under its sign vocabulary, the true structure Husserl will defend. The appearing thing gives an X to be determined; physical determination works from appearances; and physics, in turn, predicts perceptions. Ideen I §52 keeps all three elements, and drops only the claim that the X is something unexperienceable behind the appearing thing rather than the appearing thing itself determined by physics. The phrase "determinable X" returns in Ideen I §131 for the noematic core of the object.

So the situation presents itself from the standpoint of a realism built on the fact of natural science and its kind of judgments, with the purifications needed for consistency (89.35–90.3). That a certain truth must dwell in this view is obvious; but we cannot accept it without reflection. Husserl presents realism at its strongest before criticizing it, and he grants it a truth. The critique of §§5–6 will be a reinterpretation, not a rejection, of what physics says.

First, a double transcendence emerges: that of the perceptual thing, the thing of simple experience, and that of the physical thing (90.4–30). The order of rational knowledge in natural science brings with it a sequence of levels of transcendence, starting from and grounded in immediate experience, which must therefore be examined first. One must not confuse the "subjectivity" of sense qualities, and of the whole sense-thing, with the subjectivity of perceptual consciousness. The sense-thing is "merely subjective appearance," it is said, a sign for the real in itself; but this appearance is not the being of something immanent. The seen table as such, with its seen qualities, is not the perceptual experience but transcendent to it, as is an illusory or hallucinated table, whether or not a table exists. Considering the pure essence of perception in the eidetic attitude, we recognize that the transcendence of the perceptual thing as it appears is no accidental but an essentially necessary transcendence, running through all its sensory determinations and having a peculiar systematic character. This is the decisive distinction of the lecture. The scientific realist calls the sense-thing "subjective," and the word slides between two meanings: relative to sensibility, which may be true, and immanent to consciousness, which is false. The sense-thing is already transcendent to the experience in which it appears, even if it is a hallucination. This separates two problems the realist confuses, the transcendence of the perceived to the perceiving and the relation of the perceived thing to the physical thing. Ideen I §§41 and 52 make the same separation.

The perception of a thing, of the table before us, is by its essence variable (90.31–36). As consciousness it is always consciousness of something, and this something, however perceptual consciousness changes, is what appears and can be described by its content of appearance. The analysis now begins on the eidetic level announced, with the pairing of a changing consciousness and an appearing object that is the starting point of Ideen I §41.

A priori, several possibilities exist (90.37–91.13). What appears, always the actually "bodily" correlate of consciousness, not the physical thing, either remains the same thing throughout the flow of perceptual change or does not. The latter happens if the illusory character of the object suddenly becomes conscious and another object is substituted: in half-light we see a man and, looking and approaching, suddenly realize that it is not a man but a figure, a statue set up as garden ornament. If perception remains perception of the same object, there are again a priori possibilities: the object appears unchanged or changing, moving or changing qualitatively or both. The example of the statue mistaken for a man is the paradigm of perceptual correction that Husserl uses again in Experience and Judgment §21 (the "mannequin" case). It shows that the identity of the perceived object across a flow of perception is itself an achievement that can fail, so that "same object" is a feature of the synthesis, not of the stream as such.

We are considering essential possibilities as in geometry, when in an ideally projected figure we let a ray move continuously or an angle grow (91.14–27). The existence of the example does not matter; what is seen are a priori connections. So the topic is not existing perceptions and existing objects but the essence of perception in general, its essentially possible changes, and the essential possibilities for perceived objects as such. These belong necessarily to the perceptual experience and are nevertheless transcendent to it. The comparison with geometry is the methodological model of Ideen I §§70–75: free variation in phantasy discloses essential possibilities, and the existence of the exemplar is irrelevant. The final sentence states the paradox the following paragraphs resolve, that the perceived belongs necessarily to the perception and yet transcends it.

Take the ideally possible case that the perceived is conscious as unchanged (91.28–92.9). In continuous perception this unchanged table is constantly conscious. Evidently the continuous perceiving is not itself unchanged but changing. The modes of time-consciousness alone bring a continuous, ever-flowing manifold into the perceptual experience: a phase of perceptual now, a continuum of phases of what has just been, a horizon of what is coming. This constant form is always filled anew, a constant flow. Meanwhile the object stands there unchanged, and it belongs to the essence of consciousness of the unchanged that it is such a constant flow. One must see the contrast between the experience in its flowing reell stock and the unchanged perceived object, and see that the object is not a reell piece of this flow but what is constantly intentional in it and transcendent to the flow itself. This is the argument of Ideen I §41: even if the perceived thing does not change, the perception does, so the thing cannot be a part of the perception. Time-consciousness supplies the simplest proof of transcendence, since a flowing experience cannot contain an unchanging part as its reell component. The transcendence of the perceived to the perceiving is thus demonstrated without reference to physics, and it holds equally of a hallucinated table.

The distinction becomes clearer when we note that the perceived object can present itself in manifold perceptions of differing content, passing continuously into one another, from different sides (92.10–26). This too is no accidental fact but an essential law. Empirically we say that we change our position and see the same unchanged thing from above, from below, from this side or that, and ever new aspects become visible. It is not seen all at once in all its determinations; seeing is superficial and one-sided. We do not see into the "interior"; to do so we would have to divide the thing and view the parts, again superficially, from different sides. The second argument for transcendence is perspectival: the same object in incompatible presentations. The remark on the interior repeats Text No. 4 (66): even dissection only yields new surfaces, so the inside of a thing is never given as inside. Ideen I §§41 and 44 describe the same adumbrational structure.

Are these mere facts (92.27–30)? It is strange that no one has noticed that essential necessities are expressed in all this, which can and must be pursued and described systematically. The rhetorical question closes §3 by turning what the empiricist treats as contingent features of human perception, viewpoint, one-sidedness, opacity of the inside, into essential laws. Once they are essential, no higher intellect can escape them, and the argument against the divine "adequate" perception of things, which §4a begins with, is already implicit.

### § 4. The Pure Essence of Perception

#### a) The Givenness of the Thing in Orientations

Something easily misleading must be addressed (93.3–11). The perceived thing is always given in a certain orientation. One might think this contingent and empirical: I who perceive see the thing now from here, now from there, and it presents itself differently for psychological reasons. God, it is said, is everywhere; what is scattered, fragmented, imperfectly visible for us is given to God at one stroke, absolutely, not through appearances but adequately. The theological objection is placed at the head of the analysis because it is the most natural way to deny that adumbration belongs to the essence of thing-givenness. If God could see things without perspective, perspective would be a human limitation, and the inference theory's model of a hidden, adequately knowable thing would be restored.

To avoid turning theology philosophically in a bad sense, consider this (93.12–21). God represents the idea of absolute knowledge. If it is to be a possible idea, its possibility must be capable of legitimation; it must be an insightful possibility, an eidetic insight, and if it conflicts with eidetic insights it is an impossible possibility, a countersense. The thing, it is said, is given "to us" in orientation; let us see what in this is contingent. We, the empirical real egos, are posited here; let us suspend that positing, since the consideration is to be purely eidetic. The principle is the one Ideen I §§43 and 79 apply: an idea of knowledge, including divine knowledge, is admissible only if it is compatible with the essence of the object known. The suspension of the empirical ego is an application of the reduction within the eidetic analysis: orientation is to be examined in the pure essence of perception, not as a feature of human beings.

Holding the empirically real ego as an idea, we can vary much without changing the essence of the perception of the thing, of the table taken as example (93.22–36). In ideal possibility we can modify our body at will and modify whatever we attribute to ourselves as mental egos; perhaps what is thought here as empirical ego can be struck out altogether. But one thing is evident: if we hold fixed the essence of the perception with what is perceived in it as it is perceived, the orientation remains. What appears as such gives itself as a There to a Here, and this Here is a point of reference unaffected by whatever we do in imagination with our human ego, body, and personality. It is a matter not of the human being but of the essence of perception and its perceived as such. The variation detaches orientation from the body and the person and assigns it to the noematic structure of the perceived: the "here" is a moment of every appearance as such. Ideen II §18 and §41 develop the "zero point of orientation" in connection with the lived body; the lecture first shows that the here is essential before asking what bears it.

Holding to this eidetically graspable essence, we can place it in connected series, let perception pass into perceptual connections that are always perceptions of the same object yet of different essential stock (94.1–17). Each such perception or phase carries its zero point of orientation and a mode of orientation, which we express empirically by "I approach, I withdraw, I turn," or "the object turns, approaches, recedes." All this expresses essential occurrences in the perceived as such, which do not concern the perceiving empirical human ego at all, or concern it only insofar as the perceptions are attributed to it as its own, that is, only because they already have their essence with their oriented givenness. The empirical descriptions, I move or the object moves, are interpretations of an essential structure that precedes the attribution of perceptions to a person. Orientation is not a fact about humans that happens to affect perception; humans are oriented because the perceptions attributed to them are.

We then recognize the essential law that the Here and the There, the centre of orientation and the relative place of the thing, are necessarily separate (94.18–23). The Here can approach the There at will, but only asymptotically; it cannot reach it. This can be seen a priori: so long as the object stands there bodily at all, it stands opposite the Here; it is a There. The asymptotic law excludes the limiting case in which perceiver and perceived coincide. A thing perceived without distance would no longer be perceived as a thing; the law states, in spatial form, the transcendence of the perceived.

But God is everywhere, it is said (94.24–95.8). Does that mean that God could perceive the same thing that appears in such orientations in one closed perception, in an image as it were, without orientation? And if he is everywhere, could he perceive from here and at the same time have another Here, the There as his Here? All this is countersense. To each Here belongs a definite appearance, and the manifold appearances corresponding to different Heres can come to unity a priori only in a temporally extended continuum of appearances; they are incompatible in coexistence. If God has the appearance of what is There in the Now, he cannot at the same time take his orientation in the There. A systematic connection of ideal possibilities obtains in which nothing but an appearance can ever occur in the unity of identification. This is as evident as that intersecting straight lines cannot intersect a second time however far extended, or that 0.333... does not finally yield a rational number if we only count far enough. Orientation and adumbrating appearance are inseparable, and the relation to orientation attaches equally to every component of the appearance. Omnipresence is analysed as simultaneous possession of all perspectives, and this is shown to be contradictory: perspectives on the same thing are compatible only in succession, as phases of a synthesis. So the infinity of a thing's appearances can be unified only in time, the point Text No. 5 (77) made with the multidimensional idea. The comparisons with geometry and arithmetic insist that this is an eidetic necessity binding any intellect. Ideen I §43 states the conclusion: even God has things only through appearances, and to think otherwise is to confuse an idea with an absurdity.

#### b) What Is Properly Given and What Is Merely Co-Meant

In perception a thing appears, but by its essence it appears only on its surface, on one side of the surface, and with the qualitative determinations belonging to this side and orientation (95.10–34). It belongs to the essence of perception that it means to grasp more than it actually grasps: one must distinguish what of the thing properly falls into the perception from the thing itself, which is nonetheless what is perceived, what the perception means. Perception is giving consciousness, giving in bodily presence, and yet a stock of what is actually and properly given in bodily presence necessarily separates from a stock of what is merely co-conscious, so to speak co-meant. In the "sense" of the perception the thing is not just a front side; it is a full thing with its back sides and interior, though these do not come to actual bodily presentation. Consequently perception points ahead to new perceptions, essentially possible ones, in which what is co-meant, more or less determinately, would come to givenness and closer determination. Every perception has the ideal possibility of passing over, or being freely carried over, into new perceptions that enrich the perceiving consciousness with bodily present determinations. The distinction between the properly given and the co-meant is the one Text No. 1 (9) used for critique; here it is a structure of perception itself. Perception does not first give a surface and then infer a thing; it gives the thing, with a sense that exceeds what is presented. Ideen I §44 formulates the same: the thing is given with "a core of what is actually presented" and a "horizon" of non-genuine co-givenness.

The unfolding manifold is a temporal continuum of perceptions, each phase necessarily one-sided (95.35–96.19). With each step new determinations fall into proper givenness while those just properly perceived sink into the dark of improper givenness. Through this continuum runs a unity of consciousness: it is not an external stringing together of ever new perceptions, though they join continuously, but one perceiving consciousness extends through the continuous passing over. The whole is one intentional experience, the continuity of a changing perceiving, and as a whole it is itself one perception of the one same unchanged object, a many-sided perceiving, in which ever more sides gradually fall within the range of givenness, but never so that we terminate in a perceptual Now that gives all sides at once. The continuum of perception is itself a perception, a synthesis of identification in the sense Ideen I §118 calls continuous synthesis. The impossibility of a terminal Now restates the law of §4a: the sides of a thing are compatible only in succession, so the thing is given only in a process.

Again there is an a priori: however far we extend such manifolds, we always obtain only continuously unified perceptions that give the object imperfectly (96.20–97.10). We reach no conclusion. Perception always leaves much of the thing open and undetermined; there is always a horizon of possible determinability "in my power," always new continua to be added in which the supposedly unchanged object shows new determinations. A note: open capability is anticipated for me according to my capacity to continue the perception harmoniously. This talk of horizon is to be understood purely immanently. Whether perception shows the thing from the same side or brings new sides, the horizon moment necessarily belongs to it, in which more of the same thing is co-meant, indeterminately and non-intuitively. The empty indeterminacy is not empty in general but a determinable indeterminacy. Perception is perception of a thing, of a table, and however unknown the table's unseen parts may be, a rule (my capacity) of possible further perceptions is prescribed, which must run in definite ways with definitely regulated contents if the continuing perception is to be perception of a table and of the same table already perceived here and now. The concept of horizon here receives its classical form: determinable indeterminacy, prescribed by a rule, correlated with an "I can." The note's "capability" (Vermöglichkeit) is the earliest form in this volume of the kinaesthetic "I can" that Ideen II §§18 and 60 make central. The insistence that horizon talk is "purely immanent" matters for the idealist argument: the horizon is a feature of the perceptual sense, not a hypothesis about unperceived things, so the transcendence of the thing is described entirely within consciousness. Ideen I §44 uses the phrase "horizon of determinable indeterminacy" verbatim.

Consequently every perception is consciousness of bodily givenness and yet a consciousness that constantly demands legitimation, as it were (97.11–24). Perception posits the object as existing, and this positing with its perceptual stock motivates further possible perceptions with their positings and particular content. If an actual extension of perception occurs, the perception is "confirmed" as to its positing while its givenness is enriched and determined further. As said earlier, every "external" perception has a certain transcendence in that it posits in its object more, by anticipation, than it brings to proper bodily presentation in its own content. Motivation now appears as the link between the actual perception and its real possibilities: the positing of the present side motivates expectations of further sides with determinate content. This is the "motivated possibility" of Texts No. 2–5 described from inside the perception. Ideen I §§47 and 138 rely on it: real possibility is what the horizon of an actual perception motivates.

No perception is thinkable that is not an inseparable mixture of what properly appears and what is emptily co-meant; but what it co-means can in principle be brought to proper givenness (97.25–98.3). Essentially, components belong to every perception that point to new possible perceptions, or possibilities (capacities) of carrying it over into continuous series in which the object would come ever more perfectly to bodily givenness. But the new perceptions bring losses as well as gains: as new sides become visible, sides previously visible become invisible; if the back becomes the front, the original front becomes the back. A note: this is not a loss of knowledge, of acquired knowledge, of what remains. The note distinguishes intuitive givenness from habitual knowledge. What leaves the field of vision is not lost to the perceiving subject, which retains it as known. This is the germ of the doctrine of sedimentation and habitual acquisition in Ideen II and the genetic texts; for the argument here, it explains how a thing can be progressively known although never given all at once.

A further imperfection is that the process is infinite and not completely predetermined by the essential content of the perceptions already carried out (98.4–12). The two are connected. It is infinite insofar as, however far the process has gone, much of the object as meant was not yet given or need not have been given. And a horizon of indeterminacy, of what is left open, is always there. The incompleteness has two dimensions: quantitative (infinitely many sides) and qualitative (the not-yet-determined, which may surprise). The second is what makes perception revisable.

However well we know an object, absolutely good knowledge does not exist; it always remains open that on approaching or in further experience something comes to light that earlier perceptions did not determinately mean (98.13–99.3). It belongs to the essence of perception that, however far continued, it leaves the appearing conscious as undetermined in various directions. But the indeterminacy is a determinability; it points to possible perceptions of a more determining character, not to any perceptions possible in themselves. Not just any perceptions can enter the perceptual connection in which this table is given; which can enter is essentially prescribed. However undetermined the table may be, a rule of possible perceptions is prescribed with this indeterminacy, which must run and pass over into one another in definite ways for the unified consciousness to be consciousness of a table, of the same table perceived from the start. Continua of perception are not just continua of experiences called perceptions; unity of consciousness runs through them. Determinability is bounded by the type of the object, so the horizon prescribes not anything at all but what fits a table. This regional prescription is the content of the "clue" function of the object in Ideen I §§149–150, and the reason a thing-perception can be falsified: a perception that violates the rule breaks the unity.

Essentially different occurrences must be distinguished (99.4–25). The object as perceptually meant, the object in the how of its givenness, as properly appearing or emptily co-meant, determined or undetermined, can show ever new sides and be determined ever more closely in connected series. Experience advances harmoniously if a continuously harmonious unity of these possible perceptions actually occurs. But the other ideal possibility is that it does not: perception goes on and suddenly a break comes. The break can take various a priori forms: I saw the object as uniformly red, came closer, and saw that it is in truth patchy; I took its shape for a sphere and further experience shows another shape. Or experience may lead to a far more radical break: the perceived proves illusory, nonexistent. Every perception and perceptual series leaves this open as a possibility. The typology of breaks distinguishes partial correction (being-otherwise) from total cancellation (non-being). Both are possibilities internal to the sense of thing-perception, and the second is the seed of the annihilation hypothesis: if the radical break is possible for any perception, it is possible, as an ideal limit, for all. Ideen I §§46 and 49 pass from the possibility of non-being of any thing to the possibility of a "world-annihilation" by exactly this generalization.

So we must say a priori that perception is consciousness of a thing's bodily givenness, yet it constantly "demands" further legitimations, since the double possibility of confirmation and rejection always remains (99.26–100.16). Every perception contains a doxic consciousness, a component by virtue of which what appears bodily is characterized as existing; in this respect we speak of positing being. The positing is necessarily positing of a determinate content, with a sense that prescribes what is meant as a thing so constituted. If new perception joins so as to form a harmonious, advancing experience of the same thing, the moment of positing continues, and in this harmonious continuation the original positing is strengthened and confirmed. Along with this something happens in the appearance-content and sense: a consciousness of identity runs through it, continuously of the one unbroken thing, and while this one thing is posited its sense is enriched and determined further. Otherwise unity is preserved in the sense, but this or that property is crossed out: the thing is "other" than meant; or the unity of sense is lost altogether, even as to the identical bearer of determinations, and as correlate of this radical break full cancellation appears, the consciousness "null," referred to the identical one. The analysis separates three layers: the doxic thesis, the sense that the thesis posits, and the identical bearer (later the "determinable X," Ideen I §131). Confirmation strengthens the thesis and enriches the sense; partial correction cancels properties while keeping the bearer; radical cancellation strikes the bearer itself. The doctrine of the modalization of belief and the noematic analysis of Ideen I §§103–106 and 131 is here presented in compact form, and its use in the lecture is to show that existence and non-existence of things are events within the life of positing consciousness.

#### c) Real Content and Intentional Object

Preferring the ideal possibility of a harmonious perceptual consciousness, continuously confirming and fulfilling itself, there is again much to describe (100.18–25). The contrast between what the perceptual experience contains really in itself and what is conscious in it as intentional object confronts us again and again, showing its remarkable peculiarity ever more clearly. The heading gives the subsection its theme, the distinction that Ideen I §§41 and 97 make the hinge of the separation of consciousness and world. By choosing the harmonious case, Husserl ensures that the distinction cannot be blamed on illusion: even in perfect perception, the perceived is not part of the perceiving.

It is not that perception is merely incomplete (100.26–101.5). One might be tempted to regard what actually falls into perception, the surface colours, the surface shapes they fill, the outlines, as real components of the perceptual experience. But on closer attention to the essence of perception and the differences in mode of givenness, we soon see that in stretches of a continuous perception in which a colour is constantly actually given, it is constantly and necessarily given in changing ways. So one must distinguish the appearing colour itself from its mode of givenness, and the given colour-covered surface from the mode of givenness of that surface. The temptation is the empiricist one: to identify the properly given part of the thing with the sense data. Husserl blocks it by showing that even the properly given colour varies in its manner of givenness while remaining the same colour, so the colour is not one of the varying data.

Every part, moment, or determination of the thing that comes to bodily appearance does so in changing adumbrations or presentations (101.6–22). Everywhere one must distinguish what appears from the adumbration that belongs to the real stock of the appearing, of the concrete perceiving consciousness. Empirically: depending on my position, the same unchanged colour of the unchanged object gives itself differently. The colour adumbrates itself as the surface it fills adumbrates itself in the shape-appearance, the one parallel with the other, both necessarily. Each phase has its immanent, really belonging essential stock of adumbrations, its "sense data." These must not be confused with what appears through them, and one must not be misled by expressions. This is Ideen I §41 almost verbatim: the "colour-adumbration" is a reell moment of the experience, the colour adumbrated a property of the transcendent thing. The parallel adumbration of colour and shape anticipates the inseparability of sensuous quality and extension established in §3 against Locke.

We speak of sensed colour and roughness, and also of the thing's colour and roughness, which comes to perception and appears in it (101.23–102.2). If we then go on, with regard to physical determination, to call the sensuously appearing thing mere appearance, we are easily led to call the sensuously appearing moments merely subjective and identify them with the sense data. That is fundamentally false. Suspend physics and stick to what perceptual consciousness says by its essence: it makes good sense to say that the coloured, rough thing is there, given; that it confirms itself as actual and is determined further; and also that it may first stand there bodily and as actual and later prove non-existent or other than first perceived. The diagnosis explains how the sign theory arises: the "subjectivity" of secondary qualities (relativity to sense organs) is conflated with the immanence of sensation. Once physics is bracketed, a step the lecture explicitly takes and Ideen I §52 presupposes, the sensuous thing appears as transcendent and as the bearer of existence and non-existence.

These statements, based on actual perceptual occurrences, must have their definite right; they are evident, though within limits (102.3–27). Now it is clear that the existence or non-existence of what appears is something other than the existence or non-existence of the appearance in the sense of perceiving consciousness itself. However further perception may run, and even if it shows that what was perceived does not exist, that this colour or shape does not belong to the thing, nothing can change the fact that the perception itself has its real stock, including its sense data. What I saw as a circle may prove an ellipse, what I saw as uniformly red may prove patchy; but the presenting sense data and their "apprehensions" as uniformly red or speckled cannot be cancelled in their existence. Even leaving existence aside, we can turn the gaze back from the bodily appearing moment of the object to what is really found in perception, especially the "sense data." This title means nothing psychophysically supposed or indirectly constructed but something always really findable in perception by reflection. The contrast between the cancellability of the appearing and the uncancellability of the appearance is Ideen I §46's contrast between the contingency of the thing and the necessity of the experience, demonstrated in miniature. The final sentence separates the phenomenological concept of sensation from the psychophysical one, the stimulus-effect of physiology, which would be a construction inside the world; the hyle of Ideen I §85 is the reflectively found sense datum of this passage.

One soon sees that it is countersensical to count the moment in which a shape is adumbrated and the shape itself in the same genus, despite the similar names (102.28–103.7). The shape belongs to the co-appearing space; it is a figure in space. It makes no sense to say that the shape-adumbration, the sensation-moment through which the spatial is presented, is itself something in space or even thinkable in it, and so for all sense data. The thing's colour appears as an occurrence at the thing, in the world of things; as filling space it has place, size, extension. To say the same of the sense datum "colour" is countersense. The one figure, if unchanged, has its definite objective position; but infinitely many essentially different presentations correspond to it. Are these infinitely many figures in space, where the presented figure is? Can infinitely many figures be in the same place with the same extent? The argument from the many presentations is a reductio of spatializing sensations. If adumbrations were spatial, the countless presentations of one figure would be countless figures in one place. Sensations are therefore non-spatial, and the "visual field" of sensations is not a piece of space, a point Ideen I §§41 and 81 record and Thing and Space develops. The categorial difference, adumbration and adumbrated belong to different genera, is the logical form of the difference between consciousness and reality.

So the essence of perception wonderfully admits a double direction of evident descriptions and leads to two fundamentally different series of descriptive occurrences (103.8–34). One is immanent, really immanent to the experience; the other transcendent. Perception is consciousness of an intentional object, and everything belonging to the object, taken strictly as what appears according to this perception, however bodily given, is "separated as by an abyss" from the really immanent. The experience has its real essence with many real components: sense data, the apprehension through which the data present this and nothing else, moments of turning toward and thematic interest, belief as certain or uncertain, conjecturing, doubting positing. What appears has in turn its essence, its real stock: it is a thing with its colours, shapes, properties, and its parts as real pieces, the tabletop, the legs. But the real stock of consciousness and the real stock of the intentional object are, by this analysis, outside all connection. The relation of consciousness to its intentional object in all its intentional moments is not a unity in the sense of a real whole whose moments are connected. Connection in the proper sense is lacking here, necessarily. It is an "irreal relatedness." The double description is the noetic-noematic parallelism of Ideen I §§88–98 in its first published-lecture form, before the terms noesis and noema were introduced into teaching. The "abyss" is the image Ideen I §49 uses for the gulf of sense between consciousness and reality. The decisive clause is the last: intentionality is not a real relation between two real terms but an "irreal" relatedness. This is why the realist's questions, how the two series are connected, how consciousness reaches the thing, are ill-posed: there is no real connection to explain, and the intentional relation is a structure of sense.

This announces a fundamental difference in mode of being between the thing, and the whole thingly world, and consciousness (103.35–104.7). Thing-perception is only one kind of consciousness under the general genus "consciousness in general." Everything under this genus can enter into real unity; in the real unity of the stream that is mine, all kinds of experiences occur or can occur as real parts. But nothing like a thing can ever enter this connection, and it cannot because it cannot even enter the perception. That holds for the whole world of things. The difference in mode of being is derived from the eidetic law of real unity: what can be part of a stream is consciousness; a thing can be part of no stream; so thing and consciousness differ in genus. This is the conclusion of Ideen I §42 ("being as consciousness and being as reality") reached by the same route, and it prepares the claim of §§7–8 that the two kinds of being are related as absolute and relative.

### § 5. The Peculiar Character of the Thing's Transcendence

If a thing were a real moment of consciousness, even in the slightest determination, it would be givable as consciousness is: a simple reflective glance could be cast on it, it would be given in immanent, adequate perception, and it would not itself be something adumbrated, merely presented (104.9–27). But it belongs in principle to the essence of thinghood, by essential necessity, that it can be given only through adumbration, only through appearance; so it is in principle transcendent to all consciousness. It is therefore fundamentally false to think that the human intellect's contingent imperfection is to blame for its one-sided thing-intuition, and that an absolute, divine intellect could intuit a thing adequately, in absolute selfhood instead of through appearances, as we intuit a sense datum. That is countersensical, contrary to the essence of the relation between thing and thing-consciousness, as countersensical as saying that God, being omnipotent, could also see as true what we see as false, that 2 × 2 = 5. The argument is a contraposition: if a thing were adequately givable it would be immanent; it is not immanent; so it is not adequately givable, for any intellect. The arithmetical comparison classifies the divine-intuition hypothesis as a violation of essence, beyond even divine power. Ideen I §43 contains the same argument and uses the same rhetorical move against "God."

But the peculiar character of the transcendence of things must be kept in view, since there is transcendence to consciousness in various senses (104.28–105.17). My own past experiences are in a way transcendent to me: they are conscious in memory but not real pieces of present remembering consciousness. But the past was itself consciousness, and if the memory is clear, it does not contain the past but reproduces its essence, in principle adequately. The same holds for another's consciousness, given in empathy: individually it is transcendent to the presentifying act, but it is consciousness, and in clear empathy its essence comes to adequate givenness. Here the transcendent is of the same categorial essence as the consciousness that grasps it. A real thing, and any reality in the specific sense, is by its essence transcendent to consciousness; it can never be adequately perceived, remembered, or intuited. And this shows that the mode of being of the real is in principle, in essence, different from that of consciousness: fundamentally different modes of being correspond to fundamentally different modes of givenness. A note: "adequate" does not at first mean "evident" or "indubitable"; how they relate needs a separate consideration. The distinction between individual transcendence and essential transcendence is the precise result of the section. Past and alien experiences transcend the present act individually but not in kind, and their essence can be adequately reproduced; things transcend consciousness in kind. Ideen I §§38 and 42 draw the same line, and the axiom that modes of givenness correspond to modes of being is the principle on which the whole fundamental consideration rests. The note separating adequacy from indubitability anticipates Ideen I §§46 and 138, where immanent givenness is indubitable but the whole stream is never adequately given.

To think that consciousness could adumbrate itself, could appear as a thing appears in infinitely many possible adumbrations, is countersense (105.18–29). Consciousness can be more or less clearly, more or less perfectly conscious, but it does not appear. Givenness through appearance has nothing to do with clarity or unclarity. If we think a real thing given in ideal clarity, we arrive at clear appearances passing into one another in endless manifolds, presenting the same thing from ever new sides, but we never get beyond presentation by sides, beyond "appearances" in the specific sense; this is an unconditionally necessary state of affairs. The sentence "an experience does not adumbrate itself" is the central claim of Ideen I §42. Husserl distinguishes two scales that are often run together: clarity (degrees of intuitive fullness) and adequacy (whether givenness is by adumbration at all). A thing can be clear but never adequate; an experience can be obscure but is never adumbrated.

### § 6. Critique of the Image and Sign Theory

What has been most misleading, and has hidden the fundamental contrast between being as consciousness and being transcendent to consciousness, the being of a real world, is the thoroughly erroneous image and sign theory (106.2–16). It suggests itself already in a child's first reflections on consciousness and reality, and its motives seduce even in our age of highly developed science. What could be more obvious than to say: outside are the things, outside my consciousness; if my perceptions inform me of things, how else than that images or signs appear in my consciousness as representatives of external things, which I refer to the things themselves? The section corresponds to Ideen I §43 and develops the appendix on image theory in the fifth Logical Investigation. Its placement is diagnostic: the sign theory is not one error among others but the error that hides the difference in modes of being just established.

This theory is not only false but a complete countersense (106.17–31). What does "images or signs appear in my consciousness" mean? Are these new things, like photographs, casts, paintings of external things, only inside my consciousness? One pictures consciousness as a box containing objects resembling the external ones, forgetting to ask how to conceive the ego that grasps the images as images of external things. The ego is not a little man in the box who looks at the pictures and occasionally crawls out to compare the things outside with those inside. For this image-seeing ego the image would itself be an outside, for which an image inside its consciousness would have to be sought, and so ad infinitum. The homunculus regress is the first of two arguments. It shows that the image theory explains the perception of things by the perception of images, which is the same problem again. Ideen I §90 uses the same regress against the image theory.

Further, an image is not a thing that has, besides shape, size, and colour, the quality "image"; nor is being a sign a quality of the sign (106.32–107.11). The image, one says, resembles the thing, and like represents like. But mere resemblance does not do it: a forest has countless similar trees, and none is an image of another. The emphasis must lie on representation: the image is grasped in consciousness as image of something else. And for this the question of existence is irrelevant. Something appearing does not just appear, even if a fiction, but is apperceived in a new way, in image-consciousness. Likewise for signs: what appears perceptually can be taken as a sign, a field as a sign of nearby settlements. If these modes of apprehension fall away, we have simple perceptions or presentations. The second argument shows that being-an-image is a function conferred by a specific intentional act, image-consciousness, and not a property of the image-thing. So the image theory presupposes intentionality instead of explaining it.

These simple perceptions are presupposed for image- or sign-consciousness to be established (107.12–31). So it is countersensical to try to explain simple perceiving or presenting itself by these mediate kinds of consciousness, which would again lead to infinite regress. In perception we do not have an image before us but the thing itself; that is the proper sense of perception, its essence: to make an object conscious in the mode of bodily selfhood. But it also belongs to its essence that, as thing-perception, it brings the bodily present object only to appearance, and that the appearance refers to manifolds of appearances in which the same object presents itself from different sides. It is decisively important to see that "appearance" is something peculiar, to be understood and described in immanent intuition, and that reifying it in the form of the image and sign theory is countersense. "In the appearance something appears" does not mean that what is given in appearance is an image or sign of something else, perhaps a mystical, inaccessible in-itself.
