The conclusion has two halves, and both are needed for the idealist thesis. Perception gives the thing itself, not a proxy, so there is no hidden original behind it. Perception gives it only through appearances, so the thing is not immanent. "Appearance" thus names a mode of givenness, not a second object. This is the formula of Ideen I §43 ("in perception the thing itself is given") together with §44 ("it is given only by adumbration"), and its corollary, that no mystical in-itself is required, is the premise from which §§7–9 will derive the dependence of reality on consciousness.

### § 7. The Mode of Being of Experiences of Consciousness and the Mode of Being of Real Things

The fundamental difference between the mode of being of experiences themselves, of everything called real stock of consciousness, and that of the real things announcing themselves in experiences must be kept firmly in view (108.3–31). Things appear, present themselves through adumbrating contents and apprehensions, always from different sides, always and in principle inadequately. Experiences do not appear; that makes no sense. They are given "absolutely"; they do not merely announce themselves, and we are not driven from announcement to announcement. Experiences are not always perceived, but they are in principle perceivable, in a distinctive way: the grasping gaze strikes them quite differently from the way it strikes what merely appears. The perceptual grasping of the immanent, this absolute grasping, necessarily guarantees the existence of its object. The singular being of the experience struck by the reflective gaze is in principle not negatable; it would be countersense to hold it possible that something so given does not truly exist. My stream may be unknown and dark to a great extent, especially in its past and future parts, but what I grasp of it in immanent perception and experience I have grasped absolutely, with the indubitability of the Cartesian cogito, and doubtless more: the being of the dark, non-intuitive parts of the single experience (retention), and so of the whole stream. The section restates Ideen I §§44–46 and extends the indubitability of reflection, cautiously, to the retained and the obscure. The phrase "we are not driven from announcement to announcement" condenses the infinite deferral that characterizes thing-givenness and is absent from immanent givenness.

Every stream of experience has its own essential form of immanent "time," with a stock of the actual Now, an ever-flowing Now, and with it a horizon of pasts of the form "just was an experience," the "before" that has behind it an earlier before, and likewise a horizon of future (108.32–109.6). And the absolute possibility of a reflective gaze belongs to the stream's essence, an act of immanently perceiving cogito that grasps and posits, from the given, however flowing, a continuously streaming experience as absolutely existing and as just having been. The analysis of immanent time is from Hua X and reappears in Ideen I §§81–82. The "absolute possibility" of reflection is what makes the stream a self-guaranteeing domain: whatever belongs to it can in principle be brought under absolute reflection. Ideen I §45 states the same: an experience is never perceived "accidentally," but its readiness for perception belongs to its mode of being.

Against this we speak of an existing world of things with its form of objective time (109.7–110.9). It too is given and perceivable, but however perfect the perception, it is in principle not adequate, not grasping absolutely without reserve, not positing an existence indubitably. Real being, being through appearances, adumbrations, and analogous announcements, excludes absolutely indubitable positing of existence by its mode of givenness, and this a priori, by the essence of the real, not by contingent circumstances. The mode of givenness of consciousness excludes non-being; that of "real" being, on the contrary, never in principle excludes the possibility that the perceived does not exist, though it stands there as bodily existing. The existence of what stands there "bodily" in thing-perception is in principle "only a pretension," a legitimate one, since perception supplies an originary ground of right for the statement that the thing is and has the appearing properties. But whether this legitimate pretension of being keeps its right and is further strengthened depends on the further course and whole connection of experience, if further experience, and first the harmonious continuation of perception, constantly motivates positing the thing's being and being-so. The harmonious course is not the only one prescribed as essential possibility by the initial perception; the possibility of a transition from harmony to discord also belongs to perception's essence, as when further experience shows that the perceived is not, that the perception was illusory or hallucinatory. It is always essentially possible that the appearance, the current apprehension of the thing, flips over into another incompatible with it, which claims and wins a stronger right. All such occurrences belong only to transcendent perception, perception of the real; they make no sense for immanent perception of pure experiences. "Pretension" is the 1915 word for what Ideen I §46 calls the "presumptive" being of the thing and §138 its "legitimate but defeasible" positing. The flip of apprehension (Umschlag der Auffassung) is the mechanism of cancellation described in §4b, now named as a possibility essential to transcendent perception and impossible for immanent perception. Ideen I §46 rests the whole contrast between necessary and contingent being on this asymmetry of possible cancellation.

Applying this to the whole real world, which owes the right of its positing ultimately to transcendent perception alone, and to the whole of consciousness, the stream to which perceptions and other ego-experiences belong, "we must unfortunately say: the whole world is merely presumptive actuality; consciousness, however, is given as absolute and as the only absolutely indubitable" (110.10–22). This concerns not only consciousness but also the ego, taken exactly as subject of the cogito. Every cogito can be posited in absolute thesis, simply uncancellable, and absolute positing extends to everything necessarily belonging to it, including the subject purely as subject of this cogito. The formula is Ideen I §46's: the thesis of the world is "contingent," the thesis of my pure ego and its life "necessary, simply indubitable." The word "unfortunately" is a rhetorical concession to the natural attitude, which hears the result as a demotion of the world. The inclusion of the pure ego as subject of the cogito matches Ideen I §57, where the pure ego survives the reduction because it belongs necessarily to every cogitatio.

### § 8. Statements about Reality as Statements about Consciousness

Consider the presumptive actuality of the world (110.25–111.25). We say "the world" and constantly posit it in the form of the unspoken general thesis. This thesis is contingent and, despite its inherent right, cancellable; the right is relative, in principle capable of being counterbalanced and outweighed. Not that we advocate skepticism, deny the world, or say its existence is doubtful and should not be trusted: that would presuppose rational motives against the world's existence, of which there is no question. In particular cases we meet serious counter-motives against the existence of what is perceived, illusions and hallucinations, but these dissolve so as to confirm the truth of the world's existence; every illusion has its "explanation" on the ground of the natural apperception and positing of the world. On the whole the unity of experience closes harmoniously, and the force of positive rational motives is so immense that one cannot speak of any weight of negative instances. What interests us is not the weighing but the principled possibility of doubt, which is something quite different from doubtfulness in the ordinary sense, the claim that weighty counter-motives exist. A doubt is thinkable, or equivalently it is in principle thinkable, a priori grounded in the essence of the world as given through experience and appearances, that the whole world does not exist, while the whole field of the current cogito, hence the existence of consciousness as consciousness, stands absolutely and is presupposed by every possible doubt, even one posited as valid. The being of consciousness is in principle uncancellable; it is necessary being. The being of the world is contingent; there need be no world at all. The distinction between principled possibility of doubt and actual doubtfulness is the safeguard against reading the fundamental consideration as skeptical. Ideen I §46 makes it in the same terms, and §31 separates the epoché from Cartesian doubt for the same reason: the possibility of the world's non-being is an essential possibility, not a reasonable suspicion. The claim that each illusion "confirms" the world, since it is explained within the world, anticipates §10's "higher harmony." The last two sentences are the modal formula of §46 and §49: consciousness is necessary, the world contingent.

Further: the world is actual and really constituted as we know it (111.26–36). This contains a statement about the course of consciousness: the course of actual experiences is such that not only a transcendent world of things appears in them, but a thinking that follows experiential motives and proceeds rationally can carry out theoretical positings, descriptions, and explanations of the world according to which the appearing world can be maintained as harmoniously and rationally existing, objectively determinable thus and so. The section's title thesis is now stated. Every statement about the world's actuality translates into a statement about how consciousness runs. This is the equivalence of Text No. 2 (29), now carefully phrased so as not to identify the world with consciousness: the statement about the world contains a statement about consciousness, not the other way round.

But this is not a necessary state of affairs (111.37–112.24). The world could be quite different from what it actually is, which again says something about the course of consciousness: the connections of experiencing and scientifically elaborating consciousness could be quite different, so that the world would have to be determined quite differently before the forum of reason. By modifications we carry out fictively on the course of experiencing consciousness, we can devise manifold possibilities of other worlds, "as correlates of essentially possible modifications of the idea of experiencing consciousness in general." We can picture how experience would run if physical laws were quite different, if things changed all their physical properties in an orderly way so that the whole world got a different physical face. We can imagine that no physical lawfulness obtained that could be grasped in mathematically exact form, while thing-appearances and thing-positings occurred in the familiar form, with connections and motivations changed to fit; that is again a correlative statement about consciousness. Finally we see that the world need not exist at all, which again says something about consciousness of the world: "all experiential legitimations turn, in consciousness, into rejections." The phrase about "correlates of essentially possible modifications of the idea of experiencing consciousness" is nearly verbatim in Ideen I §47, which speaks of possible worlds and "non-worlds" as correlates of such variants. The three steps of variation, a different physics, no exact physics, no world, are the three grades of Ideen I §§47–49: other possible worlds, a world of experience without exact natural science, and the dissolution of all world into a "tumult" of sensations. The translation of non-existence into "all legitimations become rejections" is the decisive phenomenological rendering of world-annihilation: it is a possible course of consciousness, not an event in the world.

### § 9. The Dependence of Reality on an Actual Ego

Consider the relation between possibilities of being and non-being of the world and of the consciousness of it, hence of the experiencing subject (112.27–113.4). What appears, in the unlimited variation of phantasy, as an a priori necessary frame for all modifications? We imagine a thing and let it vary freely; the fiction is our momentary state, but that does not matter. That I am now in this room, carrying out this shared reflection with you and performing the fiction, has nothing to do with the essence of the imagined thing or of a possible thing in general. But let us see whether an ego-relation, if a different one, is not after all necessarily interwoven with the essence of thing. The heading states the special thesis of 1908 in egological form. The method is free variation, and the question is whether the variant always retains a relation to an ego. The factual ego of the lecturer, in a Göttingen lecture room, is excluded as irrelevant; what is sought is an ego-relation that belongs to the essence of the thing.

The fiction imagines a thing as existing, and at first not only as existing but as perceived (113.5–26). I cannot present a thing intuitively without presenting it in some orientation, from some "side," and this side it turns "to me," but not to the actually living ego in this room. The imagined water nymph does not actually face me; she does not turn her lovely face and then proudly her back to this factual ego. What turns toward me in sides and orientations are the actual things of my actual surroundings. One might say: I am involved insofar as I imagine myself into the phantasy space, the landscape in which the nymph appears. That may be; but then I give up my actual existence and am no longer the actual ego, just as when I live myself wholly into another's feeling and thinking I am no longer the actual ego and what is actually posited is the other ego. So I cannot imagine a thing without having imagined it as perceived by a perceiving ego, and if I imagine myself in that place, I am a possible ego, not the actual one. The analysis separates the actual ego from the quasi-ego of phantasy. The phantasy-thing is correlated with a phantasy-perceiver, who is a modification of me, as the empathized ego is another. Ideen I §§111–112 describe phantasy as a "neutrality modification" in which the imagining ego posits a quasi-perceiving ego; the lecture draws from this the consequence that fictive things correlate with fictive egos, as Text No. 5 (75–76) argued.

One will say that this is found whenever we consider possibilities: whenever we make a possible object intuitive, even an ideal one like a number, an intuiting ego is involved, which means only that everything possible is intuitable for a possible intuiting ego, a possible originarily intuiting ego (113.27–36). That is undoubtedly correct and expresses no empty or trivial insight. But what matters here is that the situation for things is essentially different. Husserl grants the general correlation with possible consciousness and reserves the special thesis for things. This is the structure of Text No. 5 (73–74) and of the 1908 distinction between truths of essence and truths of fact.

The ideal being of a number says only that a number can possibly be seen, that it must be able to come to givenness for a possible conscious ego; this does not imply that any subject actually exists (113.37–114.22). The being of a real thing, considered in ideal possibility, implies much more: "that a thing cannot be unless, in relation to it, an actual ego really exists for which this thing could become perceived." "Every thing lies a priori in the surroundings of an actual ego." As every actual thing lies in my actual surroundings, so every conceivable thing, even if I imagine that this actual world does not exist, lies in a conceivable world and belongs with it to the surroundings of an ego that would have to exist together with this thing. "A real world is what it is only as surroundings." The real world can be varied in many ways, finally so that it loses all reality and cannot sustain its sense as identical unity, as totality of identical things; it is something contingent. But if it is, it is the surroundings of one or many actual egos. And if it is not, if it is annihilated, the ego cannot thereby be annihilated. "Ego-existence is in a certain way the primary, the necessary existence, on which the existence of realities depends as something dependent and contingent." The concept of "surroundings" (Umgebung) gives the special thesis its 1915 form: to be real is to belong to the environment of an actual ego, actually or within its horizon. This is the "surrounding world" of Ideen I §27 turned into an ontological criterion. The asymmetry of annihilation follows: the world may cease to be surroundings without the ego ceasing to be. Ideen I §49 states the same conclusion without the term "surroundings," which reappears as "Umwelt" in Ideen II.

These are dark words, and we must go deeper (114.23–36). It belongs first to the essence of a thing that it can in principle be perceived. If I make clear any example of a thing, it is either perceived or remembered or stands before me in imagination, which means I carry out the fiction of a perceiving in which it is the perceived. Rising to eidetic consciousness, I see that an identical thing, the imagined nymph for example, can in principle be experienced only as the unity of a certain perceptual connection, an infinite, definitely structured manifold of possible experiences. You will object: possible experiences; that implies no necessity of the existence of an ego who actually perceives or to whose surroundings the thing belongs. Let us look more closely. Husserl acknowledges that the result so far is only the general correlation with possible experience, and introduces the realist objection of Texts No. 1 and 3 into the lecture hall. The next step must show that the possibility is real, not empty.

Suppose a thing exists (114.37–115.16). Is the possibility of perceiving it an empty possibility, or what kind of possibility is it? The things I actually see I need not see as I see them; I see them from this side, could as well see them from behind, and need not see them at all; they could be outside my visual field and every sense field. So every determinate perception has something contingent, as does every determinate perceptual connection, such as the continuous series of visual appearances I get in walking round the thing. But if the thing is outside my perceptual field, it is still within my surroundings. That means: I can move, or think myself moved, suitably in infinite space, and would then come into a situation in which the thing was in my perceptual field. Every actual perceptual field is limited, but each is a member of an ideal continuity of perceptual fields that finally leads to ones in which the thing would be actually experienced. The contingency of particular perceptions is distinguished from the necessity of belonging to the continuum of possible fields that starts from my actual one. Unperceived things are real because they lie on paths of possible motion from my actual position; this is the kinaesthetic concretion of "motivated possibility." Ideen I §47 compresses it in the remark that the unperceived things of the world are in the horizon of my actual experience.

You will not object that I cannot go out into airless space, since I would suffocate or my senses would fail (115.17–32). We have not been considering physical possibilities but what belongs to the ideal possibility of a thing's existence and, equivalently, to the ideal possibility of legitimating this existence. And we said: not every actual thing is actually experienced by me; but if I am to assert rationally that it exists, it must be in the range of my possible experience. This does not mean that I must empirically have the unimpeded ability to carry out the experience, but that a connection of possible experience exists, connected with my actual experience, such that in principle I have the possibility of justifying the being of the unknown thing from experiential motives within this connection. The clarification forestalls a naturalistic misreading. "Possible experience" here is an eidetic notion, the continuation of my experience by motivated steps, not a physiological capacity. The real possibility is anchored in actuality (my actual experience) but its extent is ideal: it reaches to the stars, though no human body can.

The unknown thing would have to be in the one infinite space that is distinguished as real space by the things I actually perceive: within their infinite, open and undetermined spatial-thingly horizon, in the realm of determinability that would turn not a fictively possible but a really possible experience into a realm of determinacy (115.33–116.9). Everything that is, in the manner of thingly reality, is within the prescribed spatial and temporal horizon, and this horizon means not empty but real possibilities. On both sides a fixed order is prescribed: of spatial appearances, in which one spatially formed and oriented existence adumbrates itself to infinity, and of temporal appearances, in which temporal order announces itself in consciousness. Real space is defined indexically, as the space of the things I perceive, and every real thing must belong to it. This is the conclusion Ideen I §48 draws: a world "outside our world," not in the horizon of our space and time, is a countersense. The section thus completes the special thesis: reality requires an actual ego, and it requires that the real lie in the one space and time opened by that ego's actual experience.

### § 10. The World and Its Legitimation in a Connection of Experience

One might find this unconvincing and object (116.12–32): the last consideration spoke of my ego, but my existence is contingent; the world could be without me. And it spoke of the legitimability of the world for me, this contingent ego. Of course, to legitimate the existence of realities I must experience them directly or start from my ground of experience and connect the unexperienced to the experienced by inferences of experience; so the legitimable world for me may be a unity of possible experience enclosed in one spatiotemporal horizon, that of my actually experienced things. "But what does the world itself care about my possible legitimation?" It happens that a piece of world lies within the range of experience; but much, indeed whole worlds, may be in themselves without being legitimable. Outside the spatiotemporal experienceable world there may be non-spatial, non-temporal worlds, which are in themselves while no lines of possible experience lead into my actual range, and no inference of experience offers bridges for rational thought to cross. This is the objection Ideen I §48 meets: worlds outside ours. The objection has two parts: the contingency of my ego and the possibility of worlds beyond all possible experience. The lecture answers the second first and defers the first to §12.

The answer: leave aside for now the contingency of my ego and treat it as an untouched fact (116.33–117.24). Leave all facts; suspend the positing of the given world and of our experiencing ego with its experiences. Consider without limit: what lies in the assumption of an existing world? We can only say that if it is to be a rational assumption, a rational possibility, it must be possible to see its rationality. "The idea of something existing in general is correlative to the idea of a legitimation of the existence of this same thing." So we come to the ideal possibility of legitimating the assumed world. Since "world" is a title for transcendent things, and the consciousness that gives and finally legitimates them has the character of experiencing through transcendent perceptions, we come necessarily to the idea of an ego with certain connections of experience in which this world and no other appears and is experienced as actual. "There is possibly a world" means correlatively "there is possibly an ego with a quite determinate stream of consciousness, one that would actually contain the corresponding experiences." If we stop at this kind of possibility, it gives us the necessary correlation between each determinate world and some determinate ego and experiencing consciousness, but not a real possibility. The reduction is now explicitly performed: the world's positing and the factual ego's positing are both suspended, and the question is posed eidetically. The result, the correlation between each possible world and a possible ego, is the general thesis, which the realist's own objection presupposes: worlds "in themselves" would still have to be legitimable by some possible consciousness, or they would be nonsense.

More precisely, we have established a general correlation between two general ideas, "world and corresponding experiencing ego" (117.25–118.5). But there are infinitely many particular forms of the idea "world," an infinity of possibilities of transcendent manifolds of things that can form a world, and correlatively infinitely many possible experiencing egos. These are all equally possible and evidently incompatible with each other. Fix an ego with its stream and a stream of actual experiences in it: actual perceptions, memories, and so on. In these determinate things appear and are posited doxically, precisely these and no others stand there bodily, and memory posits precisely these past events. All these experiences have their actual theses with an actual content of sense, and "what they posit as actual excludes infinitely many other possibilities of being as groundless," as contrary to experience, as conflicting with actual experience. The selection argument of Text No. 5 is restated in its eidetic form. The equally possible worlds correspond to equally possible egos; actuality of experience is what makes one world real and the others groundless.

The actual things of experience, as they appear and are posited, carry motivations of experience; from them radiate references forward and back, horizons of presumptions of being that are no longer empty possibilities but "rational possibilities that give experiential grounds" (118.6–33). Every experience evidently has a right in itself. If I see a table, I have a right to say "there is a table," the most self-evident right, a primal right (Urrecht), since I see the table itself in person. This is no accident but an essential state of affairs: experience has experiential right. Perception has a primary and pre-eminent ground of right for what is properly experienced, the determinations seen in the primary and proper sense; but a secondary right, still a right, reaches further. How the back looks is not given in one-sided perception, but perception motivates in itself the assumption of a certain back, imperfectly determined and not intuitively given: the surface continues; there is an interior. However undetermined, this is a determinable indeterminacy, co-posited, and has its claim of right, not empty, with its force, though as in all experience a force that may be outweighed. The co-positing contains directions for new, possible and motivated series of perceptions that, if actualized, would confirm, strengthen, and determine the imperfectly determined. The doctrine of the "primal right" of perception and the "secondary right" of the co-meant is the theory of rational positing in Ideen I §§136–138 and 140 applied to the horizon. Its function in the argument is to show that real possibilities are possibilities with a ground of right. Possibility motivated by the horizon of an actual perception has rational weight; logical possibility has none.

There is always the possibility that the actual course of experience disappoints instead of confirming, so that determination otherwise results instead of closer determination (118.34–119.8). But if we presuppose that the experienced reality and world actually exist, these disappointments can run only in a style that allows the identity of the appearing and posited world to be maintained. Conflicts in particular cases resolve in a higher harmony: the world determines itself otherwise than the initial experiences claimed, but as one identical and harmonious world, which after manifold corrections shows a content that demands just such a course of experience, one in which semblance in particular cases announces the higher truth. The "higher harmony" is the phenomenological definition of the world's actuality: not the absence of conflict, but a style of experience in which every conflict is corrected within the unity of one world. Ideen I §§138 and 151 and the end of §47 describe the world's actuality in the same way: the "harmonious" course of experience is what "the world is actual" means. Correlatively, Ideen I §49's annihilated world is a course of experience in which conflicts no longer resolve.

So "a determinate ego with a determinate stream of experience belongs to the assumption of a determinate existing world," and this stream excludes rather than includes the possibility of other worlds (119.9–32). A determinate ego can imagine those other worlds in its connection of experience, but then it imagines them, either by building harmonious connections of possible experience in fiction, or by imagining changes into its actual experience, but in contradiction with the theses of actual experience, just as when we imagine a Bacchic procession with satyrs and fabulous beasts into the Weender Strasse (the editor notes: a street in Göttingen). Only actual experience can motivate rationally. Real being and real possibility are always prescribed only by the stock of actual experience, which excludes what deviates from it as real impossibility. Mere fiction yields not real but merely ideal possibility, and a merely ideal possibility, so long as no actual experience extends its thesis to it, is groundless, like the existence of satyrs or nymphs. "So if one says that a world could exist without an ego that experiences it existing, that is nonsense," for the truth "a world can exist" is nothing without grounding in principle, and this groundability presupposes an actual ego related thetically to this world. The Göttingen example makes the point concrete for the audience: an imagined procession on the town's main street is a possibility, but one excluded by actual experience, and a world in itself without an experiencing ego would be on the same footing, a fiction without rational ground. The final sentence derives the special thesis from the theory of reason: the statement "a world can exist" is a truth only if groundable, and groundability requires an actual ego. Ideen I §48 reaches the same conclusion by the same route, calling such a world a "material countersense" while granting its logical possibility.

### § 11. The Difference between Ideal and Real Being with Respect to a Legitimating Ego

Contrast the mathematical sphere of being, the ideal number series and ideal manifolds, in correlation with a mathematizing ego, with the real sphere of being, the world, in correlation with the experiencing ego (120.3–16). Mathematical existences and truths have their correlate in possible legitimations, possible mathematical intuitions and deductive insights; so an ego must be possible that could carry them out. If I carry them out, I am involved with my actuality, but my actuality is extra-essential: "the mathematical 'world' does not demand my actual being." It stands in correlation only with the idea of an ego, a possible legitimating ego in general, and this possibility is ideal. The fact "I" is contingent. The section repeats the division of Texts No. 1 and 5. For ideal objects the correlation with consciousness is a correlation of ideas; the factual ego is irrelevant to the validity of mathematics. This keeps the idealist thesis from turning into psychologism about logic and mathematics.

It is different with a real world (120.17–121.3). Its legitimability presupposes a determinate possibly experiencing ego and determinate real possibilities of experience. That is a habitual stock of consciousness that cannot be attributed to every possible ego. To an ideally possible ego belongs ideally possible experience, a realm of empty possibilities that comprise all possible worlds equally and distinguish none. If I posit that a determinate world exists, this too happens in a positing ego, but that is not the point. If an ego posits "let there be a world," it may be (a clause Husserl later bracketed and marked with a question mark) that it finds no rational ground in itself to posit it as actual. But if it can present a world and considers what the ideal possibility of this world requires, it must co-posit a possible experiencing ego, capable of experience, posited together with this world as related to it, as its experiencing ego, to which it appears, however poorly. If the ego is to find rational grounds in itself for positing an actual world, it must itself be an experiencing ego, experiencing this world. The term "habitual stock" is notable: real possibility of experience is a stock of motivations the ego has acquired through actual experience, not a capacity of any ego whatever. This links the special thesis to habituality, the theme of the ego as substrate of habitualities in the Cartesian Meditations (§32). The bracketed clause, with its question mark, records Husserl's hesitation over whether an ego can posit a world hypothetically without any rational ground; the main argument does not need the clause.

These essential necessities also find expression in this: "a real world belongs necessarily as intentional correlate to a real ego, or to its real stream of consciousness." A world is what it is only as the counterpart of an experiencing ego; everything it contains is either directly experienced or belongs to the determinable horizon of indeterminacy of what is actually experienced (121.4–10). Beside this sentence the editor reports a question mark and the marginal note "jellyfish." The thesis is the summary of §§9–11 and corresponds to the sentence of Ideen I §49 that the world is "nothing more than" the correlate of a consciousness bound by rules of motivation. The note "jellyfish" appears to record a later objection to his own formula. If any experiencing subject anchors the world, a creature with the most primitive sensibility, a jellyfish, could be the actual ego around which the world is "surroundings"; then the special thesis would be satisfied by minimal animal consciousness, which seems to trivialize it, or it would require that the experienced world be the world of that creature's sensibility, which seems absurd for the world of physics. The question of which subjects count, and how the objective world relates to the restricted surroundings of animals, is taken up in Text No. 7 and in Hua XV's texts on animal and "abnormal" subjects.

### § 12. Empathy as Condition of the Possibility of One and the Same World for Several Egos

The previous results yield the following (121.13–29); the editor notes that Husserl later wrote a large question mark over the paragraph. On the usual view egos are contingent occurrences in the world, which could exist even if all egos were struck from it. There may be some truth in this, insofar as no human or animal need exist in the world we know as actual (though that is already a change of the world). And yet it follows a priori from our consideration that if we thought all egos struck, not from the world but from the totality of individual being, the world, the totality of transcendent realities, would be struck too. We shall have to consider what kind of egos these are, since they are not to be humans and animals. We already see that what counts first is the thesis of experiencing consciousness, thinkable only in the unity of a stream, and that this actual stream cannot be struck, nor the ego insofar as it is the pure ego required by it. Two strikings are distinguished: striking egos from the world, which leaves a world without animals, and striking them from the totality of individual being, which strikes the world too. The second presupposes that the egos at issue are not worldly beings, and the paragraph announces the distinction between pure and empirical ego that §§13–14 carry out. The later question mark records Husserl's dissatisfaction with an argument that names the pure ego before it has been secured, and perhaps with the implication that the world needs only the pure ego and not embodied subjects.

Having proved the necessary dependence of the thesis of an individually actual world on the actual existence of an individual ego, a question arises (122.1–9). The same world could appear to several egos, be legitimable by experiences in several streams; but different egos could also experience quite different worlds. Is it not possible that outside the world I experience there are worlds not in my surroundings, which I could not in principle experience? The question returns to §10's objection in intersubjective form: if other egos exist, their worlds might be inaccessible to me, so the special thesis would hold relative to each ego without excluding worlds beyond mine.

Consider first that we all assume the possibility of one and the same world for several egos, announced in all of them by experience (122.10–123.3). What does it presuppose? If it is to be a true possibility, and first the coexistence of a plurality of egos, it must be legitimable. A unity of grounding must be possible that recognizes a plurality of egos and, with reference to their separate connections of experience, recognizes that the things posited in experience everywhere are the same or belong to one world. That presupposes the possibility of an ego that recognizes other egos and their streams. There is in principle only one possibility for one ego's knowing another, for knowing consciousness as foreign within one's own consciousness: what we call "empathy," which must therefore have the character of an originally experiencing act carrying original right. Foreign consciousness, which does not fit into the form of my time-consciousness and stream, which I cannot immanently perceive, remember, or intuitively expect, I can posit only with a different kind of motive; and the only possible motive is that something experienced as a thing, something transcendent, indirectly motivates by way of empathy the positing of a consciousness that is presentified yet not remembered or expected. The legitimating ego must therefore be actually experiencing, having the other egos over against it. If we have a plurality of egos and streams in relation of possible empathy, the possibility arises that one and the same world constitutes itself for all, and conversely the same world represents the possibility of their constant mutual understanding. The structure of the argument is the special thesis applied to other egos. Other consciousnesses are real only if legitimable, legitimable only through empathy, and empathy is motivated only by the perception of bodies in the experiencing ego's world. So the plurality of egos presupposes one ego's actual experience of a world in which other bodies appear, and the common world and the community of egos are correlates. This is the argument of Ideen I §§29 and 48 in its expanded form; the claim that empathy must be an "originally experiencing" act anticipates the Cartesian Meditations' theory of appresentation. Note how this lecture already contradicts Husserl's later complaint (Text No. 5, 78, note) that the 1915 course forgot intersubjectivity; what it lacks is not intersubjectivity but its role in constituting the objectivity of the world itself.

The same question is posed for separate egos with separate, wholly different worlds (123.4–34). Turning again to the ideal possibility of legitimating this possibility: if it is to hold, the assumption of a plurality of egos presupposes, as shown, the real possibility of empathy and so a unified world of appearance for all. One might add that each ego could still have its own appearances, closing into a world of its own. But the legitimating ego must belong to the ego-connection, and all possible experiences and appearances of the foreign egos must be accessible to it through possible empathy. Everything the foreign egos posit, like the foreign egos themselves, is motivated as to its existence by the legitimating ego's own actual course of experience, including its course of empathy. The ego will call the actual, real "world" everything it is motivated to posit as transcendent reality through its own experiencing consciousness, not only what appears to it directly but also what appears to others and what it is inclined to posit indirectly on the basis of its primary appearances, "just as by 'world' we understand not what we ourselves see but what we all together, exchanging our experiences, establish empirically." "In the wider sense, then, everything real is ultimately the surroundings of every ego." If there are worlds at all, they are connected worlds, joining into the unity of one world; and this unity relates to every actual ego, just as the totality of egos is not a disconnected collective but a connected unity, connected through possibilities of mutual understanding (within infinite time, perhaps through tradition). The conclusion of the 1915 course is the one world for a community of egos. Separate worlds are excluded because any ego that could know of them would have to reach them through empathy, which already places them in its world. The clause about tradition extends the community through time, a theme of Husserl's late work on generativity. Ideen I §48's conclusion, that a world outside ours would have to be in principle experienceable by us through empathic connection with its experiencers, and so would not be outside ours, is the same.

### § 13. Transcendental Consciousness as Absolute Being

Various considerations follow (124.3–31). First one must be fully imbued with the sense of the eidetic insights obtained and ask what they contain, despite the paradox we feel vividly. Intuition tells us that consciousness, with all its real formations and the stock of appearances, meanings, and thoughts essentially belonging to it, is an absolute being, which the striking out of the whole real world would affect only in the sequence of its formations, not in its existence. "Affected in the sequence of formations": if the world exists, a rule is prescribed for the connection of consciousness in which it is to announce itself; so a rule is prescribed if it does not exist: all appearances and connections that would rationally legitimate a world are excluded. But other connections are not excluded, so non-existence of the world never means non-existence of consciousness. Recall too that the thesis of consciousness, performed in every glance of immanent perception, is uncancellable, absolutely justified, excluding any possibility of non-being. The thesis of the world, performed in every transcendent or "external" perception, is rational but in principle cancellable. The world need not be. (And if it is not, the last argument shows, the existence of consciousness is in no way affected; that such an argument must be possible is already obvious from the contrast between the necessity of consciousness's existence and the contingency of every real existence.) The section title and the first sentence are the title and thesis of Ideen I §49 ("Absolute consciousness as residuum of world-annihilation"), and the formula "affected in the sequence of formations, not in existence" reappears there almost verbatim: annihilation of the world would "modify" consciousness but leave its "own existence untouched." The account of what is modified is exact: the world's non-existence excludes the world-legitimating connections, so it is itself a rule for consciousness, just as the world's existence is.

Following the insight faithfully, a clean separation results between pure, or better transcendental, consciousness and empirical or psychological consciousness (124.32–125.8). The consciousness whose absoluteness emerged against the relativity of world-existence is certainly consciousness, a title for perceptions, memories, phantasies, judgments, feelings, with all that their essence demands really and intentionally, but it cannot be consciousness in the psychological sense. The natural confusion of the two, which has its essential grounds, explains the paradox of our results and why the ways of genuine transcendental philosophy have always been missed. The term "transcendental consciousness" now replaces "pure consciousness." The diagnosis of the paradox is the key to Ideen I's presentation: the thesis that consciousness is absolute sounds absurd if consciousness means the minds of animals, which are obviously dependent on nature. Ideen I §§53–54 dissolve the paradox in the same way, and the claim that its source is a confusion with "essential grounds" anticipates the Crisis's account of the transcendental naivety of psychology (§§56–58).

### § 14. The Phenomenological Reduction and the Separation of Transcendental and Psychological Consciousness

The split in the idea of consciousness must now be made fully clear (125.12–25). We perform the phenomenological reduction: we bracket the whole existence of the world and retain the sphere of immanence. The stream itself, with everything meant in it as such, appearing as such, thought as such, is not affected by this exclusion. The experienced thing in its existence falls under the exclusion; but the experiencing, and its essential property of being experience of the thing, does not. We are fully reassured that we can perform this exclusion, since we have seen that the positing of consciousness itself with its whole essential stock requires no world-existence, which could itself be struck out. The order of the lecture is instructive for Ideen I. The reduction is introduced only after the absoluteness of consciousness has been shown, and its legitimacy is justified by that result: we can bracket the world without losing our field because consciousness does not depend on it. Ideen I has the same order. The epoché is sketched in §§31–32, but the reduction is carried out as method only in §50, after §49's proof of the absoluteness of consciousness, and §50 begins by drawing exactly this reassurance from §49. The idealist thesis is thus the ground of possibility of the reduction in both texts.

But a doubt comes (125.26–35). Is this perceiving, remembering, thinking, feeling, willing, which I posited in the thesis of consciousness, not itself part of the real world? It is my experiencing and thinking, and I am a member of the world. If I strike the whole world with all animal realities, all consciousness is struck too. How can our doctrine hold that consciousness is absolute if consciousness evidently enters into the relativity of world-existence? The objection is the one Ideen I §§33 and 53 anticipate. It is the most natural objection and the one that shows whether the reader has understood the distinction between the two kinds of consciousness.

Our answer: "the consciousness that is absolute is not the consciousness that enters into the world" (126.1–21). This is no construction to save our theses but an evident difference. I, the human being, have a body, and to the body belong a sensory and psychic life; to it belong I, the personality, with character traits, knowledge, habits of will. All these so-called psychic properties announce themselves in my psychic states, which I experience in psychological experience. I, the man called so-and-so, who in saying "I" understand and posit myself as the person known to me and others, ascribe to myself these perceptions, judgments, volitions, and in connection with them these sensory capacities, abilities, habitual directions of will. All this falls under the phenomenological reduction and does not enter the immanent consciousness that remains untouched by it. "I, the human being, am also a transcendence," and no less transcendent are my mental ego, my personal properties, and finally my experiences as experiences of this mental ego, if "experience" is taken in the natural sense. The decisive admission is that even my experiences, taken as states of my person, are transcendent. The difference between psychological and transcendental consciousness is not a difference in content but in apprehension: the same perceiving is either a state of a person in the world or a moment of the absolute stream.

One must note exactly that the perception, judgment, or act of will meant in the empirical sentence "I perceive," "I judge," "I will" is not identically the same as the perception or feeling retained in the reduction as belonging to the absolute stream (126.22–34). The former is consciousness as a state of a human being who is a member of psychophysical nature. The state belongs to the world; it is really tied to human personality and body and thus placed in spatiotemporal nature. But this real tie is not an external binding together of consciousness and natural real ego and body. The non-identity is the crucial claim: the psychological state is a constituted unity, the transcendental experience its constituting moment. The last sentence denies that psychophysical union is a mechanical attachment of two things; the next unit explains what it is.

The tie is itself one that shows and legitimates itself in consciousness (126.35–127.14). The human being with his character and everything psychic is something appearing, experienced, given in consciousness, and yet not something immanent to consciousness, really contained in it. Like nature in general, the human being appears or announces himself in a transcendent way, and this holds also for the human ego in psychological self-experience. The body-thing appears, and in unity with it, by virtue of a founded, transcending apperception, the mental subject belonging to it with its mental acts. If we exclude the transcendent, this apperception itself remains, that is, the pure consciousness in which the human being appears, in which I appear to myself as natural, perceive myself naturally, posit myself as natural reality. If I bracket natural reality, the consciousness of natural reality cannot be bracketed. This residue is pure consciousness. Psychological consciousness is defined as the correlate of a "founded transcending apperception": the experience is apprehended as the state of a soul that belongs to an appearing body. Ideen I §53 describes the same apperception, by which consciousness becomes a real component of nature through its connection with the body, and §54 draws the conclusion of this paragraph: transcendental consciousness is the residue in which the apperception itself is performed. The title of Ideen I §33's goal, "the residuum," is literally this "Bewusstseinsresiduum."

Starting from a merely physical thing like this table, bracketing yields the phenomenon: the experience of the table, the belief in its being, liking or disliking it (127.15–30). This residue is not straightforwardly psychological consciousness of the table, since after the exclusion, directing the reflective gaze on consciousness, I need not apprehend it as mine, the human being's, as my human state, as an act of my real soul or mind. I can do so, but then I can distinguish again the apprehension as human state and this human state itself. If I bracket the state as state of the human being in the world, I have not bracketed the apprehension as all that: I can grasp this apprehension purely, as I can apperceive it again as a human state, and so on. The iteration shows that the reduction cannot be escaped by psychologizing. Every psychological apprehension of an experience is itself an experience that can be taken purely, so transcendental consciousness always remains as the site of the apprehension. This is the formal reason Ideen I §54 can say that the psychological is a "transcendent" unity constituted in the absolute.

Two attitudes or apprehensions are therefore possible in principle (127.31–128.5). First, a transcendent attitude: naturally I experience the world and other humans in it, and myself, the human ego. To its reality belong, on this apprehension, psychological states and acts, "I perceive," "I judge." Psychology as the science of psychic reality knows only these acts, this "empirical consciousness," experienced and posited naturally as states of the real human being, just as the body with its states is experienced as nature, as the seen colour of a face is the colour of the real face. The first attitude is the natural attitude of Ideen I §27, including its psychological variant. The comparison with the colour of the face is precise: psychic states are given as properties of a real being in the same way as perceived colours are given as properties of a thing.

The second attitude is the phenomenological or transcendental (128.6–32). Its peculiarity is that it excludes every transcendence of nature, physical and psychic, and so all human states of consciousness; yet it retains consciousness, pure consciousness, the consciousness in which everything real appears, is thought, felt, willed, but which is not itself ascribed to nature, apperceived into nature. Such ascription presupposes consciousness, which then cannot again be consciousness in nature. "Consciousness in nature" is, one must always note, first not consciousness at all, and second not a thing stuck into nature as into a box, since the nature here spoken of, into which consciousness is placed, is appearing nature, and the placing is appearing placing. We understand that appearing nature appears by virtue of an appearing that is not itself what appears. Every new apprehension brings new absolute occurrences into the stream, occurrences in which relation to transcendence can establish itself without their being transcendent. Every absolute experience can undergo transcendent, psychological apperception, can be posited and apprehended as a human or animal state; but then the being-sense "pure consciousness," or the pure experience of it, undergoes a change by which it no longer counts for me with the being-sense "self-consciousness" in the transcendental sense and no longer belongs to the stream itself. The box image from §6 returns: consciousness is not in nature as an object in a container; its "being in nature" is itself an appearance, an apperceptive sense. The phrase "being-sense" (Seinssinn) marks the transition between attitudes as a change in the sense in which an experience counts, not in the experience as a reell event. Ideen I §§53–54 and the Crisis's "paradox of subjectivity" (§§53–54) turn on this very distinction.

The transcendent apperception of a colour sensation datum illustrates it best (128.33–129.8). Taken as itself, it is a component of the transcendental sphere of being. But it also bears a thingly apprehension; living in this, I see a thing-colour. The sensed colour is not pushed out into the world of things; that would make no sense. It bears a reifying apprehension in which I can live. In the pure stream there is the sensed colour and an apprehending that animates it; living in this apprehending means having consciousness of a transcendent thing and thing-colour. So there are two attitudes: in one the attentive gaze goes to sensation and apprehension, in the other to the appearing transcendent real. The example transfers the model of §4c, sensation animated by apprehension, from the thing to the person. As the sensed colour is not in the world while the thing-colour is, so the pure experience is not in the world while the psychic state is. The parallel shows that psychological apperception is a case of constitution, not a separate problem.

It is easy to see that the predicates of absoluteness, of the grasping of being that concerns the immanent, of absolute independence from all existence of the real, belong exclusively to transcendental consciousness, not to transcendent or psychological consciousness (129.9–29). A psychological consciousness, this real state of the real human being, has real presuppositions and connections; its categorial type as "real state" necessarily requires a substantial real and connections of causality, just as a physical state or property cannot exist except in the a priori regulated connections prescribed by the idea of a physical substance, a substrate of causalities with causal laws. As soon as we carry out the a priori possible destruction of nature, by which all substances and causalities and so all thingly properties and states are struck, every positing of a consciousness as a real state loses its validity: there are no real states of consciousness, because there are no animal subjects of states. "But consciousness itself is not thereby struck out; rather it remains constantly presupposed." The section ends with the strongest consequence of the distinction: with nature annihilated, psychology's object vanishes, since states require substances and causal connections, but transcendental consciousness remains, because it is presupposed even by the thought of the annihilation. This is Ideen I §54 point for point: the psychological is a real state dependent on bodies and nature, so that with nature annihilated there would be no animal subjects and no psychological experience, while absolute consciousness remains. With §14 the lecture has reconstructed the full sequence of the fundamental consideration from §27 to §54: natural world, contrast of immanent and transcendent being, contingency of the world, dependence of reality on actual consciousness, absolute being of consciousness, reduction, and the separation of transcendental from psychological consciousness.

## Appendix IV. The Argument of the Lecture Course of Summer Semester 1915 as a Proof of Transcendental Idealism Conducted Solipsistically

The editor dates the note to 1921 or perhaps 1926. It opens with a verdict: it concerns "the failed course of the war summer of 1915," in which the proof of transcendental idealism was carried out without regard to the transcendental intersubjectivity of constitution, and so, as it were, solipsistically (130.5–8). The judgment matches the note on Text No. 5 (78) and bears directly on Ideen I, whose fundamental consideration has the same structure as the 1915 course. Read against §12 of Text No. 6, "solipsistic" cannot mean that other egos are ignored; it means that the world's constitution is analysed in the single ego's experience and other egos enter only as posited within that experience, so that the objectivity of the world is not shown to depend on a community of constituting subjects.

Should the course not be retained as a lower stage, as transcendental idealism of the primordial sphere, and supplemented by an upper stage (130.9–131.2)? We go the Cartesian way in some depth, without changing the fact that, without noticing, implicitly, with the ego cogito it restricts itself to the transcendental-egological, merely primordial sphere and so leads or pushes toward transcendental solipsism, a main point of the critique of Descartes and modern philosophy. Taking over his starting point in the critique of sensibility, I modify it (in this course too, as far as I now recall) by treating "sensible experience" as my ego's consciousness and subjecting it to intentional explication, consciousness in which any real thing and finally the world, the universe of spatiotemporal realities existing for me, alone comes and can come to original givenness and my own experience as existing and so existing. "Original givenness" means simple sensible perception; "experience," my memories and so on. Clarifying the in-principle possibility of deception of "sensibility," of the perceptual belief in its character of self-grasping of the real, shows that this belief is anticipatory: it goes not only to what actually appears itself but posits this with an anticipated stock; perception has a perceptual horizon, noetically a horizon of possible perception and synthetic progress, in which the moving self-givenness is joined into the unity of a confirmation, a perceptual self-confirmation, which, as far as it reaches, again has the character of perception, again anticipating into horizons or referring to the synthetic constitution of what is already confirmed and what is still to be confirmed. A note adds: in these syntheses the just-past perception, modified as retention, joins the new actual one; such living perceptions recur in recollection and join synthetically; "but the whole doctrine of 'experience' as habituality was missing there." The note proposes a rescue rather than a retraction. The 1915 argument, and with it the argument of Ideen I §§33–55, is valid for the "primordial sphere," the stratum that the fifth Cartesian Meditation (§44) isolates by abstracting from everything that refers to others. A higher stage must add intersubjective constitution. The terms "primordial" and "Cartesian way" are from the Cartesian Meditations and the lectures on First Philosophy (Hua VIII, 1923/24), where Husserl criticizes the Cartesian way into the reduction for leading the ego to an apparently empty and solipsistic field. The final note identifies a second gap: the 1915 course analysed perceptual confirmation as a synthesis of present and retained perceptions but lacked the theory of habituality, of experience as abiding acquisition. Both corrections, intersubjectivity and habituality, belong to the later genetic phenomenology, and both modify the thesis of Ideen I without abandoning it.

Descartes already, for lack of understanding of the questions that must be put to the cogito as cogito of its cogitatum, and to the ego as cogitans, and of the method demanded by the essence of the cogito as consciousness-of, falls into a self-evidence that secretly guides him and from which the perverse doctrine of evidence of later times arose (131.3–12). It is the prejudice that brings solipsism in its train. The world, before science, is originally given to me and everyone through sensible perception, and it is the world of habitual experience, the abidingly existing world, pregiven from the habituality of experiential validity already instituted. The diagnosis places the source of solipsism in an unexamined assumption about evidence: that the world is given only in present sense-perception, not as a world of habitual validity shared with others. The positive formula, the world "pregiven from habituality," is the concept of the pregiven lifeworld of the Crisis; it implies that the world on which the idealist thesis bears is a world of sedimented, communal validity, and that its dependence on consciousness must be shown at that level.

## No. 7. The Formally General Proof of Transcendental Idealism and the Unthinkability of Real Being without Bodily Subjectivity. Essential Relation of World-Constitution to an Open Plurality of Subjects. Birth and Death of Every Subject as A Priori Necessities

The manuscript, from 1914 or 1915, states its theme at once (132.10–15): real being requires not only some factually existing knowing subjectivity, which is "the formally general proof of transcendental idealism," but real being, or the being of a real world, is thinkable only if the correlative knowing subjectivity is bodily, human subjectivity in this world. A note advises reading first the structure of the findings set out later (139ff). The phrase "formally general proof" names the argument of Texts No. 3, 5 and 6 and of Ideen I §§47–49: real being requires actual consciousness. Text No. 7 asks what this consciousness must be like, and its answer, embodied subjects belonging to the world, goes beyond anything Ideen I states. It is the first attempt in the volume to determine the "actual consciousness" of the special thesis materially.

First (132.16–23): the proposition that an object is possible is equivalent to the proposition that valid knowledge of it is possible, and the latter includes the possibility of "experience," of originally giving consciousness. The hypothesis that this objectivity exists therefore implies that an ego must be positable that is related to it in a knowing cogito, first in an experiencing one. The general correlation is restated as starting point. The step from knowledge to experience fixes the type of consciousness required for individual objects: an originally giving, experiencing consciousness.

Second (132.24–29): what does this mean for a material world? By its sense it is a totality of real being determined in itself, in the forms of infinite time and infinite space encompassing every real member. A note: nothing is said here about the manifold-form of "objective" (as opposed to intuitive) space. What belongs a priori to possible experience of such a world? To set this out by noesis and noema is the task of the theory of phenomenological constitution. The vocabulary of noesis and noema, introduced in Ideen I, is now used as the frame of the analysis. The note restricts the argument to the intuitive form of space, leaving open whether physical space is Euclidean, a topic of Husserl's later remarks on geometry.

It can be established that noematically, possible experience includes possible appearance of the real world "from a certain Now and Here," in a temporal and spatial orientation (132.30–133.8). Temporal orientation flows during experience, is not free, and is subject to no choice; spatial orientation is subject to freedom and choice as to change or persistence, all essentially. I, the experiencer, "can" change my "position" toward things and so toward or "in" the whole world of experience. The asymmetry between unfree temporal and free spatial orientation is an important refinement of §4a of Text No. 6. It locates the "I can" of kinaesthesis on the spatial side only and prepares the question whether this freedom requires a body.

Husserl's investigations are not cleanly concluded on whether it is a priori necessary that the experiencing ego needs a freely movable body, itself appearing in space, for its free change of position (133.9–134.4). If this were shown more clearly, one could say that the possibility of experiencing a world of things, according to the given idea of ours, presupposes that the experiencer belongs to the experienced world insofar as he has a body in it. Suppose it were further shown not only that an experiencing subject must find a body as its own, a distinguished spatial object that must function as perceptual organ in all experience, but also that, without experiencing other bodies and so without empathy, it can apperceive its body in the full sense as a thing like others, although in immediate experience it experiences it as a spatial object but not in the full sense like every other (not movable in space as every other is); and suppose it were shown that it can thereby form the apperception of the human being, the rightful presentation: in space, among other things, there is a body, and I am one with it; I, as pure ego and as ego of capacities, am bound to it and form a real unity with it. Then it would be proved that a material world is not thinkable as actual unless an experiencing human (animal) can be posited with it as actual, belonging to it as member in the sense in which humans belong to our world. Would it then be proved that a merely material world is unthinkable, thinkable only as a psychophysical world in which at least one human is material by his body and his psychic life has, through the body, a determinate place in objective space and time? This remains the question: what does it mean that "the possibility of a material world requires the possibility of animals capable of knowledge in it"? The paragraph lays out a programme and marks it as unfinished. Each step is stated conditionally: the body as organ of free movement; the self-apperception of the body as one thing among things, and whether this is possible without empathy; the apperception of the human being as psychophysical unity. These steps are the constitutional analyses of Ideen II §§35–42, written in the same years. The conclusion, if the programme succeeded, would sharpen the special thesis: the actual consciousness required by a real world would have to be embodied in that world. Ideen I's absolute consciousness, which can survive world-annihilation, would then have to be distinguished from the consciousness that a world requires, a tension the text goes on to feel.

Third (134.5–28): if several subjects are to know identically the same material world, what has been said must hold for each. Independently of the doubtful points, each subject must be able to present and experience the world under a temporal-spatial aspect, in an orientation; they are related either to the same point of time and place or to different ones. In the first case: each has its Now, its Here, certain given things and a horizon. Is it not thinkable that two subjects relate to two similar worlds and not one identical world? It is certain that if the things are the same for both, knowledge of the identity must be possible. If the subject of that knowledge is a third subject, it must be able to "see into" the same world, standing to it as the other two do, and must be in commerce with them and able to re-experience their experiences, which is thinkable only as a relation of empathy and requires all three to have animal existence in the presupposed world of things. The argument turns the condition of intersubjectivity into a condition of embodiment. Identity of the world across subjects must be knowable; such knowledge requires empathy; empathy requires perceived bodies; so the subjects that share a world must be bodies in it. The question whether two subjects might have two qualitatively similar worlds rather than one world restates the problem of Text No. 6 §12 in its sharpest form.

The other case is that one of the first two subjects, or both, is the knower of the identity (134.29–36). This again presupposes that both are animal, human subjects, belonging bodily and mentally to the presupposed world, which is therefore not merely material but psychophysical, a world with bodies and persons. The alternative cases reach the same result: any knowledge of a shared world requires embodied subjects in it.

Fourth (134.37–135.14): consider the case left open, that the two subjects relate in original experience to different stretches of time in one world. One must show that one subject has experiential grounds for assuming, in its past world, a body as another's body in a past surrounding world, and so for a rightful empathy into that subject's past experience, so that it becomes rightfully possible to know that the same things belong to my past world as to the other's present one, or that what I must assume as actual in the past is what that past human perceived in his Now; so that I and that past human have a common world of experience, only under different temporal and in general spatial orientation. The case of past subjects extends empathy across time, the condition for any historical knowledge of others' experience and for the "tradition" mentioned at the end of Text No. 6 §12. It also prepares the problem of the world before any subjects, which closes the text.

Fifth (135.15–26): it is, as we know, questionable whether the constitution of an objective spatiotemporal material world has an essential relation to an open plurality of subjects, which as animal would then themselves belong to the world as psychophysical. If this were proved, it would follow at once that a merely material nature without the possibility of animal subjects in it is a priori unthinkable, always under the viewpoint that the possibility of nature presupposes or ideally includes the possibility of knowing it. This latter possibility would require, as would then have to be shown, not the mere logical possibility of "humans in nature" but their actual existence. "But there is still a decisive gap." The thesis of the title, essential relation to an open plurality, is presented as an open question. Its importance for Ideen I is that it would make intersubjectivity a condition of the world's objectivity, not merely a fact about it; Ideen I §151 sketches this but does not prove it, and the later note in Appendix IV calls the omission the failure of the 1915 course.

One might argue thus for a nature in the sense of the idea of mathematical-physical nature (135.27–38): a merely material nature would be a definite manifold; everything contained in a world infinite in time and space is completely determined. Only by complete determinability can the identity of an infinite world be maintained. What is not in it cannot be in it, and everything possible in it is actual. All consideration of possibilities in this world means that this identical world can be thought otherwise than it is, keeping its lawful form but not the content filling the form. "Definite manifold" is the term from Husserl's philosophy of mathematics (Ideen I §72): a manifold whose axioms decide every proposition. Applied to nature, it yields a Laplacean determinism in which possibility within the world collapses into actuality. The next step uses this to argue that knowing subjects cannot be merely possible in such a world.

So bodies cannot be thought into it without changing those already in it, within its infinity (136.1–28). But living bodies would not suffice for the possibility of knowing subjects in this world. Consciousness cannot fly into existing bodies at will, like an empty ghost that changes nothing in definite reality. A psychophysical world too must be absolutely definite: what is possible in it is actual and necessary, if it is to be an identical, determinate world. Whatever this requires, both regarding the limits of definiteness of the underlying material nature and regarding the relation of mind to nature, this much is certain: if acts that know the world and constitute it phenomenologically (experiencing acts) are to be able to occur in the world, they must be definitely prescribed in it and so actually occur. Spiritual subjects in the world that are to be possible subjects of knowledge cannot be mere free possibilities but must be actualities; "a world is possible only with knowing subjects in it." It is not necessary that the world be known at every time; it suffices that it is given oriented in a present from which reconstruction is possible. But now we face the embarrassment of this possibility and of the infinity of what is knowable in the world, which means infinite possibilities of knowledge, for which we would have to suppose actualities too in the development of the world; each of the infinitely many systems of appearances is a field of knowability. Must that mean real being of such knowledge? The argument from definiteness is ingenious and Husserl distrusts it at once. If the world is a definite manifold, the possibility of knowers in it implies their actuality. But the same reasoning would turn every possibility of knowledge, for every system of appearances, into an actual knower, which is absurd. The image of consciousness as a ghost flying into bodies dismisses parallelism and epiphenomenalism together: mind cannot be an inert addition to a determinate nature. The admission that the world need not always be known, provided reconstruction from a present is possible, anticipates the treatment of the world before humans at the end of the text.

So there is reason to doubt the value of the whole argument (136.29–137.18). We have an irremovable basic question: if it is shown that the possibility of knowing a material world includes the possibility of subjects belonging to it as animals, what does this possibility mean? Can it be merely logical? No. For these subjects are to be possible knowers of this determinate world, the one fact against countless possible worlds, even countless worlds of the same spatiotemporal-causal form with other content. The knowing subjects posited as possibilities with the hypothesis of this world must be equipped with distinguished experiential dispositions, determinate regulations of possibilities of sensation and perception that exclude other similar and dissimilar ones. If they were arbitrary logical possibilities, with dispositions and motivations likewise merely logical, they would not be preferred over other logically possible subjects with other dispositions, sensations, perceptions, corresponding to other possible worlds. There is evidently no way out except that subjects are actual and actually related to the determinate world, actually capable of knowing it, actually equipped with the distinguished possibilities of experience. "So I come back here to my original proof for 'idealism': a world, a quite determinate one, is thinkable only if it is knowable as existing." The text returns to the selection argument of Texts No. 3 and 5, now in the language of dispositions. Logically possible subjects correspond to all possible worlds equally, so they cannot anchor this world; only actual subjects with determinate dispositions can. The new element is the claim that the anchoring subjects must have "dispositions," the innate regularities of sensation that the later part of the text calls "accord."

"But that is not proved with a single word" (137.19–26). A serious problem lies here: what does the idea of a world in which knowing subjects are to be able to appear require? And the thought is valuable: the assumption that a nature exists requires the possibility of knowing subjects in it, and this possibility does not tolerate an equally valid counter-possibility that such subjects are not, need not be, in it. Husserl's self-criticism is severe and characteristic. What survives is the modal point: the possibility of knowers that a world requires is not a possibility with an equally good alternative, as logical possibilities are; it has the weight of reality.

The addition of spirits to nature cannot be the addition of an indifferent, contingent annex; one might point to the free bodily movements by which the spiritual subject intervenes in nature, which tells against the usual doctrine of parallelism (137.27–138.13). There is a correlation: as a material nature is unthinkable that is insensitive to the appearance of knowing subjects, and is unmodified in its determinate reality by these spirits only if they belong to it from the start as co-existents in the unique unity of a psychophysical world, so spiritual subjects are unthinkable that would be insensitive to the appearance or disappearance of a nature "in itself." The being of a determinate nature prescribes definite rules to the possible course of consciousness of spirits. But as soon as we posit spirits with such regulations as existing, we have co-posited the existence of nature. This requires an actual subject of its possible knowledge, and that a "human subject" belonging to the world itself, so it is proved that a merely material world is unthinkable, and thinkable only as psychophysical, including organic and "human-like" beings.
