(Here "idealism" is not yet stated; we reach it when we reduce to absolute consciousness, which is left aside here and needs repeated checking.) The correlation is now stated at the level of the world, not of transcendental consciousness. Nature and spirits are mutually dependent members of one psychophysical world, and the dependence of nature on knowing subjects appears as a law within this world. The parenthesis is crucial for the relation to Ideen I: this is not yet idealism, which requires the further reduction to absolute consciousness. Text No. 7 thus describes the worldly counterpart of the transcendental thesis: within the constituted world, nature is unthinkable without embodied knowers, just as, transcendentally, the world is unthinkable without actual consciousness.

So the proof can proceed in two theses (138.14–22). First: suppose a material nature exists. The possibility of subjects knowing it, the necessary correlate of its existence, cannot be merely logical with a counter-possibility of non-being; it must have the value of an actuality. For the possibilities of subjects knowing any other possible world, besides the one posited as actual, with the corresponding experiences, do not hold together with it. The first thesis is the formally general proof stated as compactly as possible, with its key premise: the knowing-possibilities of rival worlds are mutually exclusive, so only actuality can decide among them.

To leave no unclarity in such fundamental matters (138.23–139.13), recall what precedes and is included in this possibility of knowing subjects. We presupposed a nature of determinate content A; it requires and is equivalent to the possibility of its rightful knowledge, but not a merely logical possibility, as should have been shown at once; it must be real. If A is a world or any non-ideal being, a matter of fact, there are two open possibilities a priori: A does not exist, though possible; and A is not only possible but exists. If A exists, legitimation of its being, rightful knowledge of A, must be possible. If A does not exist, it is impossible; for if it were possible, rightful knowledge would be compatible with the non-existence of what is known, which is a contradiction, A and not-A, since rightful knowledge necessarily includes the existence of what is known (not by real inclusion, of course). But the possibility of knowledge as empty logical possibility holds whether A exists or not; it is the correlate of the mere logical possibility of being, of mere consistent presentability, which implies logical positability (a note: logical possibility does not equal analytic-logical). So a knowledge of A is presentable and positable in the same sense; consistent presentability of A is equivalent to consistent presentability of a legitimation of A's existence. The argument is a clean modal proof. Rightful knowledge of A implies A's existence, so where A does not exist rightful knowledge is impossible. But logical possibility of knowledge holds whether or not A exists. Therefore the possibility of knowledge that is equivalent to A's existence is not logical possibility; it is a stronger kind.

This holds for every logically possible A, existing or not (139.14–31). The non-actuality of a possible A excludes the possibility of knowledge, though not the possibility in the sense of logical possibility of knowledge. So the possibility excluded here, which belongs to A's existence, is not mere logical presentability; it is "real" possibility, as conversely the possibility of knowledge required by A's existence is "real." What does this reality require? A knowing subject, of course. But a merely logically possible subject is no substrate of real possibilities. The existence of a possible A, a contingent one whose non-existence is logically open, requires the existence of an actual subject that experiences A or knows it on the basis of experience, or that has the practical possibility, the practical ability, to experience and know A, standing to A as we stand directly to the objects of our familiar surroundings and indirectly to more distant ones. The conclusion introduces the decisive concept: real possibility needs a "substrate," and only an actual subject can be one. The "practical ability" (praktisches Können) is the "I can" of kinaesthesis turned into the criterion of real possibility. This formulation, that possibilities must be possibilities of someone, is the most precise version of the special thesis in the volume and the one Ideen I §47 presupposes when it speaks of possibilities "motivated" in an actual experiential nexus.

"I think this train of thought is gaplessly grounding," and so an important general epistemological thesis is proved (139.32–140.3): "the existence of every factual being, of every 'individual' being (including states of affairs, relations, etc.), requires the necessary co-existence of a knowing or knowledge-capable subject." The confidence contrasts with the self-criticism of 137. What Husserl regards as proved is the formal thesis; what remains doubtful is the material thesis about embodied subjects. The extension to states of affairs and relations about individuals carries the thesis beyond things to everything factual.

This cannot be said for eidetic being (140.4–8). There, too, existence is equivalent to possibility of knowledge, but the logical possibility of a legitimation is equivalent to the existence of the idea of the legitimation and hence to its validity. For ideal objects logical possibility and existence coincide, so the possibility of knowing them requires no actual subject. The contrast repeats Texts No. 1, 5 and 6 §11 in modal terms.

The sense of the proof can also be varied, keeping its core (140.9–23). I, the epistemologically reflecting subject, am actual. Considering possible individual A, I can present their legitimation as existing as well as their legitimation as non-existing. The actual existence of A, which I posit hypothetically, requires, if I am to be able to know it, more than carrying out possible legitimations: I must be able actually to experience it. That prescribes a real possibility for me, the knower, and so for everyone; and if no one has these real possibilities, A is not actual. If I posit that an idea exists, an ideal connection, I can present the grounding connection if it is possible; and then I already have its actuality, since for ideas possibility and actuality coincide. The variant is the egological form of the proof, starting from the actual reflecting ego. Its decisive phrase, "and if no one has these real possibilities, A is not actual," is the special thesis in its intersubjective form: actuality needs someone for whom it is really possible to experience.

Applying this to possible worlds, the second step follows (140.24–27): an existing world requires not only existing knowing subjects but knowing subjects that have animal existence in it. The second thesis is the material thesis of the title. It is announced, not proved; the text's last part considers its consequences.

The required actually existing knower is required not as actually knowing but as actually capable of knowing; nor is such a subject required for the whole infinite time of the world's existence (140.28–33). The question is what the actual possibility of knowledge of an actual subject must mean a priori and how these relations are to be clarified. Two relaxations of the special thesis are introduced. The anchoring subject need only be capable of knowledge, not actually knowing, and need not exist at every time. The first is already in Texts No. 5 and 6 (the "I can"); the second opens the problem of the world before and after subjects.

Must subjects in the world always be there, even if not yet actually knowing or capable of knowledge, at every time-point of the world's existence (140.34–141.15)? The answer will probably have to be yes. The "appearance" of subjects at some time cannot suffice, nor their existence for a limited stretch of the world's duration. One could say that a merely material world as lower stage and initial stretch of the world's duration satisfies the conditions of knowability if a subjectivity exists that rationally constitutes this world as its present surroundings and can rationally reconstruct backwards the preceding stretches, including a stretch of merely material nature. But the question is what it means that a world, necessarily constituted, exists even when its constitution is not actual, and was without being constitutively experienced. The problem of the world before life is the hardest test of the special thesis, and Husserl's tentative answer, that subjects must always be there, probably in some form, is the one he develops into a monadology of dormant subjects in Hua XIII–XV. The alternative, reconstruction from a later present, is the answer of Text No. 9 and of Husserl's late texts on the "earth" and geological time; here it is stated and immediately problematized.

The existence of subjects in accord, endowed with rules of experience corresponding to the constitution of things, is, in the sense of idealism, equivalent to the existence of things and the thing-world (141.16–24). The constitution of things is only potential. But what kind of potentiality? The course of consciousness is not arbitrary but, even where it is not actually constitutive (where no actual apperceptions of things have developed), prescribed by the existence of nature. But how, since something like entirely dull consciousness is possible? "Accord" (Akkord) is Husserl's term for the harmony among the monads' regularities of experience, an echo of Leibniz's pre-established harmony. The equivalence of accorded subjects with the existence of things is the idealist thesis restated monadologically. The difficulty is the possibility of "dull" consciousness without differentiation: if consciousness can exist without any constituted things, how can its course be prescribed by nature?

Here one must note that empirical apperceptions point back to an a priori genesis (141.25–142.7). Every subject is led back to an epoch of its phenomenological time in which every apperception was still lacking. We can also say: every subject has its birth, not for contingent but for a priori reasons, if it is to satisfy the conditions of a "subject in a world." It is evident a priori that every subject must be developing, at least so long as it does not sink into dullness; what that means is still a problem, perhaps: so long as something new constantly appears in its consciousness and the conditions for it are fulfilled. Birth as beginning of personal and apperceptive genesis must also be entry into the psychophysical world, which consists in a body forming from thingly components that was not yet there, entering into stimulus relations with other things (perhaps also into causal relations by the free "I move"), and in the formation of empirical apperceptions beginning under these conditions and continuing together with practical apperceptions and purposes, by which the constitution of a practical environment, a world of making, a cultural world, begins. Here the genetic turn appears in the volume. Apperceptions have a history in each subject, so each subject has a beginning of apperceptive life, and birth is the worldly appearance of this beginning. The thesis that birth is an a priori necessity for a subject in a world is the problem announced in the commentary on Text No. 1 (20): consciousness cannot arise as a real thing arises, yet each worldly subject must be born. The resolution is that birth is the beginning of personal and apperceptive life, not of the stream as such, as the next unit states. The order of constitution sketched here, perceptual world, practical environment, cultural world, is the plan of Ideen II's third section.

"Birth" means the beginning of personal being, awakening life; conscious life and existence begin, but not life in general, the stream of consciousness in a wider sense (142.8–18). Monadic being before birth can be assumed only as entirely dull life: all sense fields "black," undifferentiated, all stimuli lacking. Stimuli are salience or rather prominences; nor can the sense fields separate from one another; they must form an undivided complex, since their separation presupposes prominences within each field. The fields are already constituted objective unities, ordered possibilities and unities for possible particular prominences. The distinction between the stream "in a wider sense" and conscious, personal life makes the doctrine of Text No. 1 compatible with birth: transcendental life does not begin, waking life does. The description of pre-natal dullness, undifferentiated fields without salience, uses the concepts of the passive synthesis lectures (Hua XI), affection and prominence, ahead of their full development.

What can distinguish one "lifeless," "dead" consciousness, an ego persisting in unconsciousness without differentiated memories or apperceptions, from another such (142.19–143.3)? What determines the particular selection of future possibilities of waking life? The innate dispositions and the accord. Innate disposition means a principle of selection, a regulated, not arbitrary, "mode of reaction." Awakening: quite determinate sense data, and no others, occur in a factual sequence, and among subjects in accord there is accord in this respect. In the language of the apparently constituted world: the child wakes in possession of its child's body and through it in a determinate world-connection; so it experiences just these external stimuli, reacts to them by its bodily and psychophysical dispositions just so, and has just this course of experience. Every human and animal appears in a determinate connection of the spatiotemporal world by its generation (omne vivum e vivo) and the biological laws governing it; it experiences the world in its orientation, with its prescribed horizon, and has its individual way of moving beyond this horizon or being torn beyond it. The double description, monadic and worldly, is the method of the late Husserl in miniature. What appears in the world as heredity and biological generation ("all life from life," the traditional biological dictum) appears transcendentally as innate disposition and accord among monads. The "dispositions" are the "distinguished experiential dispositions" that the selection argument (137) required: they are what makes one actual subject the anchor of this world rather than another.

Again in the language of the absolute attitude (143.4–19): subjectivity exists before awakened subjectivity and before animal being in the constituted world. Every subject has a life-period of pre-worldly subjectivity, then a phase in the world, then a phase of post-worldly subjectivity, that of death. The world is created, that is, a group of subjects gains conscious life according to a determinate rule-order of the accord. Every subject has its individuality and its rule beside the rule of the encompassing accord. From the world of experience we gain the idea of a possible world in general as psychophysical spatiotemporal world, and from it the conditions of possibility for this world's being infinite, uniquely determined for all future. Questions: can the world have a beginning or an end, must it be infinite in space and time? Clarity is needed here. Further: must a subject in the world correspond to every time-point of the objective world? A note: infinity of the world in space and time is very well thinkable, as is the infinity of the immanent flow of time. The three phases, pre-worldly, worldly, post-worldly, give birth and death a transcendental sense: they are transitions between dull and waking life, not beginnings and ends of the monad. The definition of creation as the awakening of a group of subjects according to a rule is the idealist reinterpretation of cosmogony. The questions about the beginning and infinity of the world are Kant's first antinomy reconsidered within the framework of constitution.

A new start, by Husserl's own description "reflections in considerable disorder" (143.20–26): the possibility of the world requires possible knowing subjects, capable of carrying out constituting experience. If subjects are developed enough to have empirical intuition of a world and a psychophysical community-world, a pre-human past of nature can be imagined and interpreted monadologically. The self-description marks the following as exploratory. The proposal is to read the pre-human past through the developed present: once a world is constituted, its past, including times without humans, can be interpreted as a stage of monadic development.

Think of a plurality of "awake" subjects in development, especially in the development of apperceptions, all in proper accord (143.27–144.11). Each has its course of sensations and experiences, but not yet sufficient for constituting spatial objectivity. Let this be the lower stage of development of an open plurality of community-subjects who at a higher stage constitute the world in consciousness. What develops are individual subjects, but remarkably we have the development of an accorded "community": according to a certain lawfulness, new subjects enter this connection (hidden from the subjects themselves) and others leave (birth, death). The newly entering form a series of higher development, with ever more differentiated bodies, more developed nervous systems and sense organs; that lies along the line of development of world-constitution. A note objects: since there is no encompassing objective time-form for the plurality of subjects and their developments, what can "connection," intersubjective coordination, "new entry" mean? Evolutionary biology is reinterpreted as the development of a monadic community toward the constitution of a world. The note states the decisive objection to the idea: objective time is constituted by the community, so the community's development cannot be dated in objective time without circularity. The difficulty recurs throughout Husserl's later monadology (Hua XV, texts on "generativity").

This development would have to be necessary and unique, and its goal would have to be actually reached so that a world is phenomenally constituted; that is necessary for there to be talk of a world at all (144.12–20). Then an intuitively re-understanding presentation of these coordinations could be understood in terms of the goal, as a lower stage of world-constitution, and correlatively one could speak of "the world before the appearance of humans who know the world in which they are." A note sharpens it: it must be shown that only if actual subjects can have a nature over against them in actual experiences is the existence of a world without humans (rational, here experiencing, beings) thinkable; a world without subjects who actually experience it is thinkable only as the past of a world with such subjects. Might something similar hold for a past stretch of the world without any subjects, a purely material past? The formula of the note is the most defensible form of the special thesis for the pre-human world: a world without experiencers is thinkable only as the past of a world with them. Its past is real because it is reconstructible from an actual present of experiencing subjects. This makes the past of the world dependent on the existence of a future, an implication Husserl accepts.

From here one sees that a spatiotemporal world with merely psychic beings is quite possible, but only as a lower stage of the same world with rational beings appearing (144.21–28). Likewise a natural world with irrational beings like madmen is possible only if they appear in the familiar imperfect community with rational beings who can place them in the world. So the world is related to necessarily normal subjects. The thesis of "normality" as condition of the world appears here for the first time in the volume: the objective world is correlated with the experience of normal, rational subjects, and abnormal experience (animals, the insane) is placed within it by them. Ideen II §18 and the texts of Hua XV develop the concepts of normality and abnormality that this sentence introduces.

As correlate of an existing world we necessarily have world-development, in the language of the absolute attitude: the development of an open but not arbitrary plurality of subjects in accord toward a level at which "normal" rational subjects appear who constitute the world (145.1–20). This level must actually be reached if one is to speak rightly of the world's existence. The world thus actually constitutes itself, but necessarily in a temporal orientation, since a phase of world-development must correspond to the constituted world, with an infinite horizon of past. The developed rational subject must assume a world-past and must present it as having been perceivable; in truth it could not have been perceived if no perceiving subjects existed. The presentation of the past is not a memory but an analogon of memory: an analogical transposition back of appearances of the type I have as actual memory or perception. What does this transposition mean, and what is its footing and right? The past of the world is given by a presentification that is neither memory nor empathy but an "analogon of memory," and its right is in question. This is the exact problem that Husserl's late texts on the constitution of the "pre-given" past and of the geological world address, and it shows the limit of the special thesis: the dependence of the world on actual consciousness must be reconciled with the world's having a past in which no consciousness experienced it.

This leads back to the constitutive problem: how does not only an objective world in general constitute itself for us communicatively connected subjects, but how is a past and future infinitely filled with world constituted, how the infinity of world-space and the world-spheres in which no one is and no human could exist (145.21–26)? The editor notes that the continuation is published as Text No. 7 of Hua XIII. The text ends by reformulating the special thesis as a constitutive task. Instead of proving that the world depends on actual consciousness, one must show how actual, communicating subjects constitute a world that extends infinitely beyond any possible experience of theirs in space and time. That is the programme of the later intersubjective phenomenology, and it marks the distance between Ideen I's formally general proof and the material questions it left open.

## No. 8. The Main Pieces for the Proof of Transcendental-Phenomenological Idealism. The Possibility of Original Givenness as Correlate of the Existence of an Objectivity in General. The Equivalence of Possibility and Actuality for Eidetic Objects. The Positing of Individual Actuality

The text dates from 1918 at the latest. Its first piece is the general correlation (146.11–26): the possibility of original givenness is the correlate of the existence of any objectivity, so for nature the possibility of experience is the correlate of the possible existence of a nature. Every possible object is, in formal-logical generality, the subject of predicates that determine it; to each belongs an ideal closed totality of true propositions stating what it is. To the possibility of every true proposition belongs a priori the possibility of legitimation, which requires originally giving intuition of the state of affairs and so of the object about which it is. An object is possible without my or anyone's actually thinking it, or experiencing it if thinking it. But an object is in principle unthinkable that lacked the ideal possibility of experience and thus of a subject experiencing it. The text is a schematic outline of the proof, written after the main versions and presumably as a plan for a definitive presentation. Its first step derives the general correlation from formal logic: objects are substrates of true propositions, propositions require legitimation, legitimation requires intuition. This is the derivation of Ideen I §§136–142, in which formal logic and the phenomenology of reason meet.

Second, what does the possibility of original givenness require for eidetic objects (146.27–147.9)? Here "the possibility of an eidetic object is equivalent to its actuality." If a subject sees the possibility, it can pass over freely and with insight to seeing the actuality. And must every subject, in eidetic generality, be able to see every eidetic object, and every sense, every ideal objectivity in the widest sense? The question must be answered affirmatively; this is an a priori. Not every subject, actual or posited as possible actuality in pure consideration of possibilities, need actually know every eidetic object. The universality of eidetic access, which Text No. 1 (17) stated as "every cogitatio can occur in every consciousness," is now asked as a question and affirmed. It is the condition under which eidetic objects need only possible consciousness: since any subject could reach them, no particular subject is required.

There is an essential difference regarding the possibility of knowing them between eidetic objects of the formal and of the material sphere (147.10–23). Formal-eidetic objects, ideal objects of formal mathesis and the theory of meaning, belong to every conceivable subject in a peculiar way: they can be "produced" by free activity. To the idea of a subject and its stream belongs the difference of morphe and hyle. Every subject has its determinate hyletic stocks in positionality and neutrality, and without this determinacy the subject and its stream cannot be individually determinate. This hyle is in a sense pregiven, a substrate of possible actively produced forms, to which all logical forms belong, everything formal-ontological. A note asks: is it so simple? Must not hyletic stocks be "prominent" and function as stimuli, and must not conditions for that be fulfilled? The division of formal and material eidetics is that of Ideen I §§10 and 13 (formalization and generalization), and the pair morphe and hyle is introduced in Ideen I §85. The claim is that formal objects can be produced by spontaneous activity on any hyle, so they are accessible to every subject regardless of its sensory content. The note adds the reservation of genetic phenomenology: even formal activity presupposes affection, prominence in the hyletic field, so its universality depends on minimal conditions of sensibility.

The eidos "colour" can be given to a subject only through singular colours occurring to it in fact or falling to it in sensation; it has them in original passivity (147.24–148.13). Then it can grasp the eidos "colour" by free activity of "forming" and "producing." It can perform subject-positing and predicate-positing, constitute states of affairs and propositions productively, and form formal ideas, the idea of a proposition, of a subject of a proposition, of an object in general, of a number in general, where the content of the hyle remains inoperative and does not enter the ideas. Perhaps it is even an eidetic necessity of forming material ideas that material particulars are first given in sensation, in thetic givenness, and that phantasy modifications are possible only if corresponding sensations (of their genus) were given before; and for transcendent material ideas, that actual apperceptions, for example of sense-thingly colour, have first arisen as experiences of transcendent objects, even if not consistently harmonious ones, so that coloured objects and examples for "forming" the genus colour can offer themselves in free phantasy (not necessarily the colour differences previously experienced). The asymmetry between formal and material eidetics is now stated genetically. Formal ideas need no particular sensuous content; material ideas need at least one sensation of the genus, and transcendent material ideas need prior experience of transcendent things. This is a genetic condition on the eidetic method of Ideen I §§4 and 70, which relies on phantasy, and it shows that phantasy feeds on prior perception, a thesis Ideen I §70 hints at but does not argue.

All this does not cancel the eidetic insight we have in view: "every ego-subject can be re-thought, regarding its stream, so that it has every material eidos," has every material matter consciously in particulars, practises eidetic generalization on it (148.14–27). Thinking an ego and its stream, we think it necessarily with a determinate past course and present stock; if all this does not contain the material eidos, the a priori guaranteed determinate continuability of the stream can be thought so that this eidos appears in it. Only if we think the whole stream determined to infinity is it decided what will appear in it, as what has appeared and appears, and what not. The genetic condition does not limit eidetic universality, since any stream can be continued so as to acquire the needed sensations. Material eidetic objects therefore also correlate with possible consciousness in general: no particular stream is required, because any stream could become the right one. The phrase "re-thought" (umgedacht) anticipates the "re-fiction" (Umfiktion) of Text No. 9.

Under what a priori conditions the individual determinacy of an infinite stream stands, and correlatively the unique determinability of all its stocks, is a separate a priori problem (148.28–35). What matters is that every closed piece of the stream leaves open the further possibility of a course in which any assumed eidos can be thought as entering consciousness. The separate problem is the counterpart, for a stream, of the definite manifold of nature in Text No. 7: what makes a monad's whole life uniquely determined. Husserl brackets it, since the eidetic argument needs only openness of continuation.

Third, what are the conditions of possibility of original givenness of individual, temporal objects (148.36–149.5)? Immanent objects are only what they are as objects of consciousness and of the conscious subject. They are immanent temporal objects: either present, and then inseparable from the consciousness that constitutes them (immanent perceivednesses), or originally past in an originally retentional experience. The outline breaks off at the immanent case, which is settled by Text No. 5 (74): immanent individuals exist only as lived. The transcendent case, the core of the proof, is picked up in the following section.

What does the possibility of original givenness require for eidetic objects (149.6–10)? The possibility of eidetic objects is equivalent to their actuality, so a subject that sees the possibility of such an object can freely pass over to seeing its actuality. Husserl restarts the outline with the same proposition, now as the premise of a contrast.

Why do I need this; what is the leading thought (149.11–13)? An empirical object is to be bound, in its existence, to the existence of individual consciousness. The self-questioning names the purpose of the eidetic detour: to sharpen, by contrast, the thesis that empirical objects require individual, actual consciousness.

If I think any eidetic possibility, regardless of how I come to it genetically, I can recognize that actuality necessarily corresponds to it; I recognize that every eidetic possibility is equivalent to actuality; and that, although I know it, my existence remains out of question for the existence of the possibility and actuality, and not only mine but every existence (149.14–150.3). If a number of the number series or a proposition as such exists, this requires the possibility of acts in which such ideal objects come to givenness, not their actuality. I recognize the ideal being of "2 < 3." But if I live in phantasy and posit nothing of actuality, imagining two centaurs and three nymphs, I am no longer really the actual subject, whose actual experience conflicts with these phantasies. My actual experience, in which I am and which I cannot change without being someone else, speaks against the positing "I see these centaurs." The ego as subject of arbitrary phantasies is always another; and thinking an ego in general that has such objects and makes such groupings, I recognize that every such ego can know that 2 < 3, an ideal necessity for anyone positing groups of two and three things. I, the actual empirical subject, am not necessarily co-posited. The example ties the two sides together neatly. Arithmetic truths hold even for phantasy objects, two centaurs and three nymphs, so they are indifferent to any actual subject. The phantasy ego, by contrast, is a modification of me incompatible with my actual experience: "I cannot change it without being someone else." Individual identity is thus defined by the actual course of experience, and this is what makes the actual ego the anchor of real being: only an actual ego excludes alternatives.

Conversely, so long as we stay with presenting purely possible egos with possible harmonious series of perception, so long as we merely imagine with all actuality excluded, we have with merely fictive experiences, manifolds, and subjects merely fictive existence of things; no existence of a thing is preferred to another, no nature to one incompatible with it; I can imagine them all equally well; they are all equal possibilities (150.4–12). The selection argument is restated without change. Its persistence across Texts No. 3, 5, 6, 7 and 8 shows that Husserl regarded it as the core of the proof.

Only when we posit that an ego actually exists that is related by its experience to a thing or a nature, which surrounds an imagined thing as a more or less determinate, perhaps wholly undetermined surroundings, is one of the systems of possible experiences distinguished, even if undetermined to the widest extent (150.13–29). (The positing of the subject is indeterminate, of course, while the subject itself is determinate; to determine the subject and its experience we would have to imagine, in mere positing, infinite courses of harmonious experience, the whole of possibilities of experience in every dimension filled in harmonious form in all possible series.) With the positing of the subject as actual, phantasy loses the character of mere phantasy. Correspondingly, if a world actually exists, the corresponding possible experiences are not merely phantasy-possible but anchored in an actual domain of experience of actual subjects. The parenthesis clarifies an apparent paradox. When I posit an actual subject hypothetically, I posit it indeterminately; but what I posit is a determinate subject, whose full determination would require the infinite filling-in of its experience. The concept of "anchoring" (Verankerung) names the relation of real possibilities to actual experience, the word that sums up the special thesis.

The text ends with a single line: "Closer discussion of 'anchored'" (150.30). The memorandum shows where Husserl located the remaining work. Everything in the proof depends on what it is for possibilities to be anchored in actual experience, and that is what Text No. 9 takes up through the concept of re-fiction.

## No. 9. Argument for Transcendental Idealism. Re-Fiction in Connection with Bodiliness and Intersubjectivity

The text dates from 1921. It begins from the familiar example (151.6–29). The possibility of a centaur is the correlate of the possibility of a harmonious experience of a centaur, of presenting it as self-given, as a unity of appearances; this implies presenting an ego-consciousness that has the relevant experiences. When I bring a thing's possibility to clarity, I necessarily have a quasi-experiencing subject with corresponding experiences. If the object is imagined directly, this subject is a phantasy modification of my own ego, the one imagining and weighing the possibilities: "I translated into phantasy and re-fictioned (umfingiert) according to my factual stock of experience." If I imagine the centaur fair, directly, I can also imagine it black. This yields conflict: the phantasies of the fair and the black centaur coincide, one "changes" into the other under identity-coincidence of the object, and so exclude each other in possible coexistence; positing one for a moment, I cannot posit the other as existing. If I experienced one, the experience of the other would be negated. The same ego, the phantasy modification of me, cannot experience both at once, except as a change of one into the other. But can the fair and black centaurs not be thought as related to different, separate subjects? The key term "re-fiction" (Umfiktion) names the way every directly imagined object comes with a modified version of myself as its quasi-perceiver. The selection argument of Texts No. 3, 5 and 8 is now applied to the subjects: incompatible possible things correspond to incompatible versions of one ego. The closing question opens the 1921 problem: whether incompatible possibilities could be made compatible by assigning them to different subjects, which would allow many worlds for many egos.

I can imagine one subject as subject of the fair centaur and another as subject of the black, without connection (151.30–152.10). But how can one then speak of the same centaur? And yet it can be. Even staying within the re-fictions of my ego, I can let the fair centaur switch into the black, without connection; or rather, if I imagine the fair centaur, phantasy may switch involuntarily into a black one. The two phantasy objects then coincide; I just do not hold fast to the quasi-positing of the fair one and so do not take the phenomenon as a change of one and the same object. If I carry out the positing, holding the fair centaur fast as existing so in its quasi-present, I "think" it as actually existing in the intention toward legitimation. Two ways of passing between phantasies are distinguished. If the quasi-positing is maintained, the switch is a change in one existing object; if it is dropped, the switch is a replacement of one possibility by another. Possibility in the strict sense is thus correlated with suspended positing, and the "intention toward legitimation" is the mark of positing.

The assumption that it exists contains this intention, and then turning black means a change (152.11–27). If I drop the quasi-position, the positing of being, I take my stand on the black centaur, which remains in partial coincidence, in various layers of properties, with the fair one just posited. So I can go on at will; I then have possibilities side by side, equally justified, each freely positable in succession and related to the same time-form, which remains in self-coincidence. But does the necessity of co-presenting an experiencing ego as correlate imply the necessity of presenting every possible object as actually experienced? No. If I have experienced a thing, it remains existing for me even when I no longer experience it. That is not a mere fact; it belongs to every possibility that I can posit in imagination first emptily, symbolically, in thought, and then the possibility of legitimation lies in it, which is, as it were, the intuitive presentation. The answer to the last question preserves transcendence within the correlation: the thing is posited as existing beyond its being experienced, and this "remaining for me" belongs to the sense of every thing-possibility. Ideen I §45's "unperceived reality" is the same structure.

The thing is to be in itself, transcendent, existing even unexperienced but always experienceable (152.28–153.10). This belongs inseparably to the essence of a thing as meant, as intentional object. To present a thing as possible, intuitively, to imagine it, is to imagine it as self-given, experienced by an experiencing ego; I cannot imagine it without imagining it as experienced by a co-imagined experiencing ego. That does not say it cannot be without being experienced, but without being experienceable for an ego; it cannot be thought as existing without being thought as possibly experienced. "It cannot be thought" means either: it cannot be thought by me, who am and have my actual experience and my perceptual world ready for experience, into which this fictive being is inserted in conflict; or I "transpose myself into pure phantasy," suspend the experiential positing of co-experienced actuality, and am then a fictive ego, a re-fiction of my actual ego, conflicting with it. But I can suspend this thesis "I am" and take "myself," the re-fictioned ego, as pure possibility; I am then an ego in general, determined only as experiencer of the fictive thing. The distinction between "not without being experienced" and "not without being experienceable" is the general correlation, and the passage shows how the eidetic ego in general is obtained: by suspending the thesis "I am" on the re-fictioned ego. The pure possible ego of eidetic phenomenology is thus a re-fiction of the actual ego, a point that will matter when the text asks whether every possible ego is a variant of mine.

But there is a restriction here (153.11–26). Imagining the centaur means a direct phantasy: a quasi-perception carried out directly by the imaginer, the fictive ego "coinciding" with the imagining one; it is "as if" I experienced the centaur. It would be different if I imagined a foreign ego, externally presented, a man who presents or experiences a centaur, with the experience hovering before me as this other subject's experience. Then I would imagine more than one ego: myself, the first fictive ego, in an experience in which I experience the second ego as directly as a second permits, by empathy, and the second ego with such stock of experience that it experiences the centaur in this or that mode. In immediate fiction, then, we have only immediate re-fictions of my own ego as correlates of the fiction of the object. The distinction between direct and empathic fiction is the instrument of the rest of the text. Direct fiction yields variants of myself; empathic fiction yields others, but always through a variant of myself who empathizes. So every fictive other ego is reached through a fictive self, which places it in the same world as that self.

If I imagine "the" centaur first fair, then black, and then the surrounding nature that a thing requires, the change of fiction and the talk of the same centaur can mean that, without holding fast the quasi-thesis of one fiction, I pass over into the other with partial coincidence of the appearing objectivity (153.27–154.4). I could, so to speak, wipe the phantasy slate and write new phantasies on it; instead I only re-fiction again and again. In both cases I get equally justified possibilities; in the second, a system of possibilities that behave as "re-fictions" and coincide in conflict. Essentially, if one is held fast or posited as existing, every other is excluded; but we can freely choose any, each corresponding to a positing, only each positing invalidating every other. The two methods, erasing and re-fictioning, correspond to two kinds of possibility-system: unrelated possibilities and possibilities that coincide in conflict as variants of one object. The second is the systematic form of eidetic variation and, applied to worlds, gives the field of mutually exclusive possible natures.

Correlatively the experiencing ego is "the same," only constantly re-fictioned ego; the fictive experience is a quasi-perception and in certain moments a re-fictioning, which is an individual experience (154.5–23). So it is necessarily "the same" ego, or the system of re-fictions of the phantasy-thing corresponds to a system of re-fictions of the phantasy-ego, each ego of this manifold being incompatible with every other. In the other case the situation is less clear. But we can say: if I present anything directly in fiction, say optically, a re-fiction of my visual field serves as presenting material, and whatever appears in any re-fiction is drawn from the same fund. The visual field, "identical" and in self-coincidence throughout all re-fiction, binds the formations to an "identical" ego, to the circle of direct re-fiction of one ego. In any case every directly imaginable thing belongs to an ego of the same self-coinciding manifold of egos determined by the epistemologically reflecting, here the imagining, ego. The visual field as common "fund" is the phenomenological ground of the identity of the ego through its variants: every fictive perception is made from modifications of the same sensory field. This makes the system of re-fictions a system of versions of me, incompatible with one another as alternatives of one life.

If I imagine other subjects as external and things as experienced by them, I can present these mediately imagined things intuitively only if I can also present them directly; evidently to every externally imagined subject corresponds a subject in the manifold of my re-fictions, and to every indirectly imagined object a directly imaginable one (154.24–29). Mediated fiction is parasitic on direct fiction: the other's world can be intuited only as a world I could be given. This is the principle by which the text will argue that all compossible egos share one world.

A difficulty: I am bound in my phantasy (154.30–155.9). I cannot say that I could run through all possible colours freely in fiction, possessing all possible "quality-circles." A priori I can only say that the qualities I have actually experienced must lie within my possible fiction. I see that I may gain fictions not previously accessible, and I have insight that every direct re-fiction carries with it an ego that is a re-fiction of mine. If I imagine other egos externally, I can keep open the possibility that they experience a quality I cannot experience or imagine, but only as I may come to imagine something I could not before. I then have a conceptual positing of a foreign subject to whom I ascribe a sensuous content symbolically, and the question is how my inability to fulfil the positing within pure possibility relates to unthinkability. The genetic restriction of Text No. 8 returns as an objection to the universality of re-fiction: my phantasy is bounded by my sensory history, so others might have qualities I cannot imagine. The question whether the unimaginable is unthinkable, or only symbolically thinkable, is the limit case of the principle that whatever is must be intuitable.

What is thinkable must also be intuitable (155.10–156.13). In the realm of actual experience, empathy rests on the possibility of analogizing apperception of foreign bodiliness and mind, and only what lies within my own possibility of experience and fiction of my inner life can become intuitive of the other's interior. Beyond this, much that is non-intuitive can be ascribed to the other indirectly in thought, on good grounds. Empathy does not require that the first naive identification of the same thing, the same concrete surroundings, with exactly the same sensuous properties, be confirmed; it would remain feasible if the empathized appearances of one differed from the other's as appearances of the same thing differ within one subject. It is the same thing; we both see it, but each differently, as when one sees as equal colours that I see clearly as different, and says so and tells me. What actual experience teaches as fact I can transpose into pure possibility and ask: how can a subject be there for another at all, and the surroundings of one for the other? I can imagine an ego by re-fictioning myself, but I can also imagine an ego that is not a re-fiction of mine, positable without requiring my non-being. And I can imagine a present thing directly, re-fictioning myself; a thing experienced by another but not by me; a thing I experience together with the other; and all this for past and future things as well. I can delimit the manifold of possibilities equally justified beside others, and consider the necessities for a possible thing, or rather, since a thing is unthinkable without an open horizon of experience, for a nature and then a psychophysical world, regarding their experienceability by subjects, especially whether a nature in itself is thinkable as existing without actual subjects coexisting as subjects of possible experience of it. The example of colour-blindness answers the difficulty. Different subjects can see the same thing differently, as one subject sees it differently from different positions, so the identity of the world does not require identity of sensuous content. The decisive distinction is between egos that are re-fictions of mine, alternatives that would exclude my being, and egos that are not, which can coexist with me. Only the second are others in the full sense, and the question is under what conditions such coexisting egos are possible.

"Transcendental idealism says: a nature is not thinkable without coexisting subjects of possible experience of it; possible subjects of experience do not suffice" (156.14–28). If we stay with merely possible subjects of possible experience of nature, infinitely many incompatible natures are equally possible. As soon as we posit a determinate nature as existing, all incompatible natures are excluded, and so are possible experiencing egos for them. Thinking a nature N1, only subjects experiencing it are possible with it. Only possible? What does "co-possible" mean? Supposing N1, this being cannot exclude the possibility of other natures and their experiencing subjects in the sense of pure possibility; so the pure possibilities of those subjects are not excluded with N1. They are excluded in existence. So are only the subjects experiencing N1 not excluded in existence, compossible? The definition of transcendental idealism given here is the special thesis, stated as succinctly as anywhere in Husserl. The modal clarification introduces Leibniz's term: the issue is compossibility, which subjects can exist together with a given nature, not which are thinkable.

Starting from the idea of a possible nature: if a nature exists, not every ego can exist (156.29–157.7). No ego is thinkable without a stream, but if a nature exists the stream cannot be arbitrary, cannot contain experiences, sensations, external apperceptions in arbitrary content and order. The very occurrence of external apperceptions in a stream presupposes a prior order, since an external apperception, and the ego itself, has an immanent genesis in which sense data must occur in a certain order; admissible quality-genera (sense fields, conditions of possibility of anticipating tendencies toward optima) must correspond to rules of connection. External apperceptions must occur in levels, orders, harmonies, even when the freely intervening, "experimenting" ego acts; the possibility of intervention itself means a regulation, to infinity. A note: even if an ego never experienced the presupposed nature, its hyletic data cannot run so that a nature conflicting with the given one would be experienced. The genetic version of the constraint: a nature prescribes not only the content but the order and development of the streams that coexist with it. The note states the minimal constraint precisely: an ego need not experience the nature, but it cannot experience a rival one. The "optimum" is the concept of optimal givenness from Ideen II and Thing and Space, the norm toward which perceptual tendencies strive.

At least this negative can be said: "if a nature exists, not every a priori possible ego-monad can still exist" (157.8–24). Running through the idea of possible nature in general, to each possibility corresponds a type of possible experiencing egos, incompatible with every other such type as one nature with another. Incompatibility means that one and the same experiencing ego, if it experiences a nature and is to be able to know it as actually existing, cannot equally experience and know another nature. For a subject of possible knowledge of nature only one nature can be knowable; every other it may think cannot be experienceable and knowable by experience for this ego. This implies a rule prescribed by a determinate nature assumed to exist for all ego-subjects that are to coexist with it. A note: but a proof of this thesis is presupposed: several natures cannot exist; they are incompatible. The negative result is that a nature excludes the existence of egos whose experience would legitimate a rival nature. The note admits the gap: this follows only if several natures cannot coexist, which is what the idealist must prove against the realist's plurality of worlds.

The reverse start: from the idea of an ego in general, taken purely as the ego of a stream with capacities essential to it (157.25–158.10). This idea contains an infinity of possibilities, including nature-experiencing subjects and subjects possible a priori that experience natures of different type or of different lowest difference within the same type. But these subjects are not compossible, at least insofar as, if a nature exists, no existing ego can have a stream in which a nature incompatible with it would legitimate itself, a modification of the nature harmoniously experienced by this ego such as any free re-fiction by the same ego would yield. However evidently this re-fiction shows the possibility of another nature, it is equally evidently a possible nature excluded by the existence of the given one. A note: it would also have to be shown that every nature any ego can imagine and every nature any other can imagine would be either the same or different and incompatible. The reverse start yields the same result from the side of the ego and repeats the gap in a different form: what must be shown is that natures imagined by different egos are either identical or incompatible, never merely different and coexisting.

What of ideal objectivities such as "innate" truths, essential truths in general (158.11–159.2)? Each prescribes a negative rule for every possible ego in general: no ego can have evidence conflicting with an innate truth, evidence of something necessarily false. That A is an essential law and that B conflicts with A has its correlate in the possibility of certain modes of insightful consciousness of A, in the fact that an unfulfilled consciousness of B can only be partially fulfilled, so that when the unity-intention of the whole is to be fulfilled a necessary conflict, an "annihilation" of one by the other, occurs, or an evidence of the non-being of B appears with its own structure; or the analytic form of A excludes B. These are universal laws of evidence, laws for every possible consciousness of A and B and so for every possible ego, not just every factual one. Modes of consciousness that exclude each other essentially exclude each other for any ego that may be assumed as their subject. Essential truths are rules of consciousness for all possible egos, which is why they require no actual ego. The phenomenological description of contradiction, partial fulfilment ending in "annihilation" of the unity-intention, is the doctrine of compatibility and conflict in the sixth Logical Investigation (§§30–35) applied to ideal objects.

What distinguishes objective truths of fact and their evidences from eidetic truths and theirs (159.3–19)? An eidetic evidence is a rule of consciousness for every ideally possible ego; a factual evidence is not, since in purely ideal possibility infinitely many factual evidences are thinkable, incompatible with others. A possible evidence of something eidetic cannot conflict with any other possible evidence; insight into the possibility of an evidence is equivalent to the existence of the evidence. But possible harmonious experience, possible empirical knowledge, and finally the idea of universal harmonious experience and science, are a generality comprising infinitely many conflicting possibilities. Factual natural science corresponds to factual human experience and correlatively to factual nature. The contrast is the logical core of the whole volume. Eidetic possibility is self-consistent across all possible egos; empirical possibility is a field of rivals, and only fact decides among them. Ideen I §§2 and 7 state the corresponding distinction between eidetic and factual sciences.

Eidetically speaking, there are infinitely many possible, eidetically coordinated but conflicting natural sciences, experiences of nature, experiencing subjects, and natures (159.20–160.3). But if a nature exists and subjects consistently constitute it, or if we single out one possible nature and posit that it exists, or think a subjectivity experiencing nature whose presumptive knowledge can be carried through in progressive harmony as ideal subject of an infinitely expanding natural science, this means not only an empirical binding of the experiences and personal development of this one subjectivity; we can and must say that every other ego is subject to the rule that it cannot constitute another nature in itself. This presupposes that we can say a priori: "the nature that an I1 experiences and that would be knowable as existing to infinity, and the nature that another I2 experiences in the same way, are either absolutely the same nature or cancel each other in conflict." A note: so this proposition is a main desideratum of the proof. The desideratum is named precisely. The idealist proof needs the uniqueness of nature: any two natures legitimately experienced must be identical or contradictory. Without it, the realist can grant everything about each ego's world and still hold that there are many worlds, each relative to its subjects.

Starting as empirical ego from the given nature and varying it freely within the idea of a nature in general, I see that, experiencing this nature, I could not experience another, that two natures are not experienceable in existence for one ego; only one nature is experienceable for any ego (160.4–21). But do I not also see that two natures cannot be at the same time, that one simply excludes every other possible nature? If I know a nature, I know it could have been otherwise, or that instead of it only another nature is thinkable that shares a universal ontological form and coincides with it in conflict. So with my actual ego an infinity of other ego-possibilities coincide, all possibilities for me if "I" were otherwise, another. A second ego coexisting with me I can think only by means of phenomenal nature and empathy into bodiliness; and if each ego can have another appearance of nature, it cannot have another nature itself. This must be worked out. The argument for uniqueness runs through the body: other egos are thinkable only through empathy, empathy requires a shared phenomenal nature in which their bodies appear to me, so other egos can have other appearances of nature but not another nature. The many possible natures are thus alternatives for one ego, like its many possible lives, not coexisting worlds.

A nature is thinkable only as unity of possible harmonious experiences of an experiencing subject, and evidently one and the same subject, consistently experiencing one nature, cannot have a second given in the same way (160.22–161.15). Two natures incompatible in one subject could be compatible only with two subjects. But two subjects, I meant to be able to show, are compossible only if both are psychophysical subjects related to the same nature. An ego can present another ego, apart from its own incompatible variants, each a fictive being-otherwise of itself, only as an "external" subject constituted transcendently in its own stream, and that only by empathy into a transcendent bodiliness, which presupposes the empathizing subject's own bodiliness. The empathized subject would then be related to one and the same transcendent sphere as the empathizer; both must be able to find others as subjects related to the same transcendent. It is a question how far this goes, whether every transcendence for one ego that can legitimate itself as true must also be constituted for the other. And must all transcendences constituted for an ego join into an "all-nature" for it? Could not an optical world and an unconnected haptic one be constituted in one ego? The argument for the uniqueness of nature is complete in outline: compossible egos must be embodied in one nature. Two further questions are raised. Must every truth for one ego be constitutable for another, and must one ego's transcendences form a single nature? The second, the possibility of disconnected sensory worlds within one subject, is the question of the unity of the senses that Thing and Space and Ideen II address through the kinaesthetic system of the body.

More precisely (161.16–31): I can present another ego only if I have a transcendent body and present the other as a bodily-psychic ego, and I can assign it a determinate content of subjectivity as possibility only so far as I have foundations of analogization in myself. So in its essential content the other ego can only be a possible modification of my ego. Something can remain open only in the sense in which it is open for me that I might have experiences, kinds of sensation, I have not had but that might come by analogy with "unheard-of" experiences I occasionally have. The essence of every possible ego is represented by my own, the modifications, as to passive pregivens, not being at my free disposal but containing openness. This is evidently a necessary situation holding for every ego. The proposition that the essence of every possible ego is represented by mine is the eidetic basis of the fifth Cartesian Meditation: the other is given as an analogue of me, and the eidos ego is reached by variation of my own ego (Cartesian Meditations §34). The qualification about openness answers the difficulty of colours I cannot imagine: such qualities are open for the other in the way new sensations are open for me.

Imagining a foreign ego, I must distinguish three egos (161.32–162.7): the factual ego, I who in fact am; the modified ego into which I have re-fictioned myself as experiencing the body that I do not in fact experience but imagine, coinciding with me not only psychically but bodily, since my body enters phantasy in suitable modification and becomes the phantasy body of the phantasy-ego that "experiences the other in phantasy"; and the phantasized "foreign" ego with a body analogous to my fictively transformed body. I, the imaginer, carry out no actual empathy; "in phantasy" my fictive modified ego (2) carries out empathy into the second body of (3). The triple structure makes precise how fictive intersubjectivity is built. The empathizer in phantasy is a bodily variant of me, so every fictive other comes with a fictive body of mine and a shared fictive space in which both bodies are things.

If I imagine several subjects, ego (2) is the same in phantasy; it has several analogues of its body facing it and apprehends them all as bodies (162.8–18). So each, as presented and quasi-experienced, has its body not only in its transcendent sphere but each has one and the same body as "thing" in a transcendent sphere common to it and ego (2). So all these subjects, posited as coexisting, have a common transcendent stock if the whole transcendent sphere of ego (2) is assigned to each ego apperceived by it. Imagined others, like actual others, share the transcendent sphere of the ego that empathizes them, so a plurality of coexisting egos has a common world as a matter of essential structure.

How far the a priori necessities reach must be considered exactly; a formal and exact investigation is needed that omits nothing (162.19–163.3). Does imagining a nature, and with it a subject of its possible experience, mean imagining a totality of transcendent realities in general? Have I considered whether transcendent being "outside" nature might be thinkable for me? It is the basic thought of Kant's doctrine, wrong as Kant grounded and stated it, that sense data transcendently objectivated yield only objects lying in the one universal space-time form, and this is undoubtedly demonstrable (a note: firm concepts of the in-itself of transcendent objects are needed first). If this were proved, it would follow that all subjects that are to be able to coexist must be connected by an identical nature encompassed by that universal form, in which they must have their bodies; or, in slightly different words, all compossible subjects, which any subject can think as coexisting possibilities, must share one and the same nature. The decisive premise is identified with Kant's doctrine of space and time as the single form of outer intuition, stripped of its faculty-psychological grounding. If every transcendent objectivation of sense data falls in one spatiotemporal form, there is no transcendent being outside nature, and coexisting subjects must share it. The note links the premise to the concept of the in-itself, the theme of Text No. 12.

It is impossible that two different subjects have constituted different natures; they would all have to belong bodily to one world (163.3–18). It is not said that they must have fully developed experience of nature: they could be jellyfish. Empathy does not exclude that the empathized subject has only a remote analogy, despite the necessarily common essence on which the range of the analogy rests, as we know from the admittedly imperfect understanding of animals (a note: the analogy is one of the inner material essence). So a nature is thinkable that is common although the bodily subjects placed in it are as remote from the sense of nature, correlative to developed experience of nature, as jellyfish. It seems, then: a nature without any bodily beings is thinkable; then also a nature with bodies and so with any number of animal subjects at any stage of development, whose possibilities are not free but encompassed by an essential frame and its possible stages. The jellyfish of the marginal note in Text No. 6 (121) appears here in the text itself: a common nature does not require that all subjects in it have developed experience of it; primitive animals belong to the world although their own surroundings hardly constitute a nature. This qualifies the special thesis without abandoning it, and the tentative conclusion, that a nature without any bodily beings is thinkable, is immediately questioned.

It is questionable whether one may assert the first of these propositions, a nature without any bodiliness (163.19–164.10). I, considering, am this human being of this actual nature. In freely re-fictioning nature, can I not only shape my body differently but shape it arbitrarily, can I strike it out? If so, I would have a nature-experiencing bodiless ego, unable in principle to apperceive other subjects, that is, other bodies. One might think that if a plurality of possible subjects is imagined as a plurality of distinct possibilities, I could perform this re-fiction on each and have a plurality of bodiless subjects as coexisting possibility, each related to the nature it experiences, the same one, only unable to know it. That contradicts what was shown earlier. As soon as we present an ego bodiless, transposing ourselves into the interior of the at first bodily ego and annulling its body, it loses every difference from any bodiless re-fiction of my ego; it is no longer another ego but a re-fiction of my own, and so for all. We would lose compatible existence and have instead a manifold of identity-coincidence in conflict. Deriving it from a plurality of bodily egos does not help, since this is presentable to me only as bodily ego related to my transformed but bodily ego; if I think it bodiless, I have no plurality of bodies before me and so no plurality of subjects. The argument shows that bodies are the principle of individuation of egos as coexisting. Strip the others of their bodies and they collapse into variants of me, incompatible alternatives rather than coexisting others. So plurality of egos and embodiment are inseparable, and a world without bodies would be a world for at most one ego.

If a bodiless subject were thinkable, it would be thinkable only in conflict with thinkable bodily egos, as a limit case of the modification of my bodily ego in the system of its possible modifications (164.11–24). Positing this possibility, admitted unexamined, would strangely posit a possible ego that would necessarily be "absolutely solipsistic," tolerating no other ego beside it and making a second ego unthinkable as coexisting with it. A nature constituted in it would be absolutely solipsistic; no bodies would be possible in it as bearers of souls, only the corresponding body-things. If I gave up my body and kept my nature otherwise, I would keep the physical bodies, but they would only look "as before"; their being bodies would be senseless. The "absolutely solipsistic ego" is the eidetic limit that the solipsistic argument of Ideen I approaches. If the reduction could leave a consciousness without body, its world would contain no others, only lifeless shapes. The argument implies that a transcendental ego capable of having others must be one that can apperceive itself as embodied, the thesis of the Cartesian Meditations' primordial sphere, in which my own body is the first object constituted.

The whole of nature contains my body too (164.25–165.2). The bodilessness I impute to myself in re-fiction means either letting the physical body disappear, and it is a question how far that changes nature, or keeping it but taking away its character as body, and again the question is whether this makes sense, whether I can modify psychophysical relations and still keep experience of nature. That already leads to the question whether an absolutely solipsistic ego is a true possibility. Conversely, positing a plurality of subjects excludes a subject that would be wholly bodiless, experiencing a nature in a bodiless way, without eyes, ears. Equivalent is the case in which, considering pure possibilities, I take myself as ego (2), as bodily ego, since then the imagined environment of this ego is a nature that can at least contain bodies in any number and so many subjects. The two alternatives for bodilessness, removing the physical body or removing its "lived" character, both threaten the experience of nature itself, since perception requires organs. The question is left open whether pure solipsism is a genuine possibility, but its exclusion by any plurality is shown.

Considering the possibility of bodilessness, it seems easy at first to imagine (165.3–17). Nothing easier: as I can otherwise re-fiction my body, I can let it shrink to zero while the "images" of the environing objects remain unchanged. One can also urge that when I imagine a landscape freely, I am there as phantasy subject of perception, but not with a body in concrete bodily form; I can do so, but usually do not. Only the landscape appearance is concretely imagined and stands quasi-perceptually, seen; at least if I imagine it purely optically, I need no optical fiction of my body and still less a haptic one. The objection appeals to a phenomenological fact: imagined scenes often lack any imagined body of the viewer. It is the strongest support for the thought of a bodiless ego.

One may answer (165.18–33): I can let my body shrink completely and keep the images of things. But the question is what must adhere intentionally to these "images" for the ego to have in "truth" a nature over against it, a transcendent objectivity legitimating itself as existing. To the possibility of the nature phantasy gives me belongs the possibility of progressive legitimation, and the question is what belongs to it a priori, whether I consider possibilities of experienced nature obtained by re-fictioning my bodiliness or imagine a world in pure phantasy. Without looking, no optical legitimation; without touching, no haptic one. Kinaesthetic series with their own phenomenological character must run, connected with sensation series having presenting function. The answer moves from the static image to the conditions of legitimation. A nature requires progressive confirmation, confirmation requires kinaesthetic series, and kinaesthesis is the lived side of bodily movement. So a nature for an ego requires that ego's "I can move," which is the body as lived. This is the decisive argument of Ideen II §18 and of Thing and Space's analyses of kinaesthetic motivation, now enlisted in the proof of transcendental idealism.

But then the question is whether the kinaesthetic series, connected with the presenting data and appearance-modes, must be apperceivable and apperceived as with us, as movements of eyes, of a physical organ "eye," and touching as that of someone touching with hands or other organs (165.34–166.20). Evidently a special and difficult investigation is needed to analyse the appearance-modes of things in their subjective motivation, with the subjective circumstances, within pure phenomenology, and by phantasy variation and eidetic consideration to bring out the necessities for the possibility of a physical thinghood and nature in general, which impose laws both on the structure of experiencing subjectivity and on nature itself. Something wonderful seems to result: the possibility of a subject coexisting with me, who am absolutely given to myself, prescribes laws to my essential content and prescribes a nature common to us, at first in the very general form of a "common" sphere of transcendent objects, transcendent to each of us and yet identical and identifiable. Step by step one is driven into further transcendental investigations. The possibility of a nature is unthinkable without possible subjectivity fulfilling the conditions of its knowledge, and these conditions are very rich in content. The text ends with the "wonderful" result that inverts the solipsistic order of Ideen I. The possibility of another subject coexisting with me places conditions on my own essence: I must be embodied, and we must share a nature. The idealism of Ideen I is thus not refuted but enriched: the actual consciousness on which the world depends must be a community of embodied subjects, and the conditions of possibility of knowledge of nature, the theme of the whole volume, include body, kinaesthesis, and intersubjectivity.

## Appendix V. Correlation of the Existence of a Nature and the Existence of Nature-Experiencing Subjects

The appendix, from 1921, begins with a note on the reflecting subject (166.24–167.3). The epistemological subject reflecting on possibilities of natures constitutes for every possibility of a nature, as for every pure phantasy object, a possible subject, co-imagined, which is a phantasy modification of the epistemological subject and coincides with it. Now I am the epistemological subject, I this actual human being; but evidently what I establish for myself about such possibilities holds for every conceivable epistemological subject, and I see this by imagining any subject, a modification of myself or a second separate one, imagined in turn as imagining subject for the possibilities in question. I am then an epistemological subject of the second level, and the same holds iteratively to infinity. The iteration secures the universality of results reached by one reflecting ego: whatever I see about possible subjects, any possible reflecting subject would see, since I can imagine it as reflecting. This is how Husserl's egological method claims eidetic validity without solipsistic restriction.

Correlation of the existence of a nature and the existence of nature-experiencing subjects (167.4–17): I who in fact am cannot get rid of myself, and I see this for every possible ego; but also that every ego, imagined as subject of seeing something possible, must necessarily have co-imagined a modification of itself as subject of possible experience and thought of that possible. Taking as the possible a possible real, a nature, a world, it can be seen that the possible real and the possible experiencing subject of it are so interwoven that positing the existence of one entails the existence of the other, so that the existence of a real, of a world, is unthinkable without the co-existence of a subject of its possible experience. "They are essential correlates, and the existence of one correlate demands that of the other." The formula is the "existential correlation" of the running head. "I cannot get rid of myself" restates, in the idiom of re-fiction, the indubitability of Ideen I §46: every variation carries the varying ego along.

One can see it thus (167.18–37). When I, the actually reflecting ego, imagine a possible world in pure possibility, I live in this phantasy world, modified as phantasy-ego, necessarily. This phantasy-ego is determined and bound by the phantasy world only with respect to the possibility of experiencing just this world, which has its determinacy only through the sense-content of the quasi-perceptions in which it presents itself in phantasy. The quasi-perceptions belong to the phantasy-ego functioning as quasi-perceiving, and through their perceptual belief and open horizons they prescribe an infinite horizon of undetermined determinability, determinability by this phantasy-ego. The general structure of possible experiences, of the manifold of possible appearances of the same real and especially of this real already so determined perceptually, correlatively the ontic structure of the real, and the general structures of a subject of possible experience for this real, according to its capacities and their genesis: all this is scientifically investigable. Every closer determination of the imagined reality means a closer determination of the subject of possible experience of just this reality. The last sentence states the correlation as a principle of research: ontology of a possible world and phenomenology of its possible subject determine each other point for point. Ideen I §§149–152 propose the region as clue for the study of its constituting consciousness; here the correlation runs in both directions and includes the genesis of the subject's capacities.

Now I, the epistemological ego, posit that this imagined world exists (167.38–168.12). A hypothesis is either an actual hypothesis, the affair of an actual subject, or an imagined one, as a judgment is actual as an actual subject's or imagined as an imagined subject's. If I actually make the hypothesis that this world exists, what can and must be meant? Since I am subject of this hypothesis, for myself, and do not now live in phantasy, lost to myself and removed from my actuality, there is now for me myself and my actual surroundings, everything I find existing, immanent and transcendent, in living present, my past and past surroundings within my free recollections, my future, and the transcendent horizon I am conscious of as actual though not traversed. The distinction between imagined and actual hypothesis is the turning point of the argument. An actual hypothesis is made by me in my actual world, so it posits its object as belonging with my actuality; it is not a pure possibility but a candidate reality.

This domain of positing, of passive and perhaps in particulars active doxa in its modalities, is now extended by the hypothetical positing (168.13–29). If it conflicts here and there with my actual positings, hypothetical insights result: if this were actual, I and my given world would have to be different in this respect; and if my actuality is as it is, this cannot be.
