If there is no conflict, so far as determinate content on both sides founds one, the originally freely imagined and purely possible can fit into my actual world, as an "ideal landscape" into the actual world with its actual landscapes, though the closer determination of the open horizons of my experienced actuality and of the hypothetically added may now be bound, since the meant being is actual only if further experiences remain harmonious and a common, connected harmony is required. The "ideal landscape" (the term of landscape painting for a composed, not depicted, scene) is the counterpart of the Bacchic procession on the Weender Strasse in Text No. 6: one fits my world, the other conflicts with it. An actual hypothesis is thus a provisional extension of my world, bound to its harmony.

It is evident that I cannot posit the ideal landscape as actual, which means as agreeing with my experientially given actuality, without at once positing myself as subject of possible experience of it (168.30–169.9). Legitimating the ideal possibility required only pure phantasy, in which my actual ego was represented by a re-fictioned one. Stating or grasping the possibility as actual ego meant only that I had produced this intuitive fiction; my actual world was "there" for me but unrelated to the imagined world. Not a new world was posited, but a possibility in the sense of a free phantasy correlate. But as soon as I say "suppose this world is actual," I have changed my act essentially: the phantasy possibility, "ideal," "pure," has become real. The positing that makes it contains the intention of being coexisting with the content of my universal positings of actuality, with my actual surroundings. Having had the pure possibility evidently given with the pure phantasy, I do not yet have the real possibility, though the pure is contained in it; it needs legitimation, or consideration of its necessary conditions. The difference between pure and real possibility is now located in the act: pure possibility is the correlate of phantasy without relation to my world; real possibility is the correlate of a supposition of coexistence with my actual world. This is the clearest phenomenological account of real possibility in the volume, and it explains why Ideen I §47 can define the real possibilities of the world as "motivated" in my experience: they are possibilities of coexistence with what I actually experience.

The condition of legitimating the positing of the ideal landscape as real possibility is evidently that I myself could experience it: that the imagined ego, a re-fiction of my factual ego anyway, is not a mere re-fiction into a purely possible ego (that I, who am, could be among other possibilities), but a positional, positing presentification of my actual ego in a transformation obtainable from my present ego, namely if I actually experienced this landscape (169.10–29). "In other words: it is I myself who would necessarily have to be able to experience that ideal landscape if it were actual." The real possibility of its actuality would mean that I could present to myself a possible path of experience, starting from the circle of my actual experiences, that led me to it. I, who now and so far have experienced other actuality, can, without giving that up so far as it can be rightly maintained and without attacking myself as actual ego with my actual stream, think a continuation of it, and so a further development of my ego, that would yield the experience of that landscape and its harmonious continuation. The distinction between re-fiction (imagining myself otherwise) and positional presentification (imagining my own future or possible course) is the decisive refinement. Real possibility is a possible future of my actual life, a path of experience from my present. The special thesis is therefore the claim that the real is what lies on possible paths of the actual ego's further life, and Ideen I §47's "possible path from my actual experience" (which the 1913 book compresses into the concept of motivation) receives its full sense.

This self-thinking draws new hypothetical lines only into the open horizons left by my previous experience (169.30–170.15). As in the always presumptive positing of the actuality of experience that is mine there lies a presumptive rule for my course of experience, for possibilities and actualities in all temporal modes and so for my subjectivity in its development, so in the assumptive positing of a real possibility for me lies an assumption of further presumptive rules for courses of experience corresponding to this possibility. Every new system of possibilities of experience must agree with the system already prescribed by actual and harmonious experience. Correlatively: I am always the same ego and have one stream, with apperceptions developed in it, or possible for me as habitual ego, which include presumptive rules. "But I can only be the same if all these regulations agree." They need not: experience can conflict with experience. Not everything imaginable is really possible. One must go beyond these generalities: under "real possibilities" one may consider immanent and transcendent ones; one must ask how transcendence and nature relate; and one must consider whether it holds that if an epistemological ego is to function as subject of pure possibility of nature, if any ego is to be able to imagine a nature, it must itself have experienced a nature, so that a rule is prescribed in its actuality: natural apperception must have developed in it. The identity of the ego is tied to the agreement of its regulations: an ego whose apperceptive rules conflicted irreconcilably would not remain one ego, which is the reverse side of world-annihilation. The final question restates the genetic condition of Text No. 8: imagining a nature presupposes having experienced one, so even pure possibilities of nature are anchored genetically in an actual experience of nature.

A supplement, "the possibility of the coexistence of several egos" (170.16–24): if another ego is to be beside me, coexisting, I must have a body and be able to find in my nature a second body as that of this alter ego. So the existence of a nature for me is, as to its possibility, essentially dependent on the existence of other egos; every ego of a community prescribes something for a common nature, or the existence of every ego prescribes a rule for the possible experiential content of every other. "Monads are not independent of one another in content." The supplement states the intersubjective form of the correlation. The dependence runs not only from world to ego but from ego to ego through the shared world: each monad's existence constrains the others' experience. This is the anti-Leibnizian point of Husserl's monadology, monads with windows, which the fifth Cartesian Meditation (§§55–56) develops.

## Appendix VI. Existence of Nature. Idea of the Ontological In-Itself and the Ego

In eidetic generality, a natural thing is necessarily a thing in an infinite actual or possible universe, in infinite space and time in which other things are or could be; all things existing simultaneously are causally related (170.28–32). The appendix, also from 1921, starts from the regional ontology of nature as Ideen II §§15–16 sets it out: thing implies universe, simultaneity implies causal interaction.

Correlatively, a natural thing is necessarily given for some ego with an open horizon of possible, perhaps actual, unknown things that co-influence it, though at first in unknown ways and imperceptible measure (170.33–171.5). "A thing exists" therefore means: an open universe exists, in principle "open," undetermined, unknown, open to knowledge. The causal openness of the thing becomes an epistemic openness: to posit one thing is to posit an open world. This is the horizon structure of Ideen I §27 grounded in the causal sense of thinghood.

"Existence of this nature," or any, is the index of a rule of consciousness infinitely ambiguous in principle for every knower, but supposed to be unique in itself (171.6–24). Better: existence of an object of experience is the index of a merely presumptive rule, thought and to be thought with indeterminacies as open determinabilities to infinity. In the belief in the object, in the positing of being and perhaps the predicative assertion of existence, "lies" the belief that the object determined with a certain core-content, but further determinable to infinity through its prescribed horizons, and also (as an open possibility) determinable otherwise, even to be redetermined in its core, has before it an idea lying at infinity: the object meant as intentional, as noematic, has before it an object in itself that is an idea. This is not to be thought as the practical telos, "final goal," of a "series-system" of determinations that never need be or can be changed or overthrown in further determination, but, which needs its own description, as a mode of determination progressing to infinity, that of approximation to ideal limits. The doctrine of the thing as "index" of a rule of consciousness (Ideen I §§135, 149–150) is now refined: the rule is presumptive, and the object in itself is an idea in the sense of Ideen I §143. The new element is the explicit rejection of a finite telos: the in-itself is not an eventually reached final set of determinations but a limit of approximation, the model of convergence in mathematics.

This is the idea of the ontological in-itself of every natural object in its necessary epistemological form as ideal system of determinations in an ideal sphere of approximations (171.25–31). The situation is still imperfectly described, since every thing has an infinite spatiotemporal-causal horizon of other possible things and its determination depends on them, so the idea of a finite telos is untenable. The holism of causal nature reinforces the rejection of a finite goal: since each thing's determination depends on all others, complete determination of one thing would require completion of the whole universe, which is excluded.

Here lies one great task: to develop systematically the ontological idea of a thing and thingly connection, of a possible nature, and in relation to it the logical idea of a nature-determining process as conceptually determining (171.32–172.10). Nature is related to any knowing subject that posits nature in its experience and takes cognizance of it progressively, always with open horizons, and determines it in processes of experience-logical, natural-scientific thought. The knowing subject is the theme in respect of its acts of knowledge, whose goal is the truth or in-itself of a natural object and nature in general: first, the determination of the ideal goal at infinity, the in-itself, realizable only "imperfectly" in any actual act; second, the investigation of the acts of knowledge, the ordered productions of the knower, in which "imperfect" attempts are realized as necessary stages on a necessary line toward the in-itself, all normatively, as true paths and results. The programme pairs an ontology of nature with a logic of natural science, both related to the knowing subject. It is the "logic of experience" that Husserl planned since the 1910s and that Formal and Transcendental Logic (1929) presents as the transcendental grounding of the sciences.

This is the theme of a rational methodology of natural science, as the idea of a natural science in general, or of methodology first as science of the true method of experiential cognizance and second as true method of progressively higher conceptual and theoretical determination of the in-itself, for which the sense of the in-itself and of experience and theoretical determination of it, with all the infinite connections of ideas, must first be developed (172.11–27). This includes consideration of the possibilities of non-being, refutation instead of confirmation, illusion, in the realm of mere experience. It is a question what investigations arise besides methodology proper, noematically, in the attitude toward acts of knowledge. Methodology proper stands on the ground of a generally confirming belief in experience, a positing of nature that is only to be raised to secure progressive knowledge and science. But what stands there as existing can also not be, can be doubtful. The distinction between methodology, which presupposes the belief in nature, and transcendental investigation, which considers the possibility of non-being, marks the boundary between theory of science and transcendental phenomenology. The latter begins where the general thesis is put in question, as in Ideen I §§30–32.

Could nature as a whole not be (172.28–41)? Does not every abandonment of a natural being presuppose other being taken as existing? Is not a nature always there, an actual space with actual things, even when particulars are rejected as unreal? How could abandonment of nature simply and in general be motivated? Can the non-being of nature legitimate itself? How can an experience of nature, in which nature is there by a certain harmony and progressive confirmation, change so that nature is no longer "there"? Would not experience of nature itself, consciousness of transcendent objectivity, have to dissolve completely? And does such dissolution, "a complete madness of the ego, as one may call it," mean that nature is not? Does it not mean that perhaps only this ego is mad? These questions are the most penetrating criticism of Ideen I §49 in the volume. §49 argued that the world could be annihilated by a dissolution of the harmony of experience into a "tumult" of sensations. Husserl now asks whether such dissolution would show the non-being of nature or only the madness of one ego. Non-being of nature as a whole is not legitimable in the way the non-being of a particular thing is, since particular cancellations presuppose the world as background; and total dissolution of one ego's experience might be pathology rather than evidence. The questions do not refute §49's modal claim, that the world's non-being is thinkable, but they challenge its claim to describe a legitimating experience of non-being.

The nature as it is in itself is the goal of a knowing subject's acts of knowledge, but we must consider from the start that an open plurality of subjects can be related knowingly to the same nature, that it is an intersubjectively identical in-itself (173.1–8). If nature is accessible to knowledge by a plurality of subjects, and subjects are accessible to each other's knowledge, one knowing subject can rightly know nature and also know that mad subject. The answer to the question just raised lies in intersubjectivity. The dissolution of one ego's experience does not annihilate nature if other egos continue to experience it harmoniously and can recognize the mad ego as mad. The world's actuality is thus anchored in the community, not in any single stream, and the annihilation thought-experiment of Ideen I, carried out for one ego, proves less than it claims unless carried out for all.

But this madness cannot be arbitrary (173.9–15). Even we do not experience the same nature in the same mode of appearance. The truth of nature itself, as identical intersubjective nature, is non-intuitive; intuitive nature is merely subjective. Anomalous experience is experience of a nature that can still be maintained harmoniously but has merely subjective significance, not the objective one that is intersubjectively identical. The concept of abnormality is the key to placing deviant experience within the common world: the colour-blind or the mad experience a nature with its own harmony, but its significance is subjective, measured against the intersubjective nature. The claim that "the truth of nature is non-intuitive" places objectivity in the physical determination of the common world, the view of Ideen I §52 now given its intersubjective ground.

Can an ego objectively knowable by others experience a nature not identifiable with the nature others experience (173.16–21)? And if it has no experience of nature at all but some transcendent experience, under what norms does that stand? Must every transcendent experience it has count at least as a lower stage of experience of nature? The questions return to Text No. 9's desideratum, the uniqueness of nature, now posed for egos known by others: if I know another ego objectively, it is in my world, and its experience must be either of the same nature or abnormal relative to it.

Here we come to transcendental questions (173.22–32). It must become clear where the transcendental problems spring from, and how the ontological-scientific-theoretical (logical-noematic) direction of research joins with the noetic, or how they differ. A firmly enclosed platform, so to speak, we have on the ground of the given nature, and then of a nature in general with the same categorial framework as ours, related to a plurality of humans placed in it as knowing subjects. But now one must dismantle and generalize, formalize, and regard the knowing subjects not merely as subjects of "action" but as subjects of "consciousness." The closing methodological note distinguishes two levels of inquiry. The "platform" is the natural and scientific world with human knowers in it, the level of methodology and of Text No. 7's worldly correlation. Transcendental inquiry requires a "dismantling" (Abbau) of this platform and a shift from knowers as agents to knowers as consciousness, the step from the anthropological to the transcendental that Ideen I takes in §§33 and 50. The term Abbau will become central in the later genetic texts.

## No. 10. The Causal Inference from Immediately Given Consciousness to an External World

The text, from 1921, opens with the thesis it attacks (174.4–9): only my consciousness is immediately given to me; the external world is given only mediately, one says, through appearances in consciousness. Inferences from the immediately given to the mediately assumed are made as probability inferences on the ground of the natural world of experience; they are causal inferences. The inference theory, rejected at the start of Text No. 1 and in Ideen I §52, is taken up a last time, now with the full apparatus of the 1920s: reduction, monadology, appresentation, passive synthesis.

If we presuppose the world as given by general experience, we find psychophysical beings in it: bodies in physical nature and psychic life, consciousness, bound to them (174.10–21). All this is given by experience, and all experience is "immediate"; original experience is perception. We perceive physical things, bodies, humans. In the unity of this objective experience we find consciousness, including perception and experience causally connected with physical objects and processes. But does one have priority of immediacy over the other, if immediacy is defined by "perception"? The first move removes the asymmetry on which the inference theory rests. Within the world, both physical and psychic are perceived, so neither is more immediate.

The psychic can be perceived as well as the physical, and empirical causal inferences run both ways (174.22–27). But is the physical not privileged, the first, founding reality? Is consciousness given in the empirical world as a reality before everything physical and always separable from it? Within the natural attitude the physical is in fact founding: psychic life is given as the life of bodies, as Ideen II §§18 and 36 describe. So if anything, natural experience favours the physical, the reverse of what the inference theory claims.

But now perception is taken as pure phenomenon and the world as noematic phenomenon, included in pure consciousness (174.28–33). It lies in the sense of the realist inference from consciousness to an "external world" that it takes its stand on the Cartesian cogito, and so, if carried out correctly, performs phenomenological reduction. The decisive diagnosis: the inference theory needs the reduction to secure its premise, the indubitable given of consciousness, but the reduction transforms the world into a noematic phenomenon within that consciousness, so the inference has nowhere to go.

Does it make sense to bring this pure consciousness into causal relation with objects of nature and to make it something thingly in nature (175.1–15)? Does it make sense to posit pure consciousness as a "reality," as a totality of realities over against nature? By the reduction the whole world with all its stock, soul, body, dead nature and all unknown being, is posited as phenomenon of pure consciousness. Physical nature is by its sense a unity constituted in the immanent, whose true being consists in the rational confirmability of appearances. Does it not belong to this that among the confirming appearances are human appearances in which empirically bound ego-consciousness is posited, with the possibility for each such ego of finding itself as pure ego and pure consciousness with corresponding world-phenomena? Causality is a category of reality; pure consciousness is not a reality but the field in which realities are constituted. Ideen I §49 says the same: between consciousness and reality there is no causal relation, consciousness being "a self-enclosed connection of being into which nothing can penetrate and out of which nothing can escape."

So, one will say, pure consciousness is after all entangled in causal connections (175.16–28). The other subject's pure consciousness is assumed empirically on the basis of appearances, and so I can assume my pure consciousness with my body in my world of appearances. But: excluding the world and returning to consciousness of the world, the world as posited, with presumptive evidence, including myself as human and other humans as mere phenomena, intentional positednesses in pure consciousness, I find only motivations under a priori laws of motivation, which are the correlates of the being of nature, first physical nature. The objection is that I find other pure consciousnesses through bodies, as if pure consciousness were placed in nature. The answer restates the reduction: in the reduced field there are only motivations, not causal relations.

Empirical truths are then mere indices for motivations of consciousness occurring in pure consciousness according to rules I can see and understand (175.29–176.6). Where does pure consciousness, the solipsistic one, my pure consciousness, transcend itself? Only where it posits foreign consciousness in empathy. Then I first see that not only a rule of my consciousness is motivated but a consciousness not mine, with regulated courses harmonizing with regulated courses of mine: a common objective world, the coordination of foreign consciousness with a body as thing that is a unity of appearances of my pure consciousness and his. My consciousness is coordinated with my body, likewise intersubjectively constituted. In all, a certain coordination of pure consciousnesses (with pure ego-subjects) with one another that enables each to know not only itself but others. The one genuine transcendence of pure consciousness is empathy: other pure consciousness is the only being not constituted as a unity in my stream that I can nonetheless posit. Ideen I §§135 and 150 call real objects "indices" of rules of consciousness; here even empirical truths are such indices, and the only "beyond" is another consciousness, which corresponds to the fifth Cartesian Meditation's account of the alter ego as the first transcendence.

In the natural attitude I have the objective world, externally experienced, immediately existing and theoretically determined, and I draw many inferences from given to non-given existence, physical to physical and physical to psychic (176.7–21). In the phenomenological attitude, which is in a sense solipsistic, not empirically but in that I now think as pure ego, we recognize generally that a pure consciousness of the empirical style may posit, in rightful empirical rational motivation, a nature and foreign subjectivity and with them nature and world as intersubjective objectivity; and further that a causal inference from pure consciousness to nature is nonsense, since things and causality are only correlates of pure consciousness. The "in a sense solipsistic" character of the reduction is now explained: it is methodological, the stance of thinking as pure ego, not a thesis that only I exist. Within it, the world's existence is legitimated by motivation, not inferred by causality. This is the final form of the argument that Text No. 1 began.

A supplement follows (176.22–177.5). In the phenomenological attitude I find my pure ego, my pure monad, and can investigate it; in it, communicatively-phenomenologically, I find the universe of pure egos and their conscious relatedness, the specifically monadological relatedness and unity. Returning to the natural attitude I again have the objective world, in which I am this human being and others are humans; each of us "has" a pure consciousness, a mind, a soul, "bound" to a body. The pure consciousness, purely monadological being, is the being indicated by the other's empirical body, "soul." The indicating body is in this function for me nothing but something constituted as a thing in my pure consciousness (whether "objectively" constituted is irrelevant here), and in the phenomenological attitude I have, without positing existence, a bridge into the other consciousness. The foreign pure consciousness gives itself, in transition to the natural attitude, as what the indicating body, now posited outright, indicates as soul; and in this soul this body too is, in pure view, constituted, as in every other soul. In the natural positing of the world the foreign body and all bodies are included like the rest of the physical world, and to every body of the world a soul then "belongs." The relation between the two attitudes is described as a change of apperception. The other's body "indicates" a pure consciousness; in the natural attitude this indication becomes the relation of soul to body. "Monadological relatedness" is the intersubjective field that the later Cartesian Meditations call transcendental intersubjectivity.

The indication becomes a real relation in the world, a real belonging, by which humans constitute themselves as psychophysical world objects (177.6–25). The pure consciousness of the phenomenological attitude is indeed identical with the soul, only that what is posited in the transcendental attitude absolutely, as being in and for itself and with the world excluded, now receives an apperceptive relation to a constituted object experienced as existing, a relation of being. Its pure consciousness can be indicated in a regulated way by a transcendent object of experience, first as foreign consciousness, and so have an apperceptive layer by which it constitutes itself as transcendent, as belonging empirically to something transcendent in nature; only thereby can foreign consciousness be there for us as human. This transcendence is not cancelled but excluded in the way of monadology. So the relation between soul and pure consciousness is clear; the same holds for one's own consciousness, one's own monad: if I experience my body as a thing in the world, my conscious life and ego are the soul of the body, and this same soul becomes in phenomenological reduction the pure monad. The identity thesis is the mature form of §14 of Text No. 6. Soul and pure consciousness are the same life under two apperceptions, the worldly and the transcendental; the reduction does not separate two entities but suspends an apperceptive layer. The formula "not cancelled but excluded" is the epoché of Ideen I §§31–32 applied to the soul. The Crisis (§§53–54, 72) states the same "identity in difference" of psychological and transcendental subjectivity.

What about the "inference" from the fact of consciousness or the ego cogito to an "external world"? Why is it ridiculous (177.26–27)? The rhetorical question introduces three reasons, which summarize Husserl's mature critique of the Cartesian route to the world.

First (177.28–33): of the existence of the soul I have no "indubitable" evidence; I have a soul only through transcendent experience, given as soul of the body. What I have absolute evidence of in introspection is only the momentary cogito in itself, and that is not my psychic experience; it is so only through the psychophysical apperception, which presupposes the empirical positing of the body. The inference theory's premise, that my mind is given indubitably, is false: my mind as a reality is known as transcendently as my body. What is indubitable is the pure cogito, which is not a part of the world from which one could infer other parts.

Second (177.34–39): the thought is thoroughly wrong that a piece of the world, I myself, my psychic interior, is immediately given and that I infer onward from there. At the beginning stands not the positing of the ego, which is an artifact, but the positing of the experienced and first the perceived world; and perception has its own original evidence, only not absolute. The phrase "the positing of the ego is an artifact" is striking. The reflectively isolated ego is a product of philosophical method, while the original situation is the perceived world. This is the stance of the Crisis, which begins from the pregiven lifeworld rather than the Cartesian ego, and it corrects the impression, which Ideen I §46 might give, that the world is reached from a prior certainty of the self.

Third (177.40–178.26): experience, with its appresentation, must be sharply separated from inference. Seeing a thing, I do not infer from the visible side to the invisible; it belongs to the essence of experience that the apperception of the front is co-experience, co-perception of the back, generally appresented indeterminately. Within appresentation I can judge and infer: approaching, I see that the uniformly red colour originally appresented continues to appear uniform, so I can infer that it remains so, that it is "really" uniform red. In the continuous perceptual process there is, before the inference, continuing confirming appresentation and continuously self-motivating anticipation of "uniformly red"; but I can grasp it conceptually, judge, and infer. So too I can experience another's subjectivity, and harmony can be maintained by confirmation; I can formulate experiential inferences: since the other behaves as I saw and presupposed, I have judged him rightly and can presuppose further. Outside the continuity of experience I can formulate inductive inferences, which may be preceded by empirical expectations passively motivated by repeated events under similar circumstances. It is evident that all experiential inferences presuppose experience, and inferences from empirical-worldly existence to other existence presuppose transcendent experience. The term "appresentation," which the fifth Cartesian Meditation (§50) makes central for empathy, here covers the back side of a thing as well. The sharp distinction between appresentation and inference finally refutes the inference theory: what seems to be inference in perception is a non-inferential anticipation, and real inference is always built on it.

All inferences move necessarily on a homogeneous ground of experience (178.27–179.2). Inferences from the absolute ego cogito, or starting on the ground of phenomenological "experience," can in principle not lead into the "transcendent." The transcendent is given immediately by external experience, which must confirm itself as experience; and if it can already have validity, however limited (this validity lies in the simple fact that we experience and find transcendent being there), mediate inference can begin. To try to infer from the ego cogito, say from the "phenomenon" of perception, to the right of perception or the existence of the perceived makes no sense at all: "I would like to see the formulation of such an inference, if purely and intelligibly stated!" From having certain experiences I can infer that I will have others, motivated by them under the transcendental circumstances; but that an object will be or is "outside me" I cannot infer; it is really nonsense if I take "me" as the ego. The principle of homogeneity is a precise logical point. Inference connects premises and conclusions of the same kind; from data of reflection one can infer only further data of reflection, motivated experiences. The transcendent is not a further datum but the intentional sense of experience, so it cannot be a conclusion. The challenge to formulate the inference is a challenge Husserl knew could not be met: any formulation would either smuggle in transcendent premises or yield only expectations of experience.

The first experience already has its sense-correlate in "a thing is there," and further experience its further correlates (179.3–12). To infer I must be in the attitude of experience and infer from already given existence to other existence, not from absolute consciousness to existence. "The transcendental ego has no outside; that is completely senseless." Only the empirical ego has an outside, simply because it is posited in the transcendental ego as a member of the spatiotemporal world, as body and as animated body, and the soul of this body as knowing knows its outside, everything in the world that is not this soul placed in it. The sentence "the transcendental ego has no outside" is the most compressed statement of transcendental idealism in the volume. It does not mean that the transcendental ego is a box containing everything; it means that "outside" is a spatial-worldly relation that holds between realities in the world, so it cannot hold between the transcendental ego and the world. Ideen I §49's "self-enclosed connection of being" and the warnings against the box image in Text No. 6 (§§6, 14) say the same.

Nonsensical, if one understands the doctrine of constitution, is the doctrine that sense data carry an innate "instinctive" belief in external thinghood, only filled out in content by "experience" (179.13–19). Nonsensical is every doctrine of unconscious, or conscious but unformulated, unconceptualized, prelogical inferences from pure content of consciousness to a reality transcending consciousness. Two variants of the inference theory are dismissed: the instinctive belief of Brentano and Stumpf (Appendix II), and Helmholtz's unconscious inference. Both treat the intentional sense of perception as something added to sense data, whereas the theory of constitution shows it to be the apprehension through which data present anything at all.

Originally constituted is the unity of the monad as a purely inner connection, a "synthetic unity," or rather "originally pre-constituted," which implies that arbitrary objectivation is possible (179.20–33). If one calls "inferences" every kind of passing by motivation from one existence to another, perhaps conceptually in causal judgments, there is already a field of inferences here. The immanent connection is a unity of motivation. An emerging recollection points to new possible recollections: "now that must come, then that." Before the intuitions of recollection appear, the non-intuitive expectations are already directed, and fulfilment comes motivated. Association in the immanent sphere also belongs to it by essential law and so makes "inferences" possible. The analysis of passive synthesis (Hua XI, 1920–1926) supplies the vocabulary: association and motivation make up a field of quasi-inferences within immanence. This concedes the realist a grain of truth, that consciousness is shot through with motivated anticipation, but places it within the monad, where it constitutes rather than infers.

But one must avoid idle talk (179.34–180.9). The immanent realm of experience is a continuous synthesis in the form of immanent time and through and through a connection of motivation, of "because-so." Inductive inference is a procedure of judgment that, in relation to a ground of experience given beforehand, though with unknown horizons, is causal predication, inferring from contents already known the existence of new, unknown ones. Before all conceptually thinking judgment lies the constant motivation, the passive excitation of tendencies of expectation and also the striving or free willing to follow certain lines of expectation and to clarify the unclear and determine the undetermined. The distinction between pre-predicative motivation and predicative inference is the structure of Experience and Judgment. Induction presupposes a pregiven ground of experience, so it cannot establish that ground.

Ego-life is a synthesis insofar as it constitutes itself in itself, in relation to the ego, as a unity of life (180.10–30). But it belongs to the essence of monads that they constitute an objective world, even before they "consciously" set an immanent time over against themselves; a factual world. This constitution is noetically, according to the immanent constituting experiences, a connection of motivation, but ontically, according to the positing of identity achieved in developed intentionality, an infinite and universal connection of identities that prescribe each other's existence, standing in the unity of a "synthetic" connection, a connection of because-so. A transcendent objectivity can constitute itself only synthetically. The synthetic lies in the forms of intuition and their laws, in the constitution of phantoms and even more of causality in the usual sense, the constitution of reality. In general this is the ground of all inductive inferences concerning realities. Inferring from realities to realities presupposes a universal unity of reality, of the world. To regard the relation of constituting consciousness to constituted real unity as a real inference or a real motivation is nonsense. The parallel between noetic motivation and ontic causality is the parallelism of Ideen I §§88–98 applied to the unity of the world: motivation in consciousness corresponds to causality in the world, but the two are not related to each other causally or inferentially. "Phantoms," the merely spatial-sensory thing-schemata of Ideen II §15, are the intermediate level between sensation and causal reality. The final sentence closes the inference theory's last loophole: even "motivation" is not the relation between consciousness and world, since motivation connects experiences, while constitution relates experiences to their correlate.

A world was there for me (180.31–181.15). I constantly experienced "it" in waking consciousness, constantly saw things and processes in the consciousness of existence, and they were there for me in a universal synthetic unity, a nature, a world. Waking from sleep, I found this world again, with a new stretch of actually experienced existence as unbroken continuation of the existence experienced, now remembered, before, supplemented by mediating stretches of which I could convince myself indirectly by prescribed reconstructive methods. In this world I also find others and come to understanding with them about it as subjects for whom it is as continuously there as for me. I had to cross out many posited things as illusion or remodel what was already experienced as existing. But every such remodelling or crossing out preserves a unity of world-positing: if what was experienced did not exist, something else did in the world-connection at that place in space and time, something that could have been experienced and that belongs objectively, in this being-otherwise, to the unity of the world. The description of waking after sleep shows that the world's continuity survives gaps in experience through reconstruction, which is the answer of Text No. 7 to the world before humans, applied to everyday life. The claim that every cancellation preserves the unity of the world restates Text No. 6 §8: illusions confirm the world.

All change is bound here to an idea and form (181.16–33). I experience a world, but my experience is not always correct; there is a world in itself, a unity of possible correct experience. Underlying it is the idea of a thoroughly harmonious experience that would never need to be crossed out or changed, an experience I do not have nor anyone among us, but an experiencing ego could have. Or, more correctly: underlying it is the idea that all experiences of all egos in their communicative connection must be combinable into a synthetic unity of harmonious experience, with corresponding changes of the intentional horizons and expectation-theses accruing to them by virtue of this connection, which is to be established. "The world that is experienced there, and so posited by the ego, exists in truth; it is an equivalent and correlate of the validity of this idea." But whether the world exists is not absolutely certain. The constitution of nature means only an intentional prescription of this idea as an anticipation. The two formulations of the idea of the world in itself mark the shift from Ideen I to the intersubjective theory. The first, an ideal single experiencer whose experience never needs correction, is close to Ideen I §143's idea of a complete thing-givenness. The second, preferred as "more correct," is the idea of the harmonization of all actual egos' experiences. The world in itself is then the correlate of the validity of a communal idea, and its existence remains presumptive, as Ideen I §46 says, but the presumption now concerns the communicative convergence of many streams.

Accordingly, the question of grounding the existence of the world has good sense, but only as grounding the existence of a world constituted in immanence as intentional and, relative to it, "ideal" unity, constantly anticipated, transcendent in this sense, "but not as grounding of a mythology of things in themselves." Only on the ground of phenomenological-transcendental idealism do we have a clear question free of countersense (181.34–182.3). The epistemological question survives, transformed. It is not whether a hidden in-itself exists behind experience, a question Husserl now calls mythology, but whether the anticipated unity of all experience holds. Transcendental idealism is presented as the condition of a sensible epistemology, not as its rival.

It can be seen essentially that already in the passivity of progressing world-constitution, the success of this constitution, its continued confirmation, including the successful exclusion of discord and its replacement by harmony, has the value of a confirmation and strengthening of the general thesis concerning the world as universe (182.4–34). Subsequent thought can draw inferences here and formulate a principle of inference which, on the basis of past experience or rather the general formulation that experience has so far not only given the being of the world but confirmed it harmoniously, weighs the force of anticipation for the future and so for the true being of the world relative to the past. Natural experience, apart from points of discontinuity like doubt, has been empirical certainty. The knower no longer simply stands on this certainty, no longer goes along with it naively, no longer takes the existence of the experienced and of the world for granted. He asks this certainty about its "force," the force of its "right," and by freely voicing the possibilities co-motivated by occurrences of illusion, he converts, as judge, naive certainty into a "probability," a probability of a special kind, since ordinary probabilities occur within the naturally certain positing of the world. What is needed is special precision and concept-formation to determine the sense of the "infinite" probability of the world in its motivation purely from experience. The text ends by giving the inference theory its legitimate place. There is an inference concerning the world, but it is an inductive assessment of the force of the general thesis on the basis of its record of confirmation, carried out by a reflective judge within experience, not a causal inference from consciousness to a hidden cause. The probability it yields is "infinite" and of a special kind, since all ordinary probabilities presuppose the world. This is the reflective counterpart of Ideen I §46's "presumptive" world: the world's being is not certain, but its presumption is backed by the whole course of experience.

## No. 11. On the Doctrine of Transcendental Idealism. The Relation of Fiction and Experience. Free and Experience-Bound Phantasy. Possible Worlds of Factual Temporal Objects and the Knowing Subjects Compossible with Them

The text, probably from 1921, asks: is every conceivable object within the ideal range of knowledge of every conceivable subject (183.7–9)? The question is restricted at first to individual temporal objects. The question generalizes Text No. 8's thesis that every subject can see every eidetic object. For ideal objects the answer is yes; the text shows that for individual objects it is no, and that this is the logical form of transcendental idealism.

If we think any being as purely ideal possibility, imagining it without imagining it into an actuality fixed for us and so without connecting it with facts fixed for us, it is unthinkable without a possible knowing subject that could experience and determine it (183.10–33). We who think the ideally possible object are by no means the possible knowers to be co-thought as its necessary correlates. This object is our free fiction; as such it does not belong to our actual world, to the totality for which we are partly actual, partly possible knowers. A freely imagined object we can never experience and determine as to its truths as actually valid, for what is given in experience is separated from the domain of free fiction "by unbridgeable abysses." The fictum can be re-fictioned, and every re-fictioned one has the same equally justified "mode of being": it is equally non-being, or quasi-being, "ideal possibility of being," which can never become actuality, the actuality that binds our experience and judgment. No fictum can ever be established by us in later experience as actually existing; the only thing possible a priori is that we find in the actual world an object entirely like our fictum. The thesis that a fiction can never become an experienced actuality is a sharp formulation of the gulf between pure and real possibility. At most a real object can match a fiction; it cannot be the fiction realized, since the fiction belongs to a quasi-world of a quasi-subject. This applies, by extension, to the realist's unexperienced world in itself: if it is a pure possibility, it can never become the world of our experience.

Conversely, we call every actual thing, everything found or findable in actual experience, ideally possible only insofar as an exactly corresponding fictum can be constructed for it a priori (184.1–27). We perform the "neutrality modification," de-actualizing the actual experience and every actual doxic experience, bringing it as it were into coincidence with an exactly corresponding phantasy experience with phantasy doxa, treating it as an "image," and "looking away" from the actuality of the doxa, which does not thereby vanish, and from our actual ego as its subject. Thereby we, the actual subjects, are only subjects of the consciousness of possibility, and so for the ideally possible itself, not knowers of the possible knowledge of the being that hovers before us as possible. So it is generally when we consider a being in "pure thought," present it intuitively as pure possibility, and determine its true being "in" phantasy. What "presenting intuitively" means for us actual, imagining subjects contains an experiencing, perceiving "as if" "in" phantasy. When we say that "in" phantasy we stand before the object, look at it, move our eyes, touch, measure and weigh it, "we" are named as actual subjects practising modified activities in phantasy, but "in" phantasy we are ourselves fictive subjects, removed from actuality by fictive "transformation." The "neutrality modification" is the operation of Ideen I §§109–114. Its effect is precisely stated: when I consider an actual thing as possible, I am no longer its knower but the subject of a consciousness of possibility, while "in" the phantasy a fictive subject does the quasi-perceiving. The two levels, actual imaginer and fictive perceiver, are the double ego of Text No. 9.

The necessary "coincidence" that the transition from the experiencing attitude of actuality, in which we are conscious of ourselves and posit ourselves as actual and as actually imagining, to the attitude toward the pure subjects in phantasy as those perceiving and thinking in it, involves, must not deceive us, nor the way of speaking it permits, that "we" "as it were" perceive "in" phantasy (184.28–185.4). Even the assumption that we experience a pure possibility, something purely fictive, is nonsense. It is a countersense of a quite peculiar type: not a countersensical assumption like a round square (a material countersense), but one that lies in the incompatibility of experienced A and fictioned A in the unity of an evident consciousness of identity of this same A, or in the impossibility of an ego's experiencing at the same time what it imagines. The new type of countersense is a modal or attitudinal one: an incompatibility between modes of consciousness of the same object, experience and fiction. It completes the distinction of Ideen I §48 between logical possibility and "material countersense." A world in itself beyond experience, conceived as pure possibility, would suffer from this attitudinal countersense if it were said to be the actual world.

For a pure phantasy object, a "pure" possibility of being, experiencing and experienceability are nothing but phantasy experiencing and experienceability, and every determining and determined is phantasy determining and determined (185.5–15). Every "valid truth" for it is quasi-truth, quasi-theory; it holds, one might say, "under the presupposition" that the imagined truly is. But "presupposition" can only mean here that we hold fast the quasi-experienced in phantasy, bind ourselves by quasi-experience, and do not pass inconsistently and at will from something imagined into its incompatible "other." A note names the theme: "pure" phantasizing and the "pure," "a priori," "fact-free" thinking performed on its ground, as against phantasizing, positing, and hypothetical thinking "bound" to facts. Consistency is the only binding force in pure phantasy. Pure possibilities have their truths, but only as consequences of a self-imposed rule. The note announces the contrast between eidetic thinking, which is bound only by consistency, and hypothetical thinking about the actual, which is bound by facts.

Take instead phantasy connected with experience and thought, the ordinary cases where we imagine something experienced and held fast in its experienced character as an A and suppose it is an A: then we have quite different suppositions, decided as to actual truth and falsity, and decided in themselves (185.16–186.10). A note: as to the imagined content, "consistency" is also needed. If I re-fiction a G given in experience as α, say red, into an incompatible green, the supposition is already decided as false, and I can at most consider what would follow if G were β instead of α. But if G is still imperfectly determined, later experience decides whether the G given as α is also β. I can think that there is a centaur in some unexplored continent; this thought, this supposition or question, is decided by further experience. What G is, and perhaps whether it is, is decided in itself for every G posited in actual doxic consciousness, and so is every assumption drawn "arbitrarily" from the fund of phantasy within the bounds of essential compatibility. "Within true actuality there are no open possibilities any more." Every possibility is either true and then actuality, or false. "Thinkable," "possible" means, relative to the actual as experienced or meant in some mode of givenness, what can be drawn in phantasy into the open horizon of this mode of givenness, compatibly in essence with what is determinately posited. The possible is what is still undecided for the knower in such motivation. The centaur in an unexplored continent is the bound counterpart of the free centaur: a hypothesis about the actual world, decided in itself and to be decided by experience. The sentence "within true actuality there are no open possibilities" is the epistemic definition of real possibility: possibility is undecidedness for the knower within the horizon of the actual, not an ontological indeterminacy in the world. This is the most exact form of Ideen I §47's "motivated possibility."

In pure phantasy, in the realm of pure possibility, we have pure arbitrariness, creating quasi-objects in quasi-experiences and quasi-meanings and completing them as consistent possibilities of objects only by positing the consistency of arbitrariness (186.11–21). Every object has countless incompatible, equally thinkable objects. Every truth is a truth of imagination and fixed only while the consistency of identically maintaining what is imagined is kept; otherwise it switches, like its "objects," into an incompatible counter-truth. Pure possibilities are thus internally lawful but externally unbound: nothing decides among them. This is why, as the variation arguments showed, pure possibility cannot single out an actual world.

Under the title purely possible objectivity we therefore have not from the outset a "world," a compatible totality of possible objects, a single possible "world," but "infinities of worlds, none compossible with another" (186.22–187.5). To our actual nature correspond in the realm of freely imaginable possibilities not only the one exactly corresponding possible nature but infinitely many incompatible possible natures, every one we obtain from factual nature by varying fiction. Each has its phantasy space, phantasy time, phantasy things with phantasy causalities; each has its phantasy natural science, which, once we have bound our imagining arbitrariness so as to maintain a nature-system consistently, is no longer arbitrary but, though only phantasy-valid, has "true" validity in this "presupposed" nature projected as ideal possibility. To this possibility belongs only this one possible science (a note: phantasy world with phantasy natural science = ideally possible world, ideally possible natural science), and to it belong possible ego-subjects as subjects of possible knowledge of this nature, of possible experience and theory, the knowledge with the theoretical content of just this science. The notion of "possible worlds" receives its phenomenological definition: a possible world is a consistent system of phantasy correlated with a phantasy science and a phantasy subject. Leibniz's term "incompossible" fixes the relation among them. Ideen I §47's "possible worlds and non-worlds" are given exact sense: each possible world is a variant of the actual world, with its own space, time, causality and science.

Taking another possibility, every subject of possible knowledge that it requires as correlative possibility is necessarily another (a note: the bond in the correlation of possible subject and possible nature, possible totality of factual existence) (187.6–188.3). If a totality of being, a world, a totality of possibly existing objects, is posited, an ego, not further determined, is co-thought in this positing thought, though in "coincidence" with us who imagine this totality, as its co-possible knower; and for it is prescribed what it can and cannot experience and see as truth of being. It is no longer thinkable as any subject whatever, but only as one that can have experience of this totality of being and of nothing conflicting with it. An ego-subject as determinate has its determinate universe of possible experience; it cannot have possible experience of everything possible. As determinate it has its determinate stream, its determinate universe of immanently experienceable givens, and whether it experiences transcendent objects in it and which is fixed, as is what possibilities of experience it can reach by free activity. Every transcendently constituted object prescribes to the experiencer its system of order of sensations, manifolds of appearance, and so a determinate system of possible harmonious experience; what conflicts with it cannot in principle be harmoniously experienced by this ego. We can also say: if we think a universe of possible objects forming with any being thought in pure possibility a compossible systematic totality, a totality of possible existence, then every subject of possible knowledge for all these objects, for each one, belongs to it. The decisive formula is that the knowers belong to the compossible totality, not outside it. A possible world includes its possible knowers as members; a knower is individuated by the world it can experience. This is the eidetic version of Text No. 7's worldly thesis that knowers belong to the world they know, and of Text No. 9's argument that compossible egos share one nature.

Every object incompatible with any of these objects determines another totality and correspondingly other knowers (188.4–18). "Two such 'possible worlds' cannot contain common subjects," though perhaps other possible things. A note: given the importance of the matter, we must not go so fast but must think through our finding with the utmost care. The next question will be what demands bind the object of such a possible world with the required possible knower. Is it said that the possibility of the subject is an open possibility, or that the possibility of such an object, its possible being, co-demands being in the unity of a possibility, so that we must assert that the being of G demands the being of S? We had established that every object G thought in pure possibility, as possibly existing, must necessarily be knowable, that this possible being demands a possible knower. The exclusion of shared subjects between incompatible worlds is the eidetic core of transcendental idealism: subjects are not neutral observers who could look into any world, but are bound to one. The remaining question, whether the being of G requires the being of S, is the special thesis in its final eidetic formulation.

What does the established possible coexistence of knowing subject and object G mean (188.19–29)? G and an Sg that can become its knower (whether several is open) stand in a functional connection. If G exists, a rule of consciousness regarding possible knowledge is fixed for every subject to be assumed as coexisting with G: it cannot perform knowledge conflicting with knowledge of G. Conversely, if a subject of this rule exists, an Sg (thought as possibly existing), G must exist. But if G is, must an Sg coexist with it? The two directions already established, G constrains coexisting subjects, and a subject with Sg's rule implies G, recapitulate the variation argument of Text No. 3. The open question is the third: whether G's existence requires an Sg's existence. The realist accepts the first two and denies the third.

One might think the same can be said when an ideal object such as the number series "exists" (188.30–189.8). Every subject is of course under a rule: the possible experiences originally giving the ideal object give the rule that no subject can have experiences conflicting with them. Conversely, if a subject and every coexisting plurality of subjects stand under this rule, the ideal object "exists." But is the situation not essentially different for ideal and for contingent, factual objects? Every possible subject in general is under the rule of idealities. Every one in pure possibility, even for contingent idealities, can have only eidetic insights corresponding to the existing eide, before any actuality of such subjects. The ideal case is the test. The functional connection holds there too, but the rule binds every possible subject, so it requires none in particular. The final phrase, "before any actuality of such subjects," states why ideal objects do not require actual subjects.

As for contingent objects, their contingency, their mere "factuality," means that they could also be thought "otherwise" (189.9–31). By re-fiction we can form infinitely many "other" objects from every factual one. But if in free possibility we posit one, positing this possibility excludes all others equally justified in themselves; they are equally justified since each is a possibility, a possible being; but if one is posited, all others are excluded as coexisting with it. In the sense of our findings this means that every Sg functioning as possible knower for the posited G has a distinguished rule of intellective consciousness prescribed, excluding that this same Sg could be knower for any of the G′, G″ incompossible with G. The rule for Sg excludes the knowledge the rule for Sg′ includes, and conversely. As the G, G′ are incompossible, so are the Sg, Sg′. So we come indeed to what was stated: to every compossible totality G, G1, G2 … Gn belongs, and belongs within this totality, an Sg, Sg1 …; another totality has another S. Two such totalities have nothing in common; they are incompatible in existence as totalities, and this concerns the S they contain. Contingency is the root of the difference: because factual objects have incompossible rivals, their knowers have incompossible rules, and so each knower belongs to one world. The argument shows that a world's knowers are inside it; it still does not show, with full rigour, that a world requires an actual knower, which is why the next unit admits a gap.

But are further explanations not missing (189.32–35)? What about two compossible ideally possible facts? They can be together, one will say, and are then simultaneous (a note: facts are temporal objects). But they need not be together. The admission opens a new line: compossibility does not yet give coexistence. Two possible facts may be compatible without forming one world.

One must distinguish possibilities that are not incompatible from possibilities that, thought as coexisting together as a plurality, are to make up a unity of coexisting possibilities (190.1–12). If I imagine an A and a B wholly unconnectedly, there is no question of incompatibility, but none of compatibility either: a mere privation of incompatibility. Incompatibility is a synthetic unity of what conflicts: the temporal objects are posited in a unity but in conflict, one or both members being phantasy members. Compatibility I have when I posit both A and B, then necessarily in one time, in the unity of a harmony without conflict. The distinction between non-incompatibility (mere lack of relation) and compatibility (positive unity in one time) is a fine logical point. Unrelated phantasies have no modal relation at all; compatibility and incompatibility both require synthesis in one time. This explains why "many worlds" cannot be merely compatible alternatives: without synthesis they are neither compatible nor incompatible, simply unrelated, and so not candidates for coexistence.

Whatever I posit as fact, as temporal object or as temporally together, it remains open what else may be co-posited without conflict; and though it remains open, one can ask what must necessarily belong to the temporal field of what is already posited (190.13–20). Are there a priori rules here? Must one not say: if something already is, what is not can only be legitimable in conflict with what is? A temporal field cannot be undetermined; non-being expresses a conflict with being. The principle that non-being is legitimable only as conflict with being, and that a temporal field cannot be undetermined, is the ground of the claim in Text No. 10 that every cancellation preserves the unity of the world. It also bears on the annihilation hypothesis of Ideen I §49: a total non-being of the world would have no being to conflict with, which is the difficulty Appendix VI raised.

To posit an A (holding it in the intention of harmony) and together with it a B likewise does not mean positing each, but positing a unity of several facts, a plurality (190.21–32). A plurality as fact is a unity of coexistence or succession. All facts to be posited unitarily lie in one time as the necessary form of factual plurality. This holds for compatibility and incompatibility: compatibility of facts means compatibility in a plurality, in a temporal order, and likewise incompatibility. With every fact an open infinity of compossible facts spans a priori: an open temporal sphere of possible co-existence, of possible coexistence in the wide sense, simultaneity or succession. The text ends with the temporal form as the principle of unity of facts. Every fact opens a horizon of compossible facts in one time, the horizon of a world. Facts are therefore never isolated: to posit one is to posit an open world in one time, which is the eidetic basis of Appendix VI's "a thing exists means an open universe exists." Whether this open world requires an actual knower is left where Text No. 11 found it, as the question that Text No. 12 approaches through the concept of the in-itself.

## No. 12. On the Concept of the In-Itself of Real Objects

The last text, probably from the summer semester of 1921, opens with a diagnosis (191.4–7): objections to transcendental idealism rest on a lack of clarity in the concept of the in-itself of real objects and the real world, and the following sheets attempt to begin this analysis. The volume ends where the realist objection began. Text No. 1 started from the claim that things are "in themselves"; Text No. 2 redefined the in-itself as independence from particular acts; Appendix VI made it an idea of approximation. Text No. 12 undertakes the analysis these redefinitions require, on the view that a clarified in-itself is compatible with, and indeed explained by, the idealist thesis.

Describing the phenomenon of the external world, we find as belonging to the essence of external realities, such as natural things exemplify, being-in-itself over against the subject who experiences and on that basis knows nature (191.8–31). Things are transcendent to the experiencer and his acts, "external," which of course does not mean spatially external, and "in themselves" in that being experienced is inessential to them, that they are or at least can be even if an experiencer does not experience them, even if no one does. This cup depends on me insofar as I can break it; as a human in nature I can intervene in my natural surroundings and produce changes, but it is not essential to things to be changed by me, to the cup to be knocked, moved, broken by me. If I were not there, and it is thinkable that I were not in these surroundings, it would be for itself, have its real properties, run along the thread of its own causal chains. I need not be there, nor anyone else. How do I see this? It does not lie in the experienced thing as an experienced or experienceable property, like an experienceable back or physical determinations. And yet something related to unexperienced but experienceable determinations can be drawn on. The in-itself is described fairly, in the realist's own terms, and two senses of dependence are separated: causal dependence on me as a body in nature (I can break the cup), and the question whether the thing's being depends on being experienced. The first is a contingent worldly relation; the second is the question of transcendental idealism. The in-itself is not itself an experienced property, yet it is akin to the unexperienced but experienceable determinations of the horizon. This is the clue the text follows.

In the natural attitude I experience the thing (191.32–192.5). According to the sense of its apperception, of which I know nothing here, it has its constitutive features and stands in the connection of its experienced surroundings with their features. This material content alone is experienced, partly intuited determinately, partly indeterminate and determinable horizon. The parenthesis "of which I know nothing here" marks the anonymity of constitution in the natural attitude, the point of Text No. 6 (§2) and Ideen I §§27–28. The in-itself will be found in the structure of this anonymously functioning apperception.

What is actually experienced has its "inner horizon" (192.6–17): the co-apprehended inner determinations, indeterminate beside what is determinately apprehended, with their open horizon. The actually experienced features also have their open horizon of closer or other determinability, and over all this the open possibility of cancellability of everything. Perhaps it is better to separate as first experienced the properly intuited and the co-apprehended, then the open horizons on both sides, and finally the same for the surroundings, with the open horizon of unknown mediate objectivities of the surroundings running to infinity. The distinction of inner and outer horizon, which Experience and Judgment (§8) makes canonical, is introduced here. The layering, properly intuited, co-apprehended, open horizons, surroundings, infinite outer horizon, refines §4b of Text No. 6, and the inclusion of "cancellability of everything" keeps the presumptive status of the whole.

I change my orientations, go here and there, experience more and new of the thing and of its thingly surroundings, whether bodily given or not (192.18–193.9). As empirical subject I am myself nature, but "I" give "myself" in "freedom" changing bodily-spatial-thingly positions and so experience more of things and of myself, the human being. I am directed at nature and its determinations. These contain, regarding the thing itself, nothing of me, and regarding the thing's relations, only relations to me as human, which I can recognize as contingent, as I can recognize the contingency of the physical surroundings, of the existence of the things around the thing in view. How so? The thing I experience is such, according to its apperception and the sense or proposition carried out in it, that I can keep the thing's identity, though not its material properties, under free re-fiction of the surrounding things. I can think them otherwise; the causalities would then run otherwise, my thing would have to change, but it would be the same thing, if I hold fast its sense and continue to think it posited as actual. I can hold the other things fast as actualities but think them re-fictioned: if I suppose they were otherwise, my thing too would be otherwise. Here I cannot re-fiction freely; I am bound, and vice versa. Instead of treating the surroundings so, I can also imagine things snowing in from the open horizon of the surroundings for which nothing in my experience so far speaks, or things already there, without anything really speaking for them, making off; then too determinate or open changes result, not prescribed by my empirical knowledge. Here is the clue to the in-itself. Re-fiction shows that the thing's identity survives variation of its surroundings, including variation of my presence, while its properties change in lawful dependence. My relations to the thing, as a body in its surroundings, are contingent in exactly the way other things' relations to it are: I am one surrounding thing among others. So the in-itself of the thing, its independence from me as human, is a structure of its sense revealed by bound variation, not a hidden property. The constraint "here I cannot re-fiction freely; I am bound" is the causal holism of Appendix VI applied as a rule of variation.

But such is the eidos of a thing that it is a thing of a nature, and such the eidos "nature" that it is a connection of things determinable in open infinity but firmly closed in itself (193.10–18): one space, one natural time, an absolutely closed unity of substantial-causal community. A note: what "absolutely closed" means as a demand is still a problem; could nature not "change"? Compare later sheets (which the editor could not identify). If the thing is, a nature is, which leaves open in itself nothing, but leaves open only for me, the experiencer, what and where and when thingly being is, including what is beside, before and after my given thing. This is the concept of nature as a closed, determinate whole that Text No. 11 (186) stated epistemically: "within true actuality there are no open possibilities." The in-itself is the closedness of nature, its determinacy independent of my state of knowledge; openness belongs only to the horizon of the knower. The note's question, whether nature could "change," that is, whether its laws could alter, keeps this closedness a problem rather than a dogma.

How this necessity is grounded, and what levels of necessities and thing-senses exist here, is a question (193.19–33). Is the thing of common experience, by its own sense, already to be thought necessarily as a thing of such a nature, or is it only the thing of empirical science, the higher apperception created by scientific method, that makes this demand? An ontology of nature can begin as ontology of the nature of common experience, as ontology of phantoms, as ontology of things as substantial-causal unities; the type of material identity may still differ according to the kind of governing experience and empirical knowledge, here the "common." One can ask which eide of nature or real things are necessary and how far divergent and open possibilities exist here. The question whether the closedness of nature belongs to the lifeworldly thing or only to the physical thing is the question the Crisis (§§9, 36) answers by distinguishing the lifeworld's typical style from the idealized, mathematically determined nature of physics. The levels named, phantom, substantial-causal thing, scientific thing, are the levels of Ideen II §§15–18 and of Text No. 4 (62–63).

Accordingly the sense of the thing's "being-in-itself" may have to be determined differently (194.1–14). In what sense can a phantom be in itself? Or does it "exist" and is it "independent" of the experiencer? Can a phantom be intersubjectively "in itself"? Could a subject, if there were only phantoms, have other subjects over against it? And what can an empirical, "psychophysical" subjectivity be if instead of material things there were only spatial phantoms? But if we presuppose an apperception whose sense prescribes an "infinite" world, endlessly open in space, time, substantiality-causality, where openness does not mean actual infinity but only that an open horizon extends endlessly in which natural realities could be found, the question is, for this world and its eidetic type, its form, what "in-itself" its sense prescribes. The volume ends on a question, and the question is precise. The in-itself is not one thing: it differs with the level of constitution. A phantom, a merely spatial-sensory shape without causal properties, has at most a solipsistic in-itself; intersubjectivity and psychophysical subjectivity require material, causal things, since others are given through bodies that act and are acted on. The full in-itself of the world is the in-itself that the apperception of an open, infinite, causal world prescribes. This is the conclusion toward which the whole volume has worked. The realist's independent world is not denied but analysed: its independence is the sense prescribed by the apperception of an open causal nature shared by embodied subjects, and to clarify this sense is to show that it requires, rather than excludes, the constituting consciousness that Ideen I's fundamental consideration called absolute. The phenomenological idealism of Ideen I appears, at the end, as the first and formal stage of an analysis whose material completion, the in-itself as intersubjective, embodied, and causally structured, these texts have opened without closing.
